**Divine zeal beside knowledge** is an affection born of the love of propagating the truth, and of indignation against those whom it supposeth to be enemies, or at the least depravers of the true doctrine,
<!-- p.146 -->
but falsely. For this zeal hath for its antecedent cause ignorance, or unskilfulness of the truth, and yet an opinion of true understanding. And **Paul** himself also laboured under such zeal, **Acts 8 and 9**. And Joshua against Eldad and Medad, **Num. 11**. And at this day many, even good men, are moved by zeal of this kind, so that they condemn others who have deserved well of the Church, and who teach rightly, whom they themselves hold suspected of errors of doctrine. And this zeal, when it conceiveth flames and, by overmuch indulging itself, looseth the reins, degenerateth into blind violence and fury, envy and hatred, pride and cruelty, as examples everywhere do testify at this day.

**Divine zeal according to knowledge** is an affection sprung from true knowledge and the true fear of God, and inflamed with the love of propagating the divine word, and with indignation or hatred of those things which hinder the course of the word, or which delay, corrupt and deform the true doctrine, whether by idolatry, or by labyrinths of disputations, or by tyranny, or by inconstancy, or by perverseness of life and evil examples. Now this zeal is either seasonable or unseasonable.

**Seasonable divine zeal** is that which burneth at a fit time, and hath sufficient causes of indignation, and overpasseth not the bounds of vocation and of charity, and seeketh nothing else but the glory of God, and directeth all things to edification and to the conversion of many unto God, and to the destruction of ungodliness. Examples in the sacred Scriptures are many. Moses casteth down and breaketh the Tables, and burneth the calf, **Exod. 32**. And against Korah, Dathan and Abiram he ordereth his prayers, in **Numbers, chap. 16**. Phinehas, the grandson of Aaron, burning with zeal for chastity and for propagating the glory of God, slayeth the Israelite who was committing whoredom with the Midianitish woman, **Numer. 25**. Christ overturneth the tables and seats of the merchants in the Temple, and himself saith, **Psalm 69**: *"The zeal of thine house hath consumed me, because
<!-- p.147 -->
mine enemies have forgotten thy words,"*[^C5-13] that is, I could not refrain myself from punishing those impure profaners, neither felt I any rest, unless thy Church were reformed, which I perceived to be miserably depraved. Elias, moved by such zeal, slayeth the priests of Baal. **Luther** in like manner, kindled with divine zeal joined with knowledge, seasonably, and upon a most fit occasion, reproved the Papistical errors, and showed forth the true doctrine anew.

**Unseasonable zeal** is that which observeth no fitness of time, or of other necessary circumstances, but by a certain unwary affection of the mind stirreth certain things which might with greater profit to the Church have been deferred, and reserved unto occasions more convenient, and more useful and fruitful both to those very persons against whom the indignation goeth forth, and to others also. I will add one example.

**Theodoret, *Ecclesiastical History*, Book 5, chap. 39.**

Isdigerdes, King of the Persians, moved war against the Church upon this occasion. Audas[^C5-14] was a bishop endued with manifold virtues. He, stirred up by an unseasonable ardour, overthrew a ***πυρεῖον***, a temple of flame or of fire (for they think fire to be a deity). The King, being informed of this matter by the Magi, sendeth for Audas, and at the first, having gently accused him, commandeth him to restore the Pyreum; which when Audas said that he would not do, the King threateneth that he will overthrow all the churches, and as he had threatened, so he did. And when he had commanded that holy man to be slain, then thereafter he commandeth all the churches to be pulled down. And I indeed judge that the overthrow of the Pyreum was not seasonable. For neither did the holy Apostle, when he was come to Athens, and had seen the city to be given over to the worship of images, overthrow any altar which they venerated, but by speech
<!-- p.148 -->
he reproved their ignorance, and taught the truth, etc.

Such was the zeal of the baker who, nigh twenty years ago, at Vienna rushed into a solemn procession, in which the consecrated bread was being carried about, King Ferdinand being present, and snatched from the sacrificing priest the *Monstrance*, as they call it, and trod it to pieces under his feet.

To this unseasonable zeal is referred also overmuch indignation against the felicity and prosperity of the ungodly in this life, which indignation is forbidden as pernicious.

Now for seasonable zeal very many things are required, of which these are the chief. 1. True knowledge of the will of God, and of the true doctrine. 2. True invocation of God, and godliness of life. 3. A certain acknowledgment of the errors which conflict with the true doctrine, or of the crimes which are contrary to godliness. 4. Moderation of private affections, of anger, hatred, envy, revenge. 5. A care to perform the duties of one's vocation, and to stir nothing by any private counsel and will. 6. Consideration of opportunity, lest a spark, being stirred, bring forth a fire which cannot easily be quenched.[^C5-15] 7. An heroical and divine spirit, burning with the love of the glory of God, and yielding to no evils, but rather suffering death and any torments whatsoever, than permitting itself to be plucked away from God and his truth. 8. Modesty joined with gravity, undertaking nothing rashly, headlong and unadvisedly, but going forward step by step to show the truth and to confute falsehood, and always having regard to the weaker, lest they be offended or provoked by overmuch violence.

For the rest, **human zeal of duty** is industry and alacrity in vocation; as the preacher in the church ought to be diligent in the duties of his vocation, the prince in the kingdom, the parent in the household, the preceptor in the school, the scholar in learning useful and necessary precepts. And hither belongeth the exposition of the whole Decalogue,
<!-- p.149 -->
and the table containing the rules of our whole life, political, ecclesiastical and domestic. **Human zeal contrary to duty** is either that of the unchaste and wanton, or is that of men plainly raging, as Medea rageth, tearing her child in pieces, and Nero rageth, crying out: *"When I am dead, let the earth be mingled with fire."*[^C5-16]

**Diabolical zeal** is either a burning hatred of the true worship of God, as Cain, the Scribes and the Pharisees pursued the true worship of God with extreme hatred; or it is persecution itself, or the study and endeavour of oppressing the truth, as the Turks, the Papists, the Jews, and other tyrants and heretics are stirred up by the spirit of Satan to oppress the truth. Let it suffice to have gathered these things concerning zeal, from which it is manifest that vicious zeal is to be discerned from that which is right and laudable, and that in the Psalms and elsewhere private and vicious indignation and impatience is forbidden, but that just zeal is not forbidden, joined with silence and hope, and, when need is, with courage and liberty of the Spirit; concerning which nevertheless certain precepts cannot be delivered, seeing this whole matter belongeth to the peculiar efficacy of the Holy Spirit, and to the examination and proving of every man's conscience.

### *De promissionibus et comminationibus quinti Praecepti* · On the Promises and Threatenings of the Fifth Commandment

In **Luke 6** it is written: *"Forgive, and ye shall be forgiven."* For meekness, gentleness and placableness have always great rewards, as the Son of God saith: *"Blessed are the meek, for they shall inherit the earth. Blessed are the merciful, for they shall obtain mercy."* And examples do testify that the labours, the patience and the meekness of the godly are blessed in many ways. And the threatenings also are known: *"He that taketh the sword shall perish with the sword." "Whoso sheddeth man's blood, by man shall his blood be shed." "With the same measure that ye mete withal it shall be measured
<!-- p.150 -->
to you again." "He shall have judgment without mercy, that hath shewed no mercy."* Examples in Cain, Joab, and in the seditious and the wrathful, are before our eyes in all times. God grant that we may consider the saying of Christ: *"Learn of me, for I am meek and lowly in heart."*

## *De VI. Praecepto* · On the Sixth Commandment

This commandment fenceth the covenants of marriage, and forbiddeth all minglings and defilements outside this order instituted by God. The virtues of this commandment are:

- Chastity.
- Purity of the single life.
- Conjugal chastity.
- Modesty or shamefastness.
- Temperance.

**Chastity** or pudicity (*castitas sive pudicitia*) in general is the virtue which keepeth that cleanness of mind and of body which agreeth with the will of God, and which shunneth all lusts forbidden by God.

**Purity of the single life** (*puritas vitae caelibis*) is the virtue which keepeth cleanness of mind and of body without any abuse of seed, without mingling and defilement of bodies, and without unchaste thoughts, and without any burnings of lusts.

**Marriage** is the lawful and indissoluble conjunction of one male and one female, ordained, established and approved by God, for procreation, and for the avoiding of the confusions of lusts, that we may acknowledge God to be a chaste mind, and may serve him in chastity, and that in this manner the human race may be preserved and propagated, that out of it an eternal Church may be gathered unto God. And **conjugal chastity** (*castitas coniugalis*) is, in marriage, to keep the order instituted by the wonderful counsel of God, without wandering lusts, unto lawful procreation, with mutual love, with sincere faith, with a common fellowship of goods and of hardships, and with defence and ***ἐπιεικείᾳ*** (forbearance) used.

<!-- p.151 -->

**Modesty** or shamefastness (*verecundia seu pudor*) is the virtue which standeth in awe of true infamy, which always accompanieth offences, and which, through fear of the divine judgment, and of its own conscience, and of others who judge rightly, restraineth all desires, and shunneth offences, and so ruleth speech, gestures and the habit of the body, that it may agree with the order of nature, of persons and of places.

**Temperance** or sobriety (*temperantia seu sobrietas*) is the virtue which keepeth a mean in meat, drink and other things, agreeable to nature, and which hindereth not by gluttony prayer, meditation, the labours of vocation, and the exercises of the other virtues, nor hurteth sleep and health by overmuch fasting.

### *Antithesis* · The Antithesis

With chastity conflicteth all uncleanness, and incontinence, impurity, and wandering lusts, and whoredoms outside marriage.

With purity of the single life conflict in like manner the same sins, ravishings, incest, and the like turpitudes.

With conjugal chastity conflict all adulteries, and common and light divorces, perfidy, desertion, rancour, tyranny, frowardness, disdain, suspicion, dissembling.

With modesty conflict impudence, ***δυσωπία*** or stupor, obscenity in speech and gesture, ***εὐτραπελία***, scurrility and filthiness.

With temperance conflicteth drunkenness, voracity, surfeiting, gluttony, riot, profusion, superfluity in meats, apparel, household stuff and other things, superstitious and immoderate fasts, superstitious ***ἰχθυοφαγία*** (fish-eating), overmuch abstinence that hurteth the health.

### *Promissiones et comminationes sexti praecepti notae sunt* · The Promises and Threatenings of the Sixth Commandment Are Known

<!-- p.152 -->

**Matth. 5.** *"Blessed are the pure in heart, for they shall see God."* **1 Cor. 5.**[^C5-17] *"Fornicators and adulterers shall not inherit the kingdom of God."* **Hebr. 13.** *"Whoremongers and adulterers God will punish, or judge."* **Heb. 12.** *"Follow after sanctification, without which no man shall see God."* And examples of horrible punishments do show the wrath of God against lusts, as the burning of Sodom, the destruction of the tribe of Benjamin, the exile of David, the slaughter of the Lacedaemonians in the battle of Leuctra, because of the unpunished ravishers of the daughters of Scedasus, and daily other examples, public and private. Let us therefore be studious and lovers of chastity, and let us use seed reverently, and let us resist the depraved and wandering flames of lusts by prayer, by reading, by the study of temperance, and by the removing of the occasions and enticements whereby the foul burnings of minds are kindled or cherished; such as are wine, wherein is ***ἀσωτία*** (riotousness), immoderately taken, evil companies, ***γυναικὸς ὄμμα τοῖς ἀκμάζουσι βέλος*** (a woman's eye is a dart unto them that are in their prime), and the like enticements.

## *De VII. Praecepto* · On the Seventh Commandment

This commandment is the hedge and the wall wherewith God fenceth our little cottages and little nests, and the things necessary for sustenance, against thieves and robbers, and he approveth the distinction of ownerships both by his commandment and by the fear of punishments. For it is certain that every man's own house is divinely fenced by the Law of God and by the fear of punishments much more than by any walls, ditches, mounds, ramparts and towers, of all which it is rightly said: *"Help us, we perish."*

Now let the order of the commandments be considered: the Fifth Commandment guardeth the life and the bodies of each; the Sixth, marriages; the Seventh, the houses and nests of each, and the things necessary. And the virtues of this commandment are:

- Just possession and the good use of one's own things.
- <!-- p.153 --> Commutative justice.
- Beneficence or liberality.
- Hospitality.
- Parsimony or frugality.
- ***αὐτάρκεια*** (contentment).
- Gratitude.
- Sedulity.

**Just possession** (*iusta possessio*) is the virtue which, in the fear of God, and in faith and a good conscience, holdeth its own goods, and which, acknowledging that they are given and kept by God, diligently studieth to preserve them, and to increase them by honest means, and to bestow them upon the uses ordained by God. Hither belongeth also the definition of ownership (*dominium*). Ownership is a right or faculty ordained by law, whereby the owner, and no other, holdeth a thing by a just title, and disposeth of that thing at his own will, save so far as he is forbidden by law and by covenant. And this is to be held against the Anabaptists and the like. For it is lawful for Christians to have property, riches and goods, so that pride, avarice, security, prodigality and abuse be taken away. And it is written: *"The blessing of the Lord, it maketh rich."* And the examples of Abraham, Isaac, Jacob, Joseph and others are known. And in general the rule is to be held, that the Gospel doth not abolish political ordinances.

**Commutative justice** (*iustitia commutativa*) is the virtue which in contracts, or in the exchange of things, keepeth an arithmetical equality, lest the one party be drained dry. Hither belongeth the whole doctrine of contracts, ***ἢ συναλλάγμασιν*** (or dealings), and of obligations.

**Beneficence** (*beneficentia*) is the virtue which imparteth goods and services not owed by a civil obligation, but by a natural and divine one, where and when it is seemly, and which maketh a mean between profusions and niggardliness. Hither belong alms, of which three kinds are necessary. The first, to contribute something to the preservation of the ecclesiastical ministry, according to that saying: *"He that shall give unto the least
<!-- p.154 -->
in the name of a disciple, shall in no wise lose his reward."* The second kind is to bestow upon needy kinsfolk. *"For if any provide not for his own, and specially for those of his own house, he is worse than an infidel."* The third kind is to help strangers, whom the present occasion commendeth unto us, as it is written: *"Whoso hath goods, and seeth his brother have need, and shutteth up his bowels from him, how dwelleth the love of God in him?"* **1 John 3**. And **Deut. 15**: *"If one of thy brethren, who dwell within the gates of thy city, in the land which the Lord thy God is about to give thee, shall be brought to poverty, thou shalt not stop up thine heart."* And: *"The poor shall not be wanting in the land of thy habitation: therefore I command thee, that thou open thine hand unto thy needy and poor brother, who dwelleth with thee in the land."* And **Paul** appointeth a measure for alms, **2 Cor. 8**, saying: *"Not that other men be eased, and ye burdened: but that by an equality, at this present time, out of that which remaineth over unto us, their want may be supplied."* Now the equality is understood not as arithmetical, but as geometrical, in respect both of the giver and of the receiver. For from greater numbers more units can be taken away, from lesser fewer. And the needy ought not to abuse another's liberality, nor to use that which is another's save in necessity. And beggary which hath not a reasonable cause is a sin, and a kind of theft. And there is no need to bestow upon slothful beggars. It is also to be looked to that alms be not profusions got up for ostentation, but that they provide for the necessity of others, because of the commandment of God. And let these sayings be known: *"Deal thy bread to the hungry; then shalt thou call, and the Lord shall answer thee."* **Isa. 58**.

**Proverb. 21.** *"Whoso stoppeth his ears at the cry of the poor, he also shall cry himself in his turn, but shall not be heard."* **Proverb. 5.** *"Drink of thine own fountains, and let thy rivulets be led abroad, and be thou lord of them. Blessed shall thy vein be."*

**Matth. 25.** *"I was an hungred and thirsty, and ye gave me meat and
<!-- p.155 -->
drink."* And **Matth. 10.** *"He that shall give a drink of water for the doctrine's sake, shall not lack his reward."*

**Hospitality** (*hospitalitas*) is a kind of beneficence, whereby we embrace strangers and guests, and chiefly those who are in exile for the profession of the true doctrine, with true goodwill and with all the offices of hospitality.

**Parsimony** or frugality (*parsimonia seu frugalitas*) is the virtue which keepeth its own things, and layeth them out profitably, shunneth unnecessary charges, the itch of buying, riot, borrowings, buildings, and yet, where reason biddeth, spendeth as much as is meet.

***αὐταρκεία*** (contentment) is the virtue which is content with the present blessing of God, or with present goods, and beareth it calmly if aught be lacking, neither coveteth the goods of others, nor is broken by want, nor doeth anything against the godliness owed unto God, or against the other virtues.

**Gratitude** (*gratitudo*) is a virtue mixed of truth and of justice, that is, it professeth what and how great benefits it hath received, and studieth to render an equal return, according to its ability, unto its benefactor.

**Sedulity** (*sedulitas*) is the virtue which constantly and firmly, for God's sake and for the common welfare, doeth the labours agreeable to its vocation, God helping, who hath promised help unto them that ask.

### *Antithesis* · The Antithesis

With just possession conflict all thefts, whereby the thing of another is handled, seized and held against the owner's will, without recompense; ***πλεονεξίαι*** (covetous overreachings), frauds and impostures, usuries, robberies of what sort soever, and chiefly of ecclesiastical goods and revenues, of bishoprics and priestly livings, the ministry being neglected, and studies being neglected. It is rightly said that to steal is the same as in any manner whatsoever to lie in wait for the goods of others, and to usurp them unto oneself. This God severely forbiddeth, because, seeing he is most just, he willeth that after the rule of his own justice we should render unto every man his own. He forbiddeth therefore the desire of having, and the effect
<!-- p.156 -->
of the same, that is, theft, which is twofold, human and diabolical. For so we speak for the sake of teaching. The former is committed either in things, or in right. In things, as are:

Privy purloining of another's thing.

Violence, unjust booty, robbery.

Imposture in weight, measure, counting, contract, the exchange of things, counterfeit wares, and the feigned and false commendation of things, of which it is said: *"He praiseth his wares, who would thrust them off for sale."*[^C5-18]

Pretext of right, when we transfer unto ourselves the things of others under some show of right.

Pretext of gift, when superiors ask something of their inferiors, which these dare not deny.

Pretext of prescription, as in ecclesiastical goods. And the interruption of a possession, or quasi-possession, grounded upon lapse of time is brought in by gainsaying, and by proof of possession in bad faith, as the wiser jurists judge and speak. And since the possessor of ecclesiastical goods, the ministry being neglected, and studies being neglected, is a possessor in bad faith, a robber and a spoiler, law and equity pronounce that he is bound, without distinction, to restore all the revenues, whether they be yet extant or be consumed. And altogether the rule hath place: *The sin is not remitted, unless that which was taken away be restored.*[^C5-19] And: *"Let him that stole steal no more,"* that is, let him render that which is another's, and cease to be a thief. Yet we do not shut out ***ἐπιείκειαν*** (equity), that offences may be avoided, and infamy be guarded against.

In right theft is committed when we render not unto others those things which we owe them by covenant or by duty; as when we pay not his wage unto the labourer, or show not the offices of charity unto our neighbour. For by a natural obligation we are mutually bound one to another, and each is debtor to the other; by a civil obligation also we are bound to pay wages.

Diabolical theft is that which is committed after the imitation
<!-- p.157 -->
of the devil, who stole the hearts of our first parents, and called them away from God, that they might cleave unto himself. Such are all the seditious, the contumacious, the rebellious, the disobedient, who draw minds away from the obedience owed unto parents, preceptors, the magistrate, the ministers of the word, and masters.

With commutative justice conflict unjust contracts, or frauds in contracts, impostures in wares, usuries, or gains exacted above the principal on account of the service of lending, robberies, unjust pillagings public and private, privy thefts in courts and in public offices, the thieves whereof walk in gold and purple; likewise unjust wages and stipends, which are paid unto the slothful and the idle, beggary that is not necessary, and all things ill gotten.

But it is to be known that if any man have revenues bought upon some fruit-bearing estate, he is not an usurer, because it is a purchase, not a lending. And the substantials of a purchase concur: the ware, the price, and the consent. Likewise, if a man have not the right of demanding the money again, the judgment of the magistrate and of the laws holdeth, which distinguish between usuries and *that which is of interest* (*id quod interest*).

With beneficence and liberality conflict immoderate profusions, and unnecessary charges in banquets, buildings, garments; avarice that doeth good unto none, niggardliness, tenacity, unlawful and forbidden carefulness, and distrust, which is the fountain of thefts and of frauds, and bringeth forth ***πλεονεκτήματα*** (overreachings) of every kind; likewise evil deeds and injuries, whereby we hurt the minds, the bodies, the good name, or the goods of others, burdens overgreat, unnecessary exactions, and rapacity.

With hospitality conflict inhospitality, frowardness, tenacity, inhumanity, niggardliness, prodigality, the ingratitude of guests.

With parsimony: profusions, tenacity, negligence and sloth in guarding one's own things.

<!-- p.158 -->

With ***αὐταρκείᾳ***, of which **Paul** speaketh, **1 Tim. 6**, conflict the desire of money, insatiableness, the itch of buying, avarice, overmuch carefulness, prodigality, the abuse of one's own things.

With gratitude conflict ingratitude, lying, pride, injustice, the forgetting of benefits, and contempt of the benefactor.

With sedulity conflict sloth, which doeth not the proper labours of its vocation, and ***πολυπραγμοσύνη*** (meddlesomeness), which omitteth necessary works, and thrusteth itself into the vocation of another, stirring and troubling many things without commandment.

### *Promissiones et comminationes septimi praecepti* · The Promises and Threatenings of the Seventh Commandment

**Luke 6.** *"Give, and it shall be given unto you."* **Proverb. 19.** *"He that hath pity upon the poor lendeth unto the Lord."* **Isa. 33.** *"Woe unto thee that spoilest; shalt not thou thyself be spoiled in turn?"* **Habac. 2.** *"Because thou hast spoiled many nations, all they of the peoples shall spoil thee."* For a curse, an evil conscience, infamy, loss in goods, and other punishments, public and private, bodily and spiritual, temporal and eternal, are laid upon thieves and impostors; and *things ill gotten ill slip away.*[^C5-20]

## *De VIII. Praecepto* · On the Eighth Commandment

This commandment guardeth our good name, and requireth truth, because God is true. And the virtues of this commandment are:

- Truth.
- Simplicity.
- Candour.
- Constancy.
- Teachableness.
- <!-- p.159 --> Taciturnity.
- Eloquence.
- Courtesy or affability; and urbanity.
- Glory.
- Modesty.

**Truth** (*veritas*) is the virtue which inclineth the will, that we love true judgments, that is, such as agree with things as they are, and that we constantly speak, and do, and defend things true, right, plain and wholesome, and in our manners and in all our life flee insidious concealments and simulations.

**Simplicity** (*simplicitas*) is truth perspicuous and open, without wrappings and windings.

**Candour** (*candor*) is a virtue most near unto truth and simplicity, whereby namely we signify our own will truly and openly, and, so far as reason is probable, approve the wills of others, and bear no ill-will, and bend even certain things doubtfully said or done unto the better part, and hope well, neither conceive hatred toward others because of light suspicions.

**Constancy** (*constantia*) is the virtue which speaketh, doeth and defendeth things true, just and necessary constantly, that is, in the same manner and perpetually.

**Teachableness** (*docilitas*) is the virtue which bridleth minds, that they do not, led by arrogance or by self-love (*philautia*), stubbornly embrace false opinions, nor through admiration of their own wisdom despise the judgments of others, but that they hear others, learn, and, being convinced, yield and assent unto true judgments.

**Taciturnity** (*taciturnitas*) is the virtue which moderateth the use of the tongue in those things which it either doth not sufficiently know, or which are not necessary, or which hurt the neighbour and trouble quietness, so far as this may be done without violation of necessary truth.

**Eloquence** (*eloquentia*) is a peculiar gift, and a virtue, whereby the mind ruleth the will and the tongue, that it may speak things true, right, acceptable to God, and
<!-- p.160 -->
wholesome unto men, perspicuously, ornately, gravely and sweetly. **Courtesy** or affability (*comitas seu affabilitas*) is the virtue which inclineth us, that in speaking, in hearing, in answering, and in the performing of offices, in countenance and in gestures, we declare by a certain sweetness our goodwill toward others.

**Urbanity** (*urbanitas*) is the grace of truth, whereby, through things said merrily, or figuratively, or with salt, seasonably, truthfully and honestly, others are admonished, or are stirred up in due place, or are soothed.

**Glory** (*gloria*) is the inward approbation or testimony of a conscience that judgeth rightly, and of others that judge rightly concerning us, or concerning others.

**Modesty** (*modestia*) is the virtue which acknowledgeth its own infirmity, etc., as above in the Fourth Commandment.

### *Antithesis* · The Antithesis

With truth conflict all lies, that is, all opinions, speeches and gestures which agree not with the things themselves, and which hurt either the liar himself or others. But let us know that not every concealment is sin. For concealment is twofold, namely *insidious* and *officious*. The former ariseth from the desire of hurting, or from vanity, and is committed either in judgment, or out of judgment. In judgment, when either the plaintiff layeth a false accusation, or the defendant denieth those things which are truly objected, or the witness beareth testimony of things unknown or false, or the judge, corrupted by some affection, perverteth the true sentence: all which things, namely calumny or false accusation, lying defence, vain testimony, and the depraving of the law, conflict with truth, and are named insidious concealment. But out of judgment insidious concealment and false witness is committed in three manners, in mind, in tongue, and in deed: namely, when the mind cherisheth an evil suspicion of the neighbour, and doth not judge of him candidly, as was meet; and when the tongue teacheth perverse things, slandereth things rightly said and done, aggravateth the
<!-- p.161 -->
weakness of others, wresteth and interpreteth unto the worse part those things which are worthy of mitigation, taketh away honour from others, accuseth, and lieth in any manner whatsoever; likewise, when life, manners and pursuits bear hypocrisy before them, and lack open and natural candour. But officious concealment is a figure of speech, framed to cover certain things which it is not necessary should be laid open.

Antiquity hath thus distinguished: Of lies one is *pernicious*, another *jocose*, another *officious*. The **pernicious** lie is that whereby either the glory of God is obscured, or the neighbour is hurt, either in person, or in goods, or in good name. This lie is a mortal sin. The **jocose** lie is twofold. The one is born of vanity, boasting, flattery, wantonness, the lust of lying, and the desire of gain, without hurt of any man; as Thraso in **Terence** proclaimeth false things of himself out of mere vanity; the flatterers of Dionysius of Syracuse lick the spittle of the tyrant, and say that it is sweeter than honey; mountebanks and buffoons, ***καὶ εὐτράπελοι*** (and jesters), who study to stroke their hearers with vanities and levities. This lie neither becometh Christian godliness and gravity, nor guardeth a good conscience, nor hath the blessing of God, and it may also be a mortal sin. But the other is not against good manners, but for good manners, and serveth for the describing of things, as are dramas, and apologues, and the fables which, as it seemeth, we name *Aesopic* from Asaph. These, properly speaking, are not lies, but are images, figures and admonitions concerning good and true things, shown as it were in a picture, and they have a profitable end, and a moral ***ἐπιμύθιον*** (the lesson added to a fable), agreeing with the Decalogue, and not conflicting with charity. But the **officious** lie is that whereby those things are covered the confession whereof is not necessary, or which it is necessary to cover, lest we hurt others; as Rachel by craft hideth the idols; Michal feigneth that her husband is sick, and layeth an image in the bed; Rahab denieth that she hath Israelitish guests; **Athanasius** maketh the persecutors believe that he is not
<!-- p.162 -->
Athanasius. Hence is the rule: *He committeth no guile, who shutteth out fraud.* And, other things being equal, this also is true: *Guile or valour, who would ask in an enemy?*[^C5-21] And that saying of **Menander**: *It is better to choose a lie,* that is, a probable concealment, whereby namely, according to the commandment of God, a service is rendered unto the innocent, *than a pernicious truth.* For where a voluntary disclosing would be sin, there figures are allowed, or stratagems, or schemes of speech, which cover affairs. And those figures are not properly lies. For a lie properly is the depraving of that which ought to be said. And ironies, which have excuse in certain cases wherein we ought to profit the innocent, it is altogether lawful to use, yet so that an oath and the conscience be not violated. For the rest, unto pernicious lies, which conflict with truth, belong all false opinions, evil causes, evil and painted pretexts, and the whole of sophistry; likewise heresies, corruptions, unnecessary subtleties, calumnies, unjust sentences, and contumelious judgments, such as at this day also are those of the Theologians concerning one another; likewise perfidies, perjuries, frauds, impostures, ironies in the giving of counsels, backbiting, evil-speaking, vanity, boasting, courtly flattery, and hypocrisy of what sort soever; evasions in judgments, contracts, covenants, and in the whole life.

With simplicity conflict doubleness, ***διπλότης***, obscurity, perplexity, sophistry, ***πανουργία*** (craftiness), simulation, ***τὸ ἀκατάληπτον*** (that which cannot be laid hold of), courtly and political schemes lacking candour, and pernicious deceptions and persuasions.

With candour conflict calumnies, suspicions, distrust, and foolish credulity. With constancy conflict: inconstancy, uncertainty in doctrine, levity, stubbornness and perseverance in false opinions or actions.

With teachableness conflict stubbornness, dullness, security, negligence, and the Academic ***ἐποχή*** (suspension of judgment).

<!-- p.163 -->

With taciturnity conflict the dissembling of those things which ought, in the case of confession and in the defence of the truth, to be boldly spoken, or which were to be censured; likewise all the vices of an incontinent tongue, as garrulity, rashness, curiosity, evil-speaking, wantonness, vanity and boasting, vicious irony, ***δυσωπία*** and rusticity, or rustic shame, ***ἀκκισμός***, the feigning of shame and of humility, impudence, scurrility, obscenity, virulence, futility, insipidity, and flattery. With eloquence conflict unskilfulness, to which matter is wanting, words are not wanting; speechlessness (*infantia*), to which matter is at hand, words are wanting; garrulity, volubility, loquacity, plenty of words without good matter, or *loquentia*, as **Sallust** calleth it; impudence; speech obscure, monstrous, sordid and tedious; backbiting, ostentation, the abuse of the tongue.

With courtesy conflict levity, frowardness. With urbanity, scurrility, and rude and light ***εὐτραπελία*** (jesting); obscenity, contumely, a biting tongue, stolidity. With glory conflict levity and ambition. With modesty conflicteth impudence.

### *Promissiones et comminationes Octavi praecepti* · The Promises and Threatenings of the Eighth Commandment

**Psalm 15.** *"He that speaketh the truth in his heart, and backbiteth not his neighbour with his tongue, he shall abide in thy tabernacle, O Lord."* **Luke 6.** *"Judge not, and ye shall not be judged."* **Psal. 5.** *"Thou shalt destroy all them that speak leasing."* **Isa. 5.** *"Woe unto you that call evil good, and good evil, that put darkness for light, and light for darkness."* **Proverb. 12.** *"Lying lips are abomination to God."*

## *De IX. Praecepto* · On the Ninth Commandment

This commandment, as also the tenth, is the interpretation of those that go before, and testifieth that in the Decalogue not only outward offences
<!-- p.164 -->
are forbidden, but that inward vices also are accused and condemned, as uncleanness of heart, vicious affections, depraved inclinations, concupiscence. And this is the meaning: Not only let thy hands abstain from the violation of thy neighbour, of his body, his things, his means and his goods, whether movable or immovable, but also let there be a perfect agreement in the whole nature, so that the heart or the will do not so much as desire to hurt another, and that all unjust desire and affection of hurting be absent, and that there be present a true, perfect, pure, perpetual love of God and of the neighbour, in no wise hindered and in no wise counterfeit, a true study of helping, defending and preserving the body and the goods of thy neighbour.

Here let there be considered the difference between political laws and the divine law; then, what is called *concupiscence* in the doctrine of the Church; afterwards, who is named *neighbour*.

Political laws command only concerning outward actions, and are a bridle, of what sort soever, whereby outward discipline is ruled. But the divine laws command concerning entire obedience, inward and outward, and accuse depraved affections, doubtings concerning God, the burnings of minds in unjust hatreds or in lusts, as hath been said more largely above, in the *locus* on the Law, out of the fifth chapter of **Matthew**.

Concupiscence in general signifieth the appetite of good or of evil things. The appetite of good things is not forbidden, as to desire meat, drink, marriage, and other necessary and profitable things in their order; as when Isaac desireth Rebecca, Samson Delilah. For appetites were created in nature, and would have been also in nature unfallen, as the love of God, ***στοργαί*** (natural affections), just anger, zeal, etc. But evil concupiscence is accused, which is an ***ἀταξία*** (disorder) of all the powers of man, which ought to have been conformable unto the law of God. The virtues of this commandment are:

- Original righteousness.
- The lawful desire of good.
- <!-- p.165 --> Perfect cleanness of the human heart.
- Perfect love of God and of the neighbour, or obedience toward the whole Law.
- Perpetual mortification, and repentance.

### *Antithesis* · The Antithesis

Original sin. Evil concupiscence, which is an ***ἀταξία*** of all the powers in this depraved nature. The appetite of the things of others. Assent unto desires, and the tasting of the same. Security and impenitence.

Now although concupiscence is forbidden in the former commandments also, yet a distinction is to be made between the fountain, which is here stopped, and the rivulets, which in the remaining commandments are severally pointed out, that they may be dammed up. Therefore also it is thus said, that here *passive* concupiscence, in the other commandments *active* concupiscence is forbidden. But concerning concupiscence it hath been spoken in the *locus* on sin.

Further, every man is named *neighbour* who needeth our work and help, and who is to be helped by us with heart, with mouth, and in very deed. And he is named neighbour by reason of creation; then, of likeness; afterwards, of common life and of human society. But Christian neighbours are bound unto us by a closer kindred: by reason of the mystical body; then, of regeneration; afterwards, of the common vocation of the godly unto the unity of the Spirit in the bond of peace; and lastly, by reason of the glory to come.

All these things we ought therefore to consider, that, the evil of our origin being perceived, we may acknowledge and bewail it, and may flee for refuge unto the Son of God, our Lord Jesus Christ, the common and only Physician, praying that he would purify us with his precious blood, and endue us with his Holy Spirit, that we may be ready and cheerful to perform our duties unto our neighbour.

## *De X. Praecepto* · On the Tenth Commandment

The meaning of this commandment is the same as that of the preceding one, save
<!-- p.166 -->
that the ninth commandment is rightly referred to the genus, but this to the species, and to the things contained. For the ninth forbiddeth all desire which conflicteth with charity, and by name, in general, mentioneth the house of the neighbour, or his possession and quietness, or the place wherein our neighbour is, and liveth with his household. But the tenth expresseth the things contained in the house of the neighbour, and commandeth that no man, upon any occasion, under any show whatsoever, draw the things of another unto himself.

For the rest, the promises and threatenings added to the Decalogue are often repeated. **Levit. 18.** *"He that doeth these things shall live in them."* **Deut. 27.** *"Cursed is he that continueth not in all things which are written in the law."* But let all temporal promises be understood with the exception of the cross and of chastisement; the threatenings also with the exception of repentance and of conversion unto God, according to the doctrine of the Gospel.

Let it suffice to have briefly pointed out these things concerning the Decalogue. Now something is to be added concerning the Apostles' Creed, which among the parts of catechetical doctrine holdeth the second place. And that we may the more conveniently, in the exposition of the Creed also, speak ***χρήσιμα παισί*** (things profitable for children), let these questions be always before the eyes of the boys, which we shall subjoin as an *Anacephalaeosis* (a summing up) and repetition of all the former things of which it hath been spoken hitherto. And this very *Anacephalaeosis* may serve in the place of a Catechesis.

[^C5-1]: *Historia Augusta*, Life of Pescennius Niger, 10 (the life passes under the name of Aelius Spartianus).

[^C5-5]: Cicero, *De Oratore* II. 75–76: the Peripatetic Phormio lectured at Ephesus on the duties of a general in the presence of Hannibal, who judged him the greatest of all the doting old men he had seen. Selnecker means that a divine should not lecture soldiers on their own art.

[^C5-6]: Homer, *Iliad* IX. 63–64. The Latin verses that follow in the print, here Englished, are a free paraphrase of the two Greek lines.

[^C5-7]: Lucan, *Pharsalia* X. 407–408. The words *vel saltem rara* ("or at the least rare") are Selnecker's own insertion, and the received text of Lucan reads *proxima merces* ("the nearest wage") where he has *maxima merces*.

[^C5-8]: Eusebius, *Ecclesiastical History* V. 5. The print reads *exercitui Antonii*; the Emperor is Marcus Aurelius Antoninus.

[^C5-9]: Ferrandus of Carthage (d. 546/7), the letter to Count Reginus on the seven rules of innocence for a Christian commander.

[^C5-10]: So printed, in Latin letters; the Hebrew word is קִנְאָה (*qin'āh*), "jealousy, zeal".

[^C5-11]: The print reads *animi executionem*; the Greek ***διέγερσιν*** requires *excitationem*, "a rousing", and it is so translated.

[^C5-12]: So printed, in Latin letters; the Hebrew word in Jer. 48:10 is רְמִיָּה (*remiyyāh*), "slackness, deceit".

# *Quaestiones Catecheticae* · Catechetical Questions

## On Catechesis

### Whence is Catechesis so called?

From ***κατηχεῖν***, which is, to deliver some doctrine by the living voice, and to require it again of the hearers when it hath been delivered. ***ἠχεῖν*** signifieth to sound with the voice: whence is ***ἠχώ***, a sound, an echo, which giveth back the voice, or a resounding. Now both ***κατηχεῖν*** and ***κατηχίζειν*** are said, although the Apostles use the former only, and the Ecclesiastical writers the latter: whence also ***κατηχήτης*** and ***κατηχίστης***, *Catecheta* and *Catechista* (catechete and catechist), and ***κατήχησις*** and ***κατήχισμος*** (catechesis and catechism) are said.

<!-- p.167 -->

### Where is this word found in the Holy Scriptures?

**Luke 1**: *"I will write the history of the things done by Christ, that thou mightest know the certainty of the doctrine which thou hast received in Catechesis,"* ***περὶ ὧν κατηχήθης λόγων τὴν ἀσφάλειαν***. And **Galat. 6**: ***κοινωνείτω ὁ κατηχούμενος τὸν λόγον τῷ κατηχοῦντι ἐν πᾶσιν ἀγαθοῖς***, that is, let the Catechumens be thankful toward their Teacher, and maintain him who hath instructed them in Catechesis. And **Acts 19**, of Apollos, who had been initiated with the Baptism of John, it is written, ***κατηχημένος τὴν ὁδὸν τοῦ Κυρίου***, *"instructed in the way of the Lord."* And **Rom. 2**: ***κατηχούμενος ἐκ τοῦ νόμου***, *"instructed out of the law."* For they who are instructed are named ***κατηχούμενοι*** and ***κατηχήμενοι*** and ***κατήχητοι***; but they who instruct are named *Catechetae* (catechetes), such as of old the sponsors and godfathers also were, who were for that cause named Catechetes.

### Whence is the word ***κατηχεῖν*** taken?

From the ancient custom of the Patriarchs and of the holy Fathers even before Moses, who did not commit the mysteries of Religion to writings, but delivered them by the living voice to those who were already initiated in the sacred things (in the Old Testament the circumcised, in the New the baptized). Whence also *Cabala* was of old the name, which signifieth tradition, ***παράδοσιν***. And **Irenaeus** and others name the Creed the Apostolic tradition.

Hither pertaineth the excellent saying of **Epiphanius**, book 2, tome 1, against the Apotactics or Apostolics, such as are the Franciscans:[^D1-1] ***πάντα τὰ θεῖα ῥήματα οὐκ ἀλληγορίας δεῖται, ἀλλὰ ὡς ἔχει· θεωρείας δὲ δεῖτε, καὶ αἰσθήσεως, εἰς τὸ εἰδέναι ἑκάστης ὑποθέσεως τὴν δύναμιν, δεῖ δὲ καὶ παραδόσει κεχρῆσθαι.*** That is, the divine words need not allegory (or a foreign and alien sense, such as the Monks have feigned in a fourfold order, the Literal <!-- p.168 -->or Historical, the Tropological, the Allegorical, and the Anagogical). For the Moral law, the promises of grace, the Articles of faith, the Sacraments, and the Creeds or confessions, do not admit Allegories. But there is need of speculation, that is, of attention, reading, study, consideration of the context and of the members, of the definition, the division, the arguments, and of the whole order, according to that of **John 5**: *"Search,"* ***ἐρευνᾶτε***, that is, read, repeat, inquire, turn them over one with another (for this is ***ἐρευνᾶν***, from ***ἐρῶ***, I ask, and ***εὐνή***, a conversing and abiding, ***διατριβή, ὁμιλία καὶ συζήτησις*** (a spending of time, converse and joint inquiry), or ***συνουσία*** (a being together)). Paul nameth this ***θεωρείαν*** (speculation) ***διδασκαλίαν*** and ***ἔλεγχον***, **2 Timoth. 3**, that is, the certainty and confirmation of doctrine, and the judging or refutation of false opinions. Next there is need of sense, of practice, of the motion of the Holy Ghost, or, as Paul nameth it, ***παιδείᾳ*** and ***ἐπανορθώσει***, that is, of godliness and repentance, of the cross, of sorrow, faith, consolation, gladness. All these doth tradition serve, that is, Catechesis, and the testimonies of the Church, which confirm, illustrate, and propagate the doctrine.

### What then is Christian Catechesis?

It is the first instruction of Novices, wherein the sum of Christian doctrine, comprehended in a certain Method, is delivered to the more unlearned by the living voice of him that teacheth. Paul, **2 Timoth. 1**, calleth it ***ὑποτύπωσιν***, that is, a delineation, a form, a pattern of sound words, wherein the chief Articles of Christian doctrine are comprehended, being brought into one body.

### For what causes is Catechesis necessary?

The first cause is the commandment of God, **Psalm 68**: *"Bless ye God in the congregations, even the Lord, from the fountain of Israel."* **Isa. 8**: *"To the law <!-- p.169 -->and to the testimony."* **Matth. 10**: *"Hear ye him."* **1 Tim. 4**: *"Give attendance to reading, to exhortation, and to doctrine."*

The second cause is the worship of God, wherein God is most of all delighted, according to that saying: *"Suffer little children to come unto me."* And **Psal. 8**, **Matth. 21**: *"Out of the mouth of babes and sucklings thou hast perfected praise,"* that is, thou doest great things through the voice of the ministry of thy word.

The third cause is, that the knowledge of the doctrine may be confirmed and illustrated by frequent exercises, according to that of **Luke 19**: *"Unto him that hath shall be given,"* that is, to him that exerciseth the doctrine the light of the doctrine is increased. The little bees that make honey in the garden of the Lord

> *"drive from the hives the drones, a slothful herd."*[^D1-2]

The fourth, the propagation and preservation of the doctrine. **Psalm 78**: *"Our fathers have told us. We will not hide them from their children, and from the posterity, shewing the praises of the Lord, and his strength, and his wonderful works that he hath done: that the generation to come might know them, even the children which should be born, and should arise, and shall declare them to their children: that they might set their hope in God, and not forget the works of God, but keep his commandments."*

The fifth, the profit, which consisteth in these things:

1. In the receiving of the doctrine concerning the essence and Will of God, and concerning ourselves, the fall, death, the restoration, salvation.
2. In ***προασφαλίσει***, a fortifying beforehand and steadfastness against all temptations, heresies, calamities, death, according to that saying: *"Thy word is a lamp unto my feet, and a light unto my path."*
3. In godliness of life, according to that saying: *"Wherewithal shall a young man cleanse his way? By keeping thy words."*
4. In true consolation in all adversity, as it is written: *"This is my comfort in my affliction: for thy Word hath quickened me."*
5. In patience and the hope of eternal life.

Of all these Paul speaketh, **2 Tim. 3**: *"From a child thou hast known the holy scriptures, which are able to make thee wise unto salvation."* And **Roman. 15**: <!-- p.170 -->*"Whatsoever things were written were written for our learning, that we through patience and comfort of the scriptures might have hope."* The end of these is eternal life, which we find in Catechesis, or in the Reading of the holy Scriptures which testify of Christ.

Let the younger sort hold fast the saying of David, **Psalm 86**: *"Teach me thy way, O Lord; I will walk in thy truth: UNITE my HEART to fear thy Name."*

### How many, and which, are the chief heads of the Catechism?

Five: the Decalogue or Law, the Creed or Gospel, the Lord's Prayer or invocation, Baptism, and the Lord's Supper. **Psalm 116**: *"I said in my consternation, All men are liars."* (The Decalogue.) *"What shall I render unto the Lord for all his benefits toward me?"* (The Gospel.) *"I will take the cup of salvation, and call upon the name of the Lord."* (The Lord's Prayer.) *"I will pay my vows unto the Lord in the presence of all his people."* (Baptism, ***τὸ ἐπερώτημα***, the answer, of a good conscience.) *"Precious in the sight of the Lord is the death of his saints."* (Being founded in the precious Blood of the Son: the death, namely, of those who have been made partakers of the body and Blood of Christ crucified and dead for us.)

### Which are the most general heads of the heavenly doctrine?

The Law and the Gospel. **Isa. 8**: *"To the law and to the testimony."*

### To what places (*loci*) may all things which are read in the Holy Scriptures be most conveniently referred?

To six places.

1. The first is of God.
2. Of the creature.
3. <!-- p.171 -->Of the Church.
4. Of the Decalogue, and of the Gospel.
5. Of the Sacraments of the Old and New Testament.
6. Of the consummation, the judgment, eternal life and eternal death.

## On the Law, the First Part of the Catechism

### Which is the first part of the Catechism?

The doctrine of the divine law, or the Decalogue.

### Whence hath the Decalogue its name, and what doth it contain?

It hath its name from the number of the ten commandments, which in **Deuteron. 10** are named the ten words. And it containeth the sum of the Law, which is commonly called the moral law (*lex moralis*).

### What things are chiefly to be held concerning the law?

These especially.

1. The diverse signification of the word *law* in the Holy Scriptures.
2. What the Decalogue, or moral law, is.
3. Whether any man can satisfy the law, and why no man can satisfy it.
4. Of the use of the law.
5. How far the law is abrogated.
6. How Christ hath fulfilled the law.
7. What is the division of the law.
8. What is the difference of the law and the Gospel.
9. What are the parts of the law, and the explication of them.

### I. What are the significations of the word *law*?

Law in general signifieth doctrine: with the Hebrews *Thora*, from *Iara*, that is, he hath taught; as **Psal. 1**: *"In the law of the Lord is his delight and <!-- p.172 -->meditation day and night."* **Psalm 19**: *"The law of the Lord is undefiled,"* that is, the heavenly doctrine, or the word of God.

Law signifieth the Pentateuch, the five books of Moses, Genesis, Exodus, Leviticus, Numbers, Deuteronomy; as **Rom. 3**: *"The righteousness of faith hath witness in the Law and in the Prophets."* **Luc. 24**: *"All things must be fulfilled, which are written in the law of Moses, and in the Prophets, and in the Psalms, concerning me."*

Law set over against the Gospel signifieth the moral law, as: *"We are justified freely without the deeds of the law."* Set over against grace, it signifieth wrath and the curse, as: *"We are not under the Law, but under grace."* Set over against the Truth, and the time of Christ Incarnate, it signifieth the types and shadows of the law; likewise the Mosaic Polity, the kingdom and the priesthood, as **Gal. 3**: *"Before faith came, we were kept under the law."* Whence **Epiphanius**: ***οἱ τύποι ἐν τῷ νόμῳ, ἡ ἀλήθεια ἐν τῷ Εὐαγγελίῳ*** (the types are in the law, the truth in the Gospel). Likewise: Christ hath the body, but Moses the shadow of the body. *"The law was given by Moses, Grace and Truth came by Christ."*

Law sometimes ***μετωνυμικῶς*** (by metonymy) signifieth some force that impelleth, either to good or to evil, as: the law of the mind, the law of the spirit, the law of sin, the law of the members. The law of faith, that is, faith itself. The law of works, that is, good works.

### II. What is the Decalogue, or Moral law?

It is the eternal and immutable wisdom in God, which in the creation was poured over into rational minds, and afterward on mount Sinai was published by the voice of God in the year of the world 2454, 3081 years ago (reckoning backward from this current year 1572),[^D1-3] with great majesty, and confirmed with miracles; which discerneth things just and unjust, commanding what manner of persons we ought to be, what things we ought to do, what to leave undone; requiring entire obedience <!-- p.173 -->and agreement with the will of God which is expressed in the word itself; and pronouncing that God is horribly angry with sin, and punisheth in this life, and after this life condemneth with eternal punishments, all in whom there is not entire agreement, unless remission be made for Christ's sake; and promising rewards to the obedient.

### Why is it named Law (*Lex*)?

Because it ought to be read (*legi*) and set before the people continually, publicly and privately, that it may be known to all, and observed of all, and be the norm and rule of life and of actions. Therefore in **Deuteronom. 21** it is ordained that it be read every year in the solemn general assembly.

### Why is it called Moral?

Because it is the perpetual rule of living, according to which the manners (*mores*) of every man are to be squared.

### III. Can any man fulfil the law in this life?

No man, save Christ. **Psalm 14**: *"There is none that doeth good, no, not one."*

### How is it shewn that no man can satisfy the law in this life?

This is shewn first by the depravation of the nature of men through sin. *"For all have sinned, and come short of the glory of God." "They are all gone out of the way,"* **Rom. 3**. Next, by universal experience, because for sin we all are subject to the wrath of God and to death, **Psal. 90**: *"We are consumed in thine anger; all our days are passed away in thy wrath."* Thirdly, the same is shewn by all the testimonies of the whole Scripture, as: *"The imagination of man is evil from his youth." "They are all become unprofitable." "All men are liars."* <!-- p.174 -->*"In thy sight shall no man living be justified."* Fourthly, by the Sacrifice and Death of Christ. For if we could satisfy the law, Christ had died in vain, **Gala. 2**. Lastly, by the judgment of God, which can never be unjust.

### But what is to be determined concerning outward discipline? Is this possible?

Discipline is called the government of the locomotive power, that is, of the outward gestures, and it is outward obedience according to the commandments of God, in such wise that the outward members commit not the offences forbidden by the law of God. And this discipline is possible to all men, even to the unregenerate; but it is not the fulfilling of the Law, nor righteousness before God, nor doth it merit the remission of sins.

### But for what causes is discipline necessary?

For five causes. The first is the necessity of obeying the commandment of God. The second, the necessity of avoiding the punishments, present and eternal, wherewith God punisheth the violation of discipline. The third, the necessity of not troubling others, but of cherishing and preserving the common tranquillity and society. For foul is the word of Cain: *"Am I my brother's keeper?"* What is it to the Romans, that the Greeks perish? Rightly saith **Euripides**: ***ὁ γὰρ δίκαιος τοῖς πέλας πέφυκε ἀνήρ***. *"The just man is born for his neighbours."*[^D1-4] The fourth cause is, that we may be a good example to others, and invite them to virtue. The fifth cause is, that discipline may be a certain Pedagogy and leading by the hand unto Christ, not by its own virtue, but by reason of that which followeth. For men cannot be taught where there is no discipline, and the benefits of Christ cannot be understood without the acknowledgment of sin, nor be received and retained when a man liveth and persevereth in wickednesses against conscience.

<!-- p.175 -->

### IV. What is the use of the Moral law?

The use is threefold. The first is universal, outward, or political, or pedagogical. The second is Theological, inward, or spiritual. The third is proper to the Gospel in the regenerate.

### What is the Political use of the Law?

That which pertaineth to the outward man, even the unregenerate, or the Heathen, whom the Law governeth outwardly[^D1-5] in discipline, and bendeth to virtue, either by the love of honesty and the judgment of right reason, or by the fear of punishments, according to that saying, **1 Timoth. 1**: *"The law is good to him that useth it lawfully."* **Matth. 26**: *"He that taketh the sword shall perish with the sword."* **Isa. 33**: *"Woe to thee that spoilest: shalt not thou be spoiled?"* **Proverb. 12**: *"Lying lips are abomination to God."* **Hebr. 13**: *"Whoremongers and adulterers the Lord will judge."*

### What is the spiritual use of the law?

It is the use proper to the law, pertaining to the conscience of man, which the Law convicteth as guilty and condemneth for the whole depravation, the inward and the outward sins, laying open the judgment and wrath of God against sin, according to that saying, **Rom. 3**: *"By the law is the knowledge of sin."* **Rom. 4**: *"The law worketh wrath."* And this use of the Law is not in all men, but only in those who wrestle with sin and with the sense of the wrath of God, and either sink under the sorrow, as Cain, Saul, Judas, and rush into despair, or struggle out of it, laying hold of the promise of mercy for Christ's sake, as Peter, Mary Magdalene, the Thief on the cross, and the like.

### What is the third use of the Law, which regardeth the Regenerate?

It is to admonish the believers concerning true works, and to teach what <!-- p.176 -->worships please God, and withal to restrain and reprove the remnants of sin, that repentance may increase, faith in Christ being the guide and mistress; the fruits of which faith flow forth from this third use of the Law, as ministers of faith.

### How do the Regenerate begin the works commanded in the Law? And how doth the obedience begun please God?

The Regenerate first acknowledge by faith the Mediator, for whose sake the person is made acceptable to God. Next, the Holy Ghost being received, there is begun an obedience agreeing with the Law of God; and although it is imperfect, yet it pleaseth God in the person reconciled for the sake of the Son the Mediator, through faith. For when the person pleaseth, the work pleaseth.

### V. Is the Mosaic Law abrogated, and how far?

The whole Law and all its parts are abrogated, as touching the justification of men, and the condemnation of those who are converted. But the parts of the Mosaic Law are to be distinguished.

The Ceremonial Laws are rightly said to be abolished as touching the use, not as touching the signification, because the things figured by those Ceremonies endure for ever.

The Judicial Laws also are said to be abolished, or rather to be free, as touching the use. For the Magistrate may either use them or not use them. But in their signification they are perpetual and immutable. For the Mosaic Polity was a Type of the kingdom of Christ.

The Ceremonial and Forensic Laws are also said to be abolished as touching the species, not as touching the genus. For it is necessary that there be Ceremonies for the sake of good Order and of discipline, and it is necessary that there <!-- p.177 -->be Judgments and certain Laws. And that which in the ceremonial and judicial laws is natural or moral is perpetual and immutable. But the Moral Law hath two parts, namely the Rule of life, or the precept, and the Appendix concerning the promises and threatenings, to wit this:

> *"I am the Lord thy God, visiting the iniquity of the Fathers upon the children, unto the third and fourth generation of them that hate me, and keep not my commandments: and shewing mercy unto thousands of them that love me, and keep my commandments."*

LIKEWISE:

> *"He that doeth these things shall live in them."* And: *"Cursed is he that continueth not in all things which are written in the book of the Law."*

As touching therefore the former part of the Moral Law, that is, the norm of life, it is not abolished, but is perpetual and immutable, both in this and in the life to come. Therefore it is commonly wont to be said: The Moral Law is not abrogated as touching obedience. **1 Timoth. 1**: *"The sum of the Law is Love,"* saith Paul. This love of God and of our neighbour God willeth to be perpetual, **Deuteronom. 6**, **Matth. 22**. But as touching the Appendix of the Law, the promises are made sure to the godly in Christ, but the threatenings are abolished to them that are converted, according to that saying: *"There is now no condemnation to them which are in Christ Jesus."* Likewise: *"We are not under the law, but under grace."* Likewise: *"The law is not made for the righteous,"* that is, it doth not press them. And **Galat. 3**: *"Christ was made a curse for us."*

### V. [VI.] In how many ways doth Christ fulfil the law for us, and in us?[^D1-6]

<!-- p.178 -->

In five ways.

1. By perfect obedience toward the whole law.
2. By the turning of the punishment upon himself. For he fulfilled the law actively and passively, and is ***τέλος τοῦ νόμου***, the perfection of the law, superabundantly.
3. (Namely, imputatively.) By the imputation of his active and passive obedience and satisfaction.
4. (Namely, inchoatively.) By the gift of the Holy Ghost, or by the beginning whereby we begin to do the things which are of the Law.
5. (Namely, consummatively.) By the consummation in eternal life, according to that saying: *"We through the Spirit wait for the hope of righteousness by faith."*

### VII. What is the division of the Law?

1\. One Law is Ceremonial, *Kirchengepreng* (church ceremony); another Judicial or Forensic, *Statut und Recht* (statute and law); another Moral, which prescribeth to men the universal norm of living.

The Ceremonial Laws in Moses were precepts delivered by God concerning the outward rites of the Sacrifices, and of certain exercises in meat and apparel, given that they should endure for a certain time, so long as the Jewish Priesthood was to remain.

The Judicial Laws in Moses were precepts delivered by God concerning outward actions, governing this Civil society in the division of inheritances, in Contracts, in judgments, in punishments, etc., given that they should endure for a certain time, so long as the Mosaic Polity was to remain.

2\. Some Laws regard God immediately, others our neighbour. And this is the sum of the Moral Law, according to that saying:

> *"Thou shalt love the Lord thy God, and thy neighbour as thyself."*

<!-- p.179 -->

### VIII. How do the Law and the Gospel differ?

They differ in six ways. The first difference is taken from the knowledge of them: the Law is in some manner known by nature. But of the Gospel it is written: *"The Son, which is in the bosom of the Father, he hath declared it unto us."*

**To the contrary.**

> Paul saith, **Roman. 7**: *"I had not known lust to be sin, except the law had said, Thou shalt not covet."*
>
> Therefore the Law is not known by nature.

I answer. The Law is sometimes naturally known, as Paul saith: ***τὸ ἔργον τοῦ νόμου γραπτὸν ἐν ταῖς καρδίαις αὐτῶν*** (the work of the law written in their hearts), namely, as touching conscience, or the judgment concerning outward actions whether honest or dishonest. But the entire law, so far as pertaineth to the inward obedience, is not naturally known. Therefore the inward uncleanness or concupiscence, the imperfection, and the weakness, is not accounted to be sin whereby God is offended.

### Why was the Law given, seeing it is known by nature?

1. That the authority of the Law of nature might be confirmed by a new publication.
2. That those things which had begun to be darkened by the custom of sinning and by the multitude of the wicked might be made manifest.
3. That there might stand fast a certain manner of worshipping God.

**Another.**

> The power of God is naturally known, **Rom. 1**.
>
> Christ and the Gospel is the power of God, **Rom. 1**.
>
> <!-- p.180 -->Therefore the Gospel also is known to men by nature.

I answer to the Major: the power, namely of the creation and preservation of things, is naturally known. To the Minor: Christ is the power, namely, of Redemption and Salvation.

The second difference is taken from the matter. The Law principally commandeth, accuseth, condemneth. The Gospel principally promiseth, comforteth, saveth.

The third, from the form of the promises. The Legal promises are conditional, that is, they have joined to them the condition of the law fulfilled. But the condition of our works or merits the Evangelical promises have not, which are free, and require only faith, as the instrument whereby the mercy of God is to be laid hold on.

The fourth, from the form of the threatenings. The legal threatenings are without all exception and condition. The threatenings of the Gospel (which are referred to the Gospel by consequence and antithesis, and yet are made by the ministry of the law) have the exception of conversion and repentance. For this cause the law was graven in Tables of stone.

The fifth, from the objects. The Law is to be set before the secure and impenitent; the Gospel before the penitent.

The sixth, from the effects. The Law justifieth no man, saveth no man, but sheweth, accuseth, increaseth, and condemneth sin. But the Gospel is the power of God unto salvation to every one that believeth, and freely, for Christ's sake, giveth to the believers remission of sins, righteousness, and eternal life.

### IX. What are the parts of the Divine Law?

There are ten commandments, comprehended in two Tables, whereof the former containeth three commandments, which shew what <!-- p.181 -->worships are to be rendered immediately to God. For the first commandment preacheth of the knowledge of God in the mind, and of the motions or affections in the will and heart. The second commandeth concerning the profession or confession of that knowledge and of those affections. The third preacheth of the outward worships, and of the whole ministry whereby the propagation of the Heavenly Doctrine is made.

The latter Table commandeth concerning the duties toward our neighbour, and containeth seven commandments, whereof the order is this. The fourth fenceth the common society of men, and appointeth governors. The fifth fenceth bodies and life. The sixth defendeth marriages. The seventh, houses and goods. The eighth defendeth good name, and ordereth judgments. The ninth and tenth declare the meaning of the whole Decalogue, and shew that not only an outward, but an inward, pure, perfect, perpetual, and nowise hindered obedience is required, and that the inward uncleanness and concupiscence is condemned.

### Recite the Sum of the whole Table.

**Matth. 22**: *"Thou shalt love the Lord thy God with all thy heart, and with all thy soul, and with all thy mind. This is the first and great commandment. And the second is like unto it, Thou shalt love thy neighbour as thyself. On these two commandments hang all the Law and the Prophets."*

### Why is the second said to be like unto the first commandment?

This is not so by reason of the order, or of the object, or of the degree of love (for the love of God is in order the former, in object the worthier, and in degree higher than the love of man), but it is so by reason of the affection, the obligation, and the consequence. The affection ought to be pure. The obligation is either to obedience or to punishment. The <!-- p.182 -->consequence is, that he who loveth not his neighbour, the same loveth not God either.

### What things are to be considered in each several commandment of the Decalogue?

These five.

1. What virtues or works are commanded.
2. How they can be done, and how they please.
3. What sins are forbidden.
4. What promise obedience hath.
5. What threatening and punishment the violation hath.

Here a short Examination is to be instituted concerning good works, concerning the worships of God, concerning sin, concerning the promises and threatenings.

## On Good Works

### What is to be said concerning good works?

1. What good works are.
2. What is the efficient cause, and how they can be done.
3. What is the formal cause, and how they please God.
4. What are the final causes, and why they are to be done.
5. Of what men the obedience pleaseth, and of what men it pleaseth not, seeing all men have sin.

### I. What works then are good, which are to be done?

The works commanded by God himself, which are done with faith in Christ shining before, to this end, that God may be honoured, and the obedience due may be rendered to him.

### Are no works to be accounted good, which are not commanded by God?[^D1-7]

None. For it is written, **Ezech. 20**: *"Walk ye not in the statutes of your <!-- p.183 -->fathers, neither observe their judgments, nor defile yourselves with their idols: I am the Lord your God; walk in my statutes, and keep my judgments, and do them."* Likewise: *"In vain they do worship me with the commandments of men."*

[^D1-1]: Epiphanius, *Panarion*, heresy 61 (against the Apostolics, also called Apotactics), ch. 6. The Greek is given as the print spells it: ***θεωρείας*** and ***δεῖτε*** stand for ***θεωρίας*** and ***δεῖται***, and so ***θεωρείαν*** below.

[^D1-2]: Virgil, *Georgics* 4.168 (the same line at *Aeneid* 1.435): *ignavum fucos pecus a praesepibus arcent.* Selnecker runs the verse into his own sentence.

[^D1-3]: So the print, "1572", although this edition is dated 1579.

[^D1-4]: Euripides, *Heraclidae* 2, where the received text reads ***ὁ μὲν δίκαιος***.

[^D1-5]: The print has *aeterne* ("eternally"), an evident misprint for *externe* ("outwardly"), which the sense requires: the political use concerns the outward man and outward discipline.

[^D1-6]: The print numbers this question "V." a second time; it is the sixth of the nine points announced above ("How Christ hath fulfilled the law"), and the next is numbered "VII."

[^D1-7]: The print reads *quae à Deo praecepta sunt* ("which are commanded by God"); the answer ("None") and the texts cited shew that *non* has fallen out.

### How manifold are good works?

The kinds of good works are to be distributed according to the order of the commandments of the Decalogue, as shall be shown a little hereafter in the table following. But in general good works are threefold, namely: works of the love of God; works of charity toward the neighbour; works of every man's calling.

### What are the works of the love of God?

Those which are contained in the first table of the Decalogue.

### What are the works of charity toward the neighbour?

Those which are commanded in the second table, concerning which these testimonies are to be held fast. **Luke 6**: *"Be ye merciful, as your Father also is merciful."* **Matt. 9**: *"I will have mercy, and not sacrifice."* **Prov. 14**: *"He that hath pity upon the poor lendeth unto the Lord."*[^D2-1]

### Who is called our neighbour?

Every man, whether known or unknown, whether he hath deserved well or ill of us, who standeth in need of our counsel and help; whom we ought to help with heart, mouth, and deed, by thinking well of him, by speaking well of him, and by succouring him either with counsel or with the thing itself.

### For what causes is he called neighbour?

Because, first, all men are bound one to another by a <!-- p.184 -->threefold bond: of creation, of likeness, and of the common life or human society. Secondly, Christians are moreover bound one to another by a fourfold bond, namely:

1. Of the mystical body, whose head is Christ.
2. Of regeneration.
3. Of the common calling unto the unity of the Spirit in the bond of peace.
4. Of the glory to come.

### What are the works of the calling?

Those which every man's lawful function, whether ecclesiastical, or political and civil, or domestic (*oeconomica*), requireth, *"with all lowliness and meekness,"* **Ephes. 4**.

### How manifold is the calling?

Twofold: common and personal.

### What is the common calling?

It is the calling whereby we all and every one have been called out of the world by the voice of the Gospel, that we may be Christians, that is, that we may perform kingly and priestly offices, in faith in Christ, the highest King and Priest.

### What are the kingly offices of Christians?

1. To be angry with oneself because of sin.
2. With courageous faith and firm confidence to lay hold on the shield, which is the promise of mercy for the Son's sake.
3. To have dominion over sin, the flesh, and the world.
4. In the victory of Christ patiently and valiantly to bear the cross, to overcome the devil and death.

Being clothed with righteousness, to be a good example unto others. These kingly offices are most elegantly described in the Christian soldier, **Ephes. 6**.

### What are the priestly offices of Christians?

<!-- p.185 -->

To teach, to call upon God, and to offer unto God:

1. A broken spirit.
2. The sacrifice of righteousness, or an humble faith, laying hold on the merit of Christ and applying it to itself.
3. The sacrifice of praise and of confession.
4. The sacrifices of beneficence, which are the offices of charity.
5. The reasonable sacrifice of their own bodies, that is, the new obedience in general.

These priestly offices have for their foundation humility in the blood of Christ, even as the kingly offices have fortitude in the same blood of Christ.

### Why is the new obedience of Christians called a sacrifice in holy Scripture?

For four causes:

1. That we may know that our obedience pleaseth God for the Son's sake.
2. That we may know that obedience is earnestly required by God.
3. That the godly may be discerned from the ungodly.
4. That there may stand forth a testimony of our thankfulness toward God.

### Of what sort are the sacrifices of Christians?

They are all ***εὐχαριστικά***, that is, of praise. For the right of teaching the Word of God (every man's calling being kept), and the right of calling upon God, and the right of offering the sacrifice of praise, proceed from a thankful mind, which acknowledgeth the immeasurable mercy of God. But the sacrifice of Christ alone is ***ἱλαστικόν***, or propitiatory, meritorious, and expiatory.

### What is the personal calling?

It is the lawful and faithful discharge of some office proper to a certain person, commanded and founded in the Word of God, done in faith and charity, patiently bearing adversities, and directed to the glory of God and the profit of the commonwealth, the help of God being implored.

<!-- p.186 -->

### What are the degrees of the personal calling?

There are three chief degrees, of which it is said:

> *Thou, pray as a suppliant; thou, protect; and thou, labour.*  

And Shem betokened the ecclesiastical, Japheth the political, Ham the domestic kind of life; as did also Abel, Seth, and Cain.

### What are the kinds of the political calling?

Every commonwealth (*politia*) is either:

- **Monarchy**
    - lawful, ***βασιλεία*** (kingship);
    - violent, ***τύραννις*** (tyranny).
- **Oligarchy**
    - ***ἀριστοκρατία*** (aristocracy), lawful;
    - ***τιμοκρατία ἢ ὀλιγαρχία*** (timocracy or oligarchy), violent.
- ***Δημοκρατία*** (democracy)
    - ***πολιτεία ἢ δημοκρατία*** (polity or democracy);
    - ***ὀχλοκρατία ἢ ἀναρχία*** (mob-rule or anarchy).

### How do the degrees of dignity differ in the ecclesiastical function?

The power of all ministers is the same, and there is but one head and one High Priest, Jesus Christ; but the orders of dignity are unequal, and these consist in three things:

1. In the excellency of gifts.
2. In the greatness of labours.
3. In the diversity of calling.

This pre-eminence is not of lordship, but of ministry.

### What is the household government (*oeconomia*)?

It is the administration and government of a private house, ***παρὰ τὸ τὸν οἶκον νέμεσθαι*** (so named from "ordering the house"), instituted by God in Paradise, the fountain of the other orders in mankind, the Church and the commonwealth, <!-- p.187 -->consisting of three yokes, as it were: husband and wife; parents and children; servants and masters; which have their authority and their degrees of offices from God, to this end, that it may be a school wherein men may be instructed concerning God and good things, and may become temples of God, and may serve the life and society of men, and be saved for ever.

### II. How can good works be done?

As the Law sheweth what works are to be done, so the Gospel sheweth when works can be done, namely in these ways:

1. By the consideration of the commandment of God.
2. By the knowledge of Christ and by confidence, or faith in Christ.
3. By quickening (*vivificatio*), that is, by the presence and efficacy of the Holy Ghost, who enlighteneth the mind with the knowledge of Christ and with faith, and moveth the will to the beginning of a willing obedience, and kindleth invocation in us.

For this cause it hath been commonly said that there are three causes of good works:

1. The Word of God, which is as it were the body.
2. The Holy Ghost, who is as it were the soul of the Word.
3. The mind and will of man, which the Holy Ghost teacheth, bendeth, moveth, ruleth, leadeth, and in which and through which he putteth forth and bringeth forth his own actions.
