Hither belong the sayings: *"Whatsoever is not of faith is sin."* *"Without faith it is impossible to please God."* Likewise: *"Without me ye can do nothing."*

### How were the heathen able to do good works?

The virtues of the heathen are not called in the holy Scriptures good works pleasing to God. For these properly are such as are done of faith in Christ. But the virtues of the heathen, even the most splendid, are comprehended under this rule: *"Whatsoever <!-- p.188 -->is not of faith is sin."* For although the work seemeth to be good, and in respect of the work considered by itself is worthy of praise and admiration, yet, seeing the person is not reconciled, the work also which is performed by a person not yet reconciled is not called by the Holy Ghost a good work which can please God, but is referred only to outward discipline, which is a good of nature, not a good of grace. But grace being taken away, the whole of nature is under sin.

**On the contrary** (*Contra*):

> The things which are of the Law are good.  
> The works which the heathen did are works of the Law, **Rom. 1**.  
> Therefore they are good works.  

**I answer.** I grant the whole, if we understand the good of nature as created by God, which even after the fall doth yet in some manner remain, and is required for the preservation and tranquillity of human society. But that same good doth not prove that nature is good and without sin. And seeing nature is corrupt, it cannot be made good save by grace. If the good of grace be wanting, the good of nature is nothing else before God than that which **Paul** saith: *"Whatsoever is not of faith is sin."* Therefore **Augustine** saith excellently: *"Human righteousness compared with the divine righteousness is unrighteousness; for a lamp also is seen to shine in the darkness, but set in the beam of the sun it is darkened."* And **Ambrose** saith: *"Not they are righteous who hear the Law; but they who believe in Christ, whom the Law promised, these do the Law."* And the rule is most true: Whatsoever is not perfect, pure, willing, perpetual, and in no wise hindered, that is rejected and condemned by the voice of the divine Law, that is, it is sin.

### How do the virtues of the heathen differ from the good works of Christians?

<!-- p.189 -->

They differ in four ways, namely: 1. in the impelling causes; 2. in the efficient causes; 3. in the form; 4. in the end.

The **impelling causes** which bend the heathen and the unregenerate to the pursuit of virtue are four. The first is the dignity of virtue. The second is the reverence due toward superiors and those who have deserved well: magistrates, parents, teachers, the aged. The third is the reward set before honesty, whether inward, as tranquillity of mind and gladness of conscience, or outward, as a good name. The fourth is punishment, which cometh at length with silent feet.[^D2-2] These four causes are not disallowed by Christians, but are adorned and made more illustrious, six other causes being added besides, which are:

1. The commandment of God.
2. The end of creation, redemption, and sanctification.
3. The remembrance of the divine mercy.
4. The glory of God.
5. The nature and safety of faith, of which he that violateth godliness maketh shipwreck.
6. The sure hope of the glorious resurrection, and of the judgment to come, and of the account to be rendered.

The **efficient causes** of virtue in the unregenerate are two: 1. the judgment of right reason; 2. the freedom of the will, which in some manner bridleth the outward members. But in the regenerate they are the Word of God, faith, and the Holy Ghost.

The **form** of the virtues in the unregenerate:

1. Is altogether maimed, and is mutilated and imperfect not only in quality, but also in knowledge, in number, and in relation.
2. Is hypocritical, and defiled with many false opinions concerning God and with depraved lusts.
3. Is very seldom willing, but for the most part wrung out by the fear of punishment.
4. Is destitute of invocation and of confidence in the divine help.
5. Is destitute also of the humility of a mind acknowledging its weakness and imperfection and its own uncleanness.

But the form of the virtues in the regenerate:

1. Is entire, as touching the knowledge of all the virtues, inward and outward, and as touching the number, and the relation to God and to the neighbour and to oneself, although the quality be not perfect; which nevertheless <!-- p.190 -->they have perfect in Christ.
2. Next, the regenerate hate all hypocrisy, and endeavour to purge out the old leaven, that is, depraved lusts and opinions.
3. Afterward, they render a willing obedience, not being constrained, according to that saying: *"Though he slay me, yet will I trust in him."*
4. Fourthly, the form of the virtues in the regenerate is always joined with invocation and with confidence in the divine aid.
5. Fifthly, the regenerate humble themselves, and confess that they are unprofitable servants.

As the **end** of their virtues the unregenerate have principally their own glory; whence also they use the name of virtue (*virtus*), taken from *vir*, a man, even as the Greeks say ***ἀρετήν*** from ***ἀρέσκω***, "I please", and the Germans *Tugend*, that is, that which is becoming, that it may be one's own honour and ornament. But the regenerate have as the end of their good works the glory of God, the hallowing of the divine Name, and also the profit of the neighbour.

### III. How do good works please God?

Good works please God in the converted. For without faith it is impossible to please God, **Heb. 11**. Therefore **Peter**, in his first Epistle, chapter 2, saith: *"Offer up spiritual sacrifices, acceptable to God for Christ's sake,"* who is both the Propitiator and now maketh intercession for us. The person therefore being reconciled and pleasing, the work pleaseth. The person not pleasing, neither doth the work please.

### What rules are to be held, when it is asked whether and how good works please?

Four rules.

The first: Let the person that doeth good works be assured that he pleaseth God freely, for Christ's sake, through faith.

The second: Let him acknowledge and bewail the manifold sins <!-- p.191 -->yet cleaving in us, which hinder us, that we cannot satisfy the Law of God.

The third: Let him be assured that God requireth also the obedience begun, which agreeth with the Law.

The fourth: Let him know that this obedience begun pleaseth God by faith for Christ's sake, not for the worthiness of the work.

### IIII. Why are good works to be done?

The causes are four: 1. The glory of God. 2. Necessity. 3. Dignity. 4. Reward.

Of the glory of God the Son of God saith, **Matt. 5**: *"Let your light shine before men, that they may see your good works, and your Father which is in heaven may be glorified."* And **1 Cor. 10**: *"Do all to the glory of God."*

### How manifold is the necessity?

Fivefold:

1. Of the commandment, as: *"Walk in my statutes."*
2. Of debt, **Rom. 8**: *"We are debtors to God, not to the flesh."*
3. Of not losing faith, **1 Tim. 1**: *"War a good warfare; holding faith, and a good conscience."*
4. Of avoiding punishments present and eternal.
5. Of regeneration, which is the beginning of a new life in true conversion.

### What is the dignity?

It is this, that the converted man is a son of God, and a temple of the Spirit of the living God, in whom Christ dwelleth by faith, and an instrument of salvation to his own soul and to others, and a member of Christ, having the holy Angels as companions and defenders of his life. To be willing to deface this dignity is devilish.

### What is the reward?

The reward is manifold: bodily, temporal, <!-- p.192 -->spiritual, eternal. Therefore it is written, **1 Tim. 4**: *"Godliness hath promise of the life that now is, and of the life eternal."* Life indeed is not given for works' sake, because eternal life is the gift of God; but in the present life and in the eternal there is made a free recompense of the obedience of believers.

### Is it rightly said: Good works are necessary to salvation?

Apart from controversy, many orthodox men have used this phrase, understanding the same thing as **Paul**: *"With the mouth confession is made unto salvation."* For they took the word "salvation" not of the righteousness of man before God, which resteth upon faith in Christ alone, but of the life to come. But at this day, seeing that phrase hath begun, at an unseasonable and unhappy time, to come into controversy,[^D2-3] it is for that cause rightly omitted:

1. Because it is ambiguous.
2. Because merit is understood by it.
3. Because it is used by the adversaries, who will have our works to be meritorious of salvation and eternal life.
4. Because it offendeth and troubleth the simpler and more unlearned.
5. Because it is rather legal than evangelical.
6. Because it breedeth and increaseth dissensions in the Church, even among good and orthodox men.

### Is it rightly said: Good works are hurtful to salvation?

Many have set this phrase over against the former, meaning that, if confidence be placed in good works, they become and are pernicious to salvation, because the self-righteous (*iustitiarii*) and hypocrites are not saved, seeing they do not repent. But since the phrase is absurd (because that which is pernicious cannot be good; and because the good works of believers are fruits of righteousness and of the Holy Ghost; likewise, because when the confidence of obtaining <!-- p.193 -->remission of sins and eternal life is placed in them, they are not good works proceeding from faith and pleasing to God), it is therefore rightly omitted.

### Why doth Scripture vouchsafe unto good works the honour of a reward?

Because God promiseth and rendereth, for duty and labour that is owed, rewards and a recompense that is free, not owed, **Luke 17**; but of free charity and bounty, not of debt or of condign desert.

### V. Whose obedience pleaseth, and whose not?

Sins are to be distinguished. There are in the converted and in the saints many remnants of sins, and vicious affections, which they resist, lest they should rush on against conscience. And although the uncleanness of the saints is no light evil, but such as that of which it is said: *"In thy sight shall no man living be justified,"* yet the Son of God is a covert unto believers, who by faith receive the imputation of righteousness. The sins of these are venial. But other sins, in the unconverted, are done against conscience, and are mortal. And when the converted fall into these, either falling away from the foundation, that is, from some article of the faith, whether they understand it or understand it not, being deceived by the juggleries of others; or rushing into heinous crimes, as David into adultery and murder; then they cast out the Holy Ghost, they cast away faith, and become guilty of the wrath of God, and, unless they repent again, they rush into eternal punishments.

## *De Cultibus Dei* · On the Worship of God

<!-- p.194 -->

### What is the worship of God?

The worship of God (*cultus Dei*) is a work commanded by God, done of faith, that is, in the true knowledge of and confidence in the Mediator, which is referred principally to this end, that honour may be given to God, that is, that we may testify that he is truly God whom we thus worship, and that he willeth to be thus worshipped.

### What things are required unto the true worship of God?

Three: the commandment of God; faith in Christ; the celebration and honour of the divine Name.

### Give the rules concerning the worship of God.

1. No worship pleaseth God, save that which he himself hath instituted by his own Word.
2. All ***ἐθελοθρησκεῖαι*** (will-worships) and superstitions, which are without the Word of God, are condemned by God.
3. The foundation of the true worship of God is the acknowledgment of God and the knowledge of ourselves.
4. The knowledge of God is to be sought from the Word and testimony, which sheweth that God is the fountain of all good, and is most ready to help, and willeth that we flee unto him, and that all glory is due unto him.
5. The knowledge of ourselves is to be sought from the end of creation, or from our duty, which is to bear the image of God in sincere righteousness and godliness; next, from the estimation of our own powers, who are not able to perform the duty that is owed; afterward, from the easiness of falling into sin.
6. Faith is the worker of all true divine worship.
7. Faith maketh the person pleasing to God, and rendereth a willing and voluntary worship, and joineth the worship of the mind with the outward work, that it may become a spiritual worship.
8. <!-- p.195 -->All worship without faith, whether it be commanded by God in respect of the work, or brought in from elsewhere, is to be condemned, even as God condemneth sacrifices, **Isa. 11**;[^D2-4] **Ps. 50**.
9. All worship ought to tend unto the celebration of the glory of God.

### What is it to glorify God?

It is to think rightly of the essence, majesty, and veneration of God, and to ascribe unto him all wisdom, mercy, and righteousness, to obey him, and by a godly example to invite others to acknowledge God.

### What is it to take away his glory from God?

1. Not to think rightly of the essence and majesty of God.
2. Not to acknowledge the virtues and benefits of God.
3. To live wickedly against the commandment of God.
4. To be a stumblingblock unto others.
5. Not to ascribe unto Christ the glory of the Redemption, and the divine Majesty, which he hath bestowed also upon his human nature.
6. In bodily things to claim the success for one's own counsels, labours, and wisdom, and for one's own fortune.

### How manifold is the worship of God?

Twofold. The expiatory, or meritorious, or propitiatory, in Greek ***ἱλαστικός***, that is, the obedience of the Son of God, which meriteth eternal life for all that believe. And the eucharistic, of all the sons of God, which of itself meriteth nothing, but of grace hath the promises of the present life and of that which is to come.

### How manifold is the eucharistic worship?

Twofold: inward and outward. The inward is a motion of the mind and heart, agreeing with the Law of God. The outward is an <!-- p.196 -->outward action, agreeing with the Law and will of God. In **1 Thess. 1** **Paul** ascribeth to the inward worship three affections: faith, charity, and hope. To the outward worship he ascribeth three effects of those affections: ***ἔργον*** (work) to faith, ***κόπον*** (labour) to charity, ***ὑπομονήν*** (patience) to hope. Work, or virtue, and labour, and patience are effects of the inward worship, and constitute the outward worships, so that these also become spiritual.

## *De Peccato* · On Sin

### What is sin?

Whatsoever is against the Law of God, **1 John 3**: ***ἁμαρτία ἐστὶν ἀνομία*** (*"sin is the transgression of the law"*); that is, sin is a defect, or corruption, or inclination, or action against the Law of God, offending God, and deserving the wrath of God and eternal punishments, unless remission be made for the sake of the Son, the Mediator.

### How manifold is sin?

Twofold: original, and actual, or personal, as **Cyprian** calleth it.

### What is original, or hereditary, sin?

It is, in all men born of the seed of man, both the corruption of human nature propagated from Adam (in the mind the loss of light, or the want of the knowledge of God and of firm assent concerning Providence, the promises, and the threatenings; in the will a turning away from God, void of the fear of God, of confidence in him, and of love toward him; in the heart concupiscence, contumacy, and violent motions against the divine order) and also the guilt (*reatus*) whereby they that are born are, because of that corruption of the whole nature, guilty of the wrath of God and of eternal death, unless remission be made for the sake of the Son of God, the Mediator.

<!-- p.197 -->

### Why is it called original sin?

For two causes. First, in respect of original righteousness, to which original sin is opposed. For as original righteousness before the fall was the integrity of the whole human nature, and the approbation whereby God had his ***εὐδοκίαν*** (good pleasure) in man, so the sin of our origin is the corruption of the whole human nature, and guilt. Next, because, being propagated from Adam, it hath infected all men, according to that saying of David: *"In sin did my mother conceive me."*

### What is called the material and the formal of original sin?

The material (*materiale*) is the corruption itself, the deformity and ***ἀταξία*** (disorder). The formal (*formale*) is the guilt, that is, the being ordained unto damnation. Whence it is said that in Baptism the formal of sin is taken away, the material remaining.

### How doth Scripture name original sin?

The chief names are these: concupiscence, the flesh, the old man, the law of sin, the law of the members, indwelling sin, rebellion, sin absolutely so called.

### Is sin the substance of man, seeing it is called the flesh and the old man?

In no wise is it the substance of man. For the substance and nature of man was created by God, and the Son of God assumed it, namely our flesh, without sin. But sin is an adventitious quality, whereby human nature hath been <!-- p.198 -->deformed, and made guilty of wrath and of eternal death. But "the flesh" and "the old man" are used by metonymy: the flesh for the viciousness or rebellion of the flesh; the old man for unrighteousness and sin. For the name of the substance is put for the adjunct, and the cause for the effect; which is to be observed for the confuting of the Manichees, who referred those names to the substance.

### What is the efficient cause of sin?

There are two causes: the tyranny of the devil, and the will of men that obey the devil. But God is not the cause of sin, neither doth he will, nor approve sin, nor move any to sin, but he hateth, abhorreth, and punisheth sin, **Ps. 5**: *"Thou art not a God that hath pleasure in wickedness. Thou hatest all workers of iniquity."*

### What is actual sin?

It is every action, whether inward, as an evil thought, doubting, concupiscence, or outward, that fighteth against the Law of God.

### What are the causes of outward sins?

1. The blindness and darkness of the human mind, or errors.
2. Depraved lusts.
3. The instigation of the devil.

### What is the matter *in which* of sin?

The mind and will of man, the heart, the faculties, and all the outward members.

### What is the form of sin?

Guilt, that is, the obligation unto wrath and eternal punishment.

### What are the effects of sin?

The wrath of God. The tyranny of the devil. All the calamities of mankind. Concupiscence, or the ***ἀταξία*** (disorder) of all the powers, which <!-- p.199 -->is at once sin and the punishment of sin. The death of the body. Eternal damnation.

### Rehearse other divisions of sin.

One sin is mortal, another venial. This division pertaineth not only to the regenerate, but to the unregenerate also. For although all the sins of the unregenerate, original and actual, are mortal or ***φονικά*** (deadly), yet when the unregenerate are converted, their sins become venial. In like manner, although all the sins of the regenerate are venial for Christ's sake, yet when they assent to an error in the foundation, or fall against conscience, they cease to be regenerate, that is, they let go grace, the Holy Ghost, and faith, and become guilty of the wrath of God and of eternal punishments, unless they repent and be converted unto God. Wherefore their sins become mortal, if they return not unto God.

### What is mortal sin, or sin reigning and having dominion?

In the unregenerate it is any sin whatsoever, whether original or actual, according to that saying, **John 3**: *"He that believeth not the Son, the wrath of God abideth on him."* But in the regenerate it is an error in the foundation, and a fall against conscience, for which sin a man casteth out the Holy Ghost, and loseth faith, and becometh guilty of wrath and of eternal death, unless he be converted unto God.

### What is venial sin?

The same as original sin in the regenerate, such as are the affections that sprout forth, and the errors that befall, to which the saints do not assent, but which they resist. Likewise, in all that turn themselves unto God, that also becometh a venial sin which before conversion was mortal, and of its own nature would bring death, did not the Father, being gracious for the Son's sake, grant pardon unto them that believe and by faith wrestle against mortal sin.

<!-- p.200 -->

### In how many ways do men sin mortally?

In two ways. Remissibly, or venially, when a man is converted unto God, repenting, and wholly ceaseth to sin that sin. And irremissibly, namely when a man persevereth in sins against conscience without repentance.

### How manifold is irremissible sin?

Twofold. The first is every sin against conscience in which a man persevereth without repentance, and dieth in it. Next, there is a certain special sin, irremissible and unto death, which is called the sin against the Holy Ghost, of which there are principally three degrees. The first of them is, knowingly and willingly to set oneself against the divine truth acknowledged and evident, and to contradict it, to persecute and to hate it. The second is, knowingly to fall away from the heavenly truth to gain the favour of men, and not to return unto it. The third is, to despair of the grace of God, and to charge him with falsehood in his promises, and in that despair to depart out of life, and finally to resist the Holy Ghost.

### Are there more divisions of sins?

Some sins are of commission, when those things are done which are forbidden in the Law. Others are of omission, when those things are not done which are commanded in the Law.

**Likewise:** Some are of infirmity. Others of ignorance. Others of malice, which are done ***ἐκ προαιρέσεως*** (of deliberate purpose).

**Likewise:** Some are voluntary, others involuntary, which a man resisteth, and wherein he taketh no delight at all.

<!-- p.201 -->

### In how many ways is sin accused by the Holy Ghost?

In eight ways chiefly:

1. In every man's conscience.
2. By the voice of the Law, or by the Word.
3. By the setting forth of the causes why the Son of God was made man.
4. By the divine threatenings in the Word; by signs in heaven, in the air, in the waters, in the earth.
5. By the rod, ***λιμῷ, λοιμῷ, καὶ πολέμῳ*** (by famine, pestilence, and war), and by every man's cross, whether inward or outward: the loss of goods, the losing of friends, diseases, infamy.
6. By the tragical examples of others.
7. By the degeneration of all things.
8. By death.

## *De Promissionibus et Comminationibus* · On the Promises and Threatenings

### Rehearse the rules concerning the divine promises and threatenings.

The first is general. All the divine promises and threatenings, in that form and sense in which God hath delivered and interpreted them, are true, certain, ratified, firm, and unmoved, because God is true.

The second. The interpretation of the legal promises and threatenings is to be taken from the Gospel.

The third. The Gospel sheweth that the legal promises, although they have annexed the condition of entire obedience, which no man can perform in this life, are nevertheless ratified and firm unto them that believe in the Son, **2 Cor. 1**: <!-- p.202 -->*"All the promises of God in Christ are Amen."* **Rom. 3**: *"We establish the law through faith."* For the whole fulfilling of the Law consisteth in the imputation of the righteousness of Christ; and the obedience begun also agreeth with the Law of God, and hath the promises of the present life and of that which is to come.

The fourth. The promises of grace, of reconciliation, and of eternal life for Christ's sake are fulfilled unto believers without any condition and exception, and are to be held fast with most firm faith; and they are universal and free.

The fifth. The promises of bodily things have a fourfold exception:

1. Of the punishment of the ungodly.
2. Of the chastisement and cross of the godly.
3. Of the liberty that is in God, to whom neither the manner nor the time is to be prescribed wherein he ought to bestow the good things promised.
4. Of the divine calling. For bodily goods are not to be snatched by our own counsels without the divine calling, as the sons of Ephraim, invading the promised land without a calling, are slain, **1 Paral. [1 Chron.] 7**.

The sixth. To the threatenings of eternal malediction the Gospel opposeth the universal and free promise of the remission of sins for the sake of the Son of God, the Mediator, who took those threatenings upon himself, sustained them, overcame them, and made perfect satisfaction for them.

The seventh. The threatenings of bodily punishments annexed to the Law have the condition of repentance, or an exception out of the Gospel concerning conversion, whereby always either mitigation of calamities or deliverance from them is obtained for the sake of the Son, who hath made entire satisfaction for all threatenings and punishments; and in whom, as the promises are fulfilled, so the threatenings cease.

### For what causes hath God annexed promises of bodily good things to good works?

<!-- p.203 -->

For five causes.

The first is, that they may be testimonies of the divine providence. For God is the author and preserver[^D2-5] of this bodily life, neither are bodily goods scattered by chance, but they are distributed by the sure counsel of God.

The second cause, that they may be testimonies that God willeth to preserve the Church in this life also, for whose sake he feedeth the rest of men likewise.

The third, that we may be put in mind of faith, invocation, hope, and thanksgiving. For God willeth that bodily goods also be asked and looked for by faith, and that we be exercised in faith, hope, expectation, patience, humility.

The fourth, that they may be admonitions concerning the promise of grace, or of spiritual good things, according to that saying: *"Whatsoever ye shall ask the Father* IN MY NAME *(here faith in Christ shineth before) he will give it you."*

The fifth, that we may know both that the Church is subject to the cross, and yet that amid the very dangers she is wonderfully preserved in this life.

### For what causes hath God annexed bodily threatenings and punishments to evil works?

For six causes. The first is the righteousness of God. The second is, that there may be a difference between virtues and vices, and that in this difference every man may know what manner of God he is, namely, good, righteous, chaste. The third, that there may stand forth admonitions concerning the eternal punishments. The fourth, that repentance may be done and the life amended. The fifth, that the public tranquillity may be preserved. The sixth, that others, being deterred by the examples of horrible punishments, may avoid the offences by which punishments are drawn down.

## *Decalogus* · The Decalogue

<!-- p.204 -->

### Why is the Decalogue to be continually meditated upon?

1. Because it admonisheth us concerning God, who and what manner of God he is.
2. It teacheth us unto what end human nature was created, and what manner of creature man was before the fall.
3. It is the rule of life and the governess of manners.
4. It is the mirror of sin.
5. It driveth us to seek the Mediator, who fulfilleth the Law for us. For the Law is a schoolmaster unto Christ.
6. It kindleth invocation, whereby we ask that conformity with the Law may be restored in us.

### Rehearse the rules concerning the Decalogue.

1. The first Table is always to be preferred before the second Table.
2. The first commandment is to be included in all the rest. For it is for GOD's sake that obedience is to be rendered in all the commandments. And in the violation of every commandment there is contained the contempt of God in the first commandment.
3. In the negatives the affirmative sense is contained withal, and in the affirmatives the negative sense is contained withal, that is, the thesis and the antithesis.

### Rehearse the words of the Decalogue.

**Exod. 20. Deut. 5.**

**I.**

> I am the Lord thy God, which brought thee out of the land of Egypt, from the house of bondage. Thou shalt have none other gods before my face. Thou shalt not make thee any graven image, or any likeness of the things that are in heaven above, or whatsoever are in the earth beneath, or that are in the waters beneath the earth. Thou shalt not <!-- p.205 -->bow down thyself unto them, nor serve them. For I am the LORD thy GOD, a jealous God, visiting the sin of the fathers upon the children, unto the third and fourth generation of them that hate me; and shewing mercy unto thousands of them that love me and keep my commandments.

**II.**

> Thou shalt not take the name of the LORD thy God in vain. For the LORD thy God will not hold him guiltless that taketh his Name in vain.

**III.**

> Keep the sabbath day to sanctify it, as the LORD thy God hath commanded thee. Six days thou shalt labour, and do thy work: but on the seventh day is the sabbath of the LORD thy God. In it thou shalt not do any work, thou, and thy son, and thy daughter, thy manservant, and thy maidservant, thine ox, and thine ass, and any of thy cattle, and the stranger that is within thy gates; that so thy manservant and thy maidservant and thine ass may rest as well as thou, and that thou mayest remember that thou also wast a servant in the land of Egypt, and that thou wast brought out thence by the LORD thy God through a mighty hand and by a stretched out arm. Therefore the LORD thy God hath appointed thee to keep the sabbath day, and to sanctify it.

**IIII.**

> Honour thy father and thy mother, as the LORD thy God hath commanded thee; that it may go well with thee, and that thy days may be prolonged upon the land which the LORD thy God shall give thee.

<!-- p.206 -->

**V.**

> Thou shalt not kill.

**VI.**

> Thou shalt not commit adultery.

**VII.**

> Thou shalt not steal.

**VIII.**

> Thou shalt not bear false witness against thy neighbour.

**IX.**

> Thou shalt not covet thy neighbour's house.

**X.**

> Thou shalt not covet thy neighbour's wife, nor his field, nor his manservant, nor his maidservant, nor his ox, nor his ass, nor his beast, nor any thing that is thy neighbour's.

These words the LORD spake out of the midst of the fire, of the cloud, and of the thick darkness, with a great voice, writing them in two tables of stone, etc.

### Why was the Law written in tables of stone?

1. That the hardness of our hearts might be shewn, in which the Law of God cannot be written until God himself with his own finger move and bend our hearts, and write his Law in them.
2. That God might shew that the obedience which he requireth in the Moral Law is abolished by no length of time.
3. That the rigour and strict right, or burden, of the Law, which presseth us, and the hard voice and preaching of the Law, might be signified.
4. That a manifest difference between the Law and the Gospel might stand forth, according to that saying: *"I will take away from you the stony heart, and I will give you an heart of flesh."*

[^D2-1]: The words are those of Prov. 19:17; Selnecker's reference to chapter 14 is kept as printed (compare Prov. 14:21 and 14:31, which teach the same thing).

[^D2-2]: An echo of Tibullus, *Elegies* 1.9.4: *sera tamen tacitis Poena venit pedibus*, "yet Punishment, though late, cometh with silent feet."

[^D2-3]: The Majoristic controversy, which arose in the 1550s from Georg Major's defence of the proposition that good works are necessary to salvation; the counter-proposition treated in the next question, that good works are hurtful to salvation, was maintained by Nikolaus von Amsdorf. Both phrases were set aside in Article IV of the Formula of Concord (1577), of which Selnecker was a co-author.

[^D2-4]: So the print ("Esa. 11"), and the reference is kept as printed; the chapter in which God rejecteth the sacrifices is Isaiah 1 (verse 11).

[^D2-5]: The print has *conseratoru*, an evident misprint for *conservator*.

<!-- p.207 -->

# *Tabella, continens appellationes virtutum, et earum extrema, iuxta seriem praeceptorum Decalogi* · A Table Containing the Names of the Virtues, and Their Extremes, According to the Order of the Commandments of the Decalogue

The virtues, or good works, which are contained in the several commandments stand in the mean. But the extremes, in the defect, which is ***ἔλλειψις*** (a falling short), and in the excess, which is ***ὑπερβολή*** (an overshooting), are vicious, and show the sins against the several commandments and against each virtue of them. Now the definitions of the virtues and vices, illustrated by an exposition of their causes and by examples, let the young seek in the most learned little book of **D. Chytraeus**, which is entitled *Regulae vitae* (Rules of Life);[^E-1] which little book let them never suffer to be shaken out of their hands, for the sake of its manifold doctrine and godliness.

## *Virtutes primi praecepti* · The Virtues of the First Commandment

1. ***ἐπίγνωσις ἀληθὴς τοῦ θεοῦ*** — *Vera Dei noticia* — The true knowledge of God.
2. ***πίστις*** — *Fides* — Faith.
3. ***ἀγάπη*** — *Dilectio* — Love.
4. ***ἐλπίς*** — *Spes* — Hope.
5. ***φόβος θεοῦ*** — *Timor Dei* — The fear of God.
6. ***ταπείνωσις*** — *Humilitas* — Humility.
7. ***ὑπομονή*** — *Patientia* — Patience.
8. ***ἐπιμονὴ ἢ καρτερία*** — *Perseverantia sive constantia* — Perseverance, or constancy.

### *Extrema* · The Extremes

**The extremes contrary to the knowledge of God are:**

1. ***ἡ ἄγνοια τῆς οὐσίας καὶ βουλήσεως τοῦ θεοῦ*** — *Ignoratio Dei* — Ignorance of God [of the essence and will of God].
2. ***ἀσέβεια*** — *impietas, neglectio, et contemtus Dei* — Ungodliness, the neglect and contempt of God.
3. ***πᾶσαι πλάναι καὶ δόξαι ἀντιπαλαίουσαι τῷ τοῦ θεοῦ λόγῳ*** — *omnes errores et opiniones repugnantes verbo Dei* — All errors and opinions that fight against the Word of God.

**In excess** there is contrary to the knowledge of God that ***κενοσπουδία*** (vain busying), which is curiosity, searching into <!-- p.209 -->the hidden Essence and Will of God not revealed in the Word.

**Contrary to faith are:**

1. ***ἡ ἀπορία περὶ τοῦ θεοῦ, καὶ περὶ τῆς προνοίας, καὶ τῆς διδαχῆς καὶ ἐπαγγελίας*** — *dubitatio de Deo, et de providentia, et doctrina, et promissione* — Doubting concerning God, and concerning providence, and the doctrine, and the promise.
2. ***ἀπιστία καὶ κακοπιστία*** — *incredulitas et falsa fides* — Unbelief and false faith.
3. ***δυσελπιστία*** — *diffidentia* — Distrust.
4. ***ἀπόγνωσις καὶ ἀνελπία*** — *desperatio* — Despair.

**In excess:**

1. ***θράσος καὶ πρόληψις*** — *confidentia et praesumtio* — Overboldness and presumption.
2. ***πεποίθησις τῆς ἀνθρωπίνης βοηθείας*** — *fiducia humani auxilii* — Trust in the help of man.
3. ***εἰδωλολατρεία*** — *Idolorum cultura* — The worship of idols.
4. ***δεισιδαιμονία*** — *superstitio* — Superstition.
5. ***Μαγεία*** — *incantatio* — Enchantment. ***χρησμοὶ ἢ μαντεύματα*** — *oracula* — Oracles. ***θεομάντεια*** — *vaticinia afflatorum, etc.* — The soothsayings of them that are possessed with a spirit, and the like.

**Contrary to love are:**

1. ***ἐπιθυμία, ἢ ἀταξία τῶν παθῶν*** — *concupiscentia, seu omnes pravae inclinationes* — Concupiscence, or all depraved inclinations [the disorder of the affections].
2. ***μῖσος ἢ ἔχθος τοῦ θεοῦ*** — *odium Dei* — Hatred of God.
3. ***βρόμος καὶ ἀγανάκτησις*** — *fremitus et indignatio adversus Deum* — Murmuring and indignation against God.
4. ***ἔκπληξις καὶ πτόησις ἢ ἀναίσθησις ἐν τῇ τοῦ θεοῦ ἀγάπῃ*** — *Stupor non sentiens flammas dilectionis Dei* — A numbness that feeleth not the flames of the love of God.

**In excess:** Superstition.

**Contrary to hope** are the same things, ***τὰ αὐτὰ αἰσχρὰ ἐν τῇ ἐνδείᾳ καὶ ὑπερβολῇ*** (the same shameful things in the defect and in the excess), as are contrary to faith: chiefly, despair and presumption.

**Contrary to the fear of God are:**

1. ***ἄδεια*** — *securitas* — Carnal security.
2. ***ὀλιγωρία ἢ οὐδένεια τοῦ θεοῦ*** — *contemtus Dei* — Contempt of God [the slighting of God, or the setting of Him at nought].
3. ***ἔμπαιγμα τοῦ θείου λόγου*** — *derisio Verbi divini* — Mockery of the divine Word.

**In excess:**

1. ***δεισιδαιμονία*** — *superstitio* — Superstition.
2. ***ὁ φόβος δούλειος*** — *servilis timor, fugiens Deum* — Servile fear, which fleeth from God.

**Contrary to humility are:**

1. ***ἀλαζονεία ἢ ὑπερηφάνεια*** — *superbia, iactantia, fastus* — Pride, boasting, haughtiness.
2. ***φιλαυτία*** — *amor et admiratio sui ipsius* — The love and admiration of oneself.
3. ***καταφρόνησις τοῦ πλησίου*** — *contemtus proximi* — Contempt of one's neighbour.

**In excess:**

1. ***ὑπόκρισις*** — *simulatio humilitatis* — The feigning of humility, such as is that of the Anabaptists and of the monks, ***οἳ θέλουσιν εἶναι ταπεινοὶ ἄνευ ὀλιγωρίας, καὶ πένητες ἄνευ ἐνδείας, καὶ πλούσιοι ἄνευ πόνου, καὶ ἅγιοι μάρτυρες ἄνευ σταυροῦ*** (who would be lowly without being slighted, and poor without want, and rich without labour, and holy martyrs without a cross).
2. ***μικροψυχία*** — *pusillanimitas* — Faintheartedness. For this sinneth in excess against humility; in defect, against hope and fortitude.

<!-- p.210 -->

**Contrary to patience are:** in defect, ***ἀτλησία*** — *impatientia* — impatience; in excess, ***ὑποκριτικὴ ὑπομονή*** — *hypocritica patientia* — hypocritical patience, as in the Hermits.

**Contrary to perseverance are:**

1. ***ἀποστασία*** — *defectio* — Falling away.
2. ***ἰσχυρογνωμοσύνη*** — *pertinacia* — Stubbornness.
3. ***τὸ ἀπαλγεῖν τῶν ἀπηλγηκότων*** — *stupor eorum, qui dedoluerunt* — The numbness of them that are past feeling.
4. ***σκάνδαλον ἢ διδόμενον, ἢ λαμβανόμενον καὶ φαρισαϊκόν*** — *offensio, et scandalum, sive datum, sive acceptum* — Offence and stumbling-block, whether given, or taken [and Pharisaical].

## *Virtutes II. Praecepti* · The Virtues of the Second Commandment

1. ***τὸ ὀρθῶς θεοκλητεῖν καὶ ἐπικαλεῖσθαι*** — *Invocare Deum* — To call upon God [aright]. ***προσκύνησις*** — *adoratio* — Adoration. ***προσευχή*** — *petitio* — Petition. ***δέησις καὶ αἴτησις*** — *oratio* — Prayer.
2. ***εὐχαριστία*** — *gratiarum actio* — Thanksgiving.
3. ***ἀληθὴς διδαχή, ἢ ἀληθὲς κήρυγμα*** — *puritas doctrinae et vera praedicatio* — Purity of doctrine and true preaching.
4. ***ὁμολόγησις*** — *Confessio* — Confession.
5. ***ὅρκος ἐπακτὸς καὶ νομιμός*** — *Iuramentum, vel impositum et requisitum a Magistratu, vel legitimum, quod fit ex officio vocationis* — An oath, either imposed and required by the Magistrate, or lawful, such as is made by the duty of one's calling.

### *Extrema* · The Extremes

**Contrary to invocation are:**

1. ***τὸ διαλείπειν ἢ ἀποτίθεσθαι τὴν προσευχήν*** — *intermittere, vel omittere precationem* — To leave off, or to lay aside, prayer.
2. ***πᾶσα ψευδὴς προσκύνησις ἐθνική, εἰδωλολατρεική, ἰουδαϊκή, ἢ τῶν νεκρῶν*** — *Omnis falsa invocatio, Ethnica, Turcica, Idololatrica, Iudaica, mortuorum* — All false invocation: heathen, Turkish, idolatrous, Jewish, and that of the dead.
3. ***βαττολογίαι*** — *Multiloquia in precatione* — Vain repetitions and much speaking in prayer.
4. ***δεισιδαιμονικαὶ προσευχαί*** — *superstitiosae preces* — Superstitious prayers.

**Contrary to thanksgiving are:**

1. ***ἀχαριστία*** — *ingratitudo* — Unthankfulness.
2. ***ὑποκριτικὴ χάρις*** — *simulatio gratitudinis* — The feigning of thankfulness.

**Contrary to purity of doctrine are:**

1. ***πᾶσα διαφθορὰ τῆς διδαχῆς*** — *Omnis corruptela doctrinae* — Every corruption of doctrine.
2. ***αἵρεσις*** — *Secta, segregatio* — A sect, a setting apart. ***Κεζᾶν***, that is, to separate oneself; whence the German *Ketzer* (heretic).
3. ***πολυπραγμοσύνη ἐν τοῖς περιέργοις καὶ κενοσπούδοις*** — *curiositas in non necessariis materiis* — Curiosity in matters not necessary.
4. ***ἀμέλεια ἐν τῷ διδάσκειν*** — *negligentia in docendo* — Negligence in teaching.
5. ***ἐξάλειψις καὶ ἀφανισμὸς τῆς διδαχῆς*** — *deletio et interitus doctrinae* — The blotting out and perishing of doctrine.

<!-- p.212 -->

**Contrary to confession are:**

1. ***ἀποστασία*** — *defectio* — Falling away.
2. ***ἄρνησις*** — *negatio* — Denial, as that of Peter.
3. ***πανουργία καὶ στροφή*** — *versura et mutabilitas* — Shifting and changeableness, as that also of Ecebolius, ***τοῦ ἀλλοπροσάλλου*** (the turncoat).[^E-2]
4. ***ἰσχυρογνωμοσύνη καὶ ἰδιογνωμοσύνη*** — *pertinacia* — Stubbornness [and self-opinion], as that of Servetus, burned at Geneva in 1552.[^E-3]

**Contrary to the oath are:**

1. ***σοφιστικαὶ καὶ διαβολικαὶ ἐξηγήσεις καὶ ἑρμηνεῖαι τῶν ὅρκων*** — *Sophisticae et calumniosae expositiones iuramentorum* — Sophistical and slanderous expositions of oaths.
2. ***ἀσεβεῖς ὅρκοι*** — *impia iuramenta* — Ungodly oaths.
3. ***βλασφημία*** — *blasphemia* — Blasphemy.
4. ***εὐχαὶ εἰδωλολατρεικαί*** — *vota Monachorum, superstitiosae precationes* — The vows of monks, superstitious prayers [idolatrous vows].
5. ***ἐπιορκία*** — *periurium* — Perjury.

## *Virtutes III. Praecepti* · The Virtues of the Third Commandment

### *Officia docentium* · The Duties of Them That Teach

1. ***τὸ πιστὸν τῶν διδασκάλων*** — *Fidelitas docentium* — The faithfulness of them that teach.
2. ***γνῶσις τῆς ἀληθοῦς διδαχῆς*** — *Notitia verae doctrinae* — The knowledge of true doctrine.
3. ***ἐπιμονή*** — *Constantia* — Constancy.
4. ***ἐπιμέλεια*** — *diligentia in sex partibus* ***τῆς διακονίας*** *sive ministerii consistens, quae sunt* — Diligence, which consisteth in six parts of the ministry, which are:
    1. ***τὰ ἀληθῆ διδάσκειν καὶ διαλέγεσθαι*** — *Vera praedicatio* — True preaching [to teach and to discourse of the things that are true].
    2. ***θρησκεία ἀληθὴς καὶ ὁσίη τῶν μυστηρίων*** — *administratio vera sacramentorum* — The true [and holy] administration of the sacraments.
    3. ***ἀναγγέλλειν τὴν τῶν ἁμαρτιῶν ἄφεσιν, καὶ ἀπολύειν τοὺς μετανοοῦντας*** — *annuntiare remissionem peccatorum, et absolvere poenitentes* — To declare the remission of sins, and to absolve the penitent.
    4. ***ἐπισκέπτεσθαι καὶ παραμυθεῖσθαι τοὺς νοσερούς, καὶ ἄλλους κακῶς ἔχοντας*** — *visitare et consolari aegrotos et alios male habentes* — To visit and to comfort the sick, and others that are in evil case.
    5. <!-- p.213 -->***ἀπαυδᾶν, ἀφορίζειν, ἐκκηρύττειν τοὺς δυσάρκτους καὶ ἀπειθεῖς*** — *excommunicare contumaces* — To excommunicate the contumacious [to forbid, to separate, to proclaim as cast out the unruly and disobedient].
    6. ***παραδείγματα καλῶν ἔργων παρέχειν*** — *Exemplis virtutum praelucere* — To shine before others with examples of the virtues.
5. ***ὁμόνοια σὺν τοῖς συνέδροις καὶ συνδούλοις*** — *Concordia cum collegis* — Concord with colleagues [with them that sit with us and serve with us].

**Contrary to these duties of them that teach are:**

1. ***ἡ ἄγνοια τῆς διδαχῆς*** — *ignoratio doctrinae* — Ignorance of the doctrine.
2. ***ἀπιστία*** — *infidelitas* — Unfaithfulness.
3. ***ἀμέλεια*** — *negligentia* — Negligence.
4. ***μικροψυχία*** — *pusillanimitas* — Faintheartedness.
5. ***περιπέτεια καὶ ἀβουλία*** — *temeritas, et inconsideratio* — Rashness and inconsiderateness.[^E-4]
6. ***ἐλαφρία καὶ κουφότης*** — *levitas* — Lightness.
7. ***ἀστασία*** — *inconstantia* — Inconstancy.
8. ***τὸ πρόσκομμα γενέσθαι τινί*** — *aliis esse malo exemplo, et offensioni* — To be an evil example and a stumbling-block to others.
9. ***διχοστασία, καὶ φιλονεικία*** — *discordia et studium contentionum* — Dissension and the love of contentions.

### *Officia auditorum* · The Duties of Hearers

***τὸ τῶν ἀκουόντων ἢ ἀκροωμένων καθῆκον*** (the duty of them that hear, or that give ear).

1. ***ἁγιασμὸς τοῦ σαββάτου*** — *sanctificatio sabbati* — The sanctifying of the Sabbath; that is, ***ἡ σπουδὴ καὶ ἐπιμέλεια ἐν τῷ γινώσκειν, ἀκούειν, μανθάνειν, καὶ ὁμολογεῖν*** — *diligentia in cognoscendo, audiendo, discendo et confitendo* — diligence in knowing, hearing, learning and confessing.
2. ***αἰδὼς πρὸς τοὺς διακόνους*** — *reverentia erga ministros* — Reverence toward the ministers. **2 Cor. 5.** **Eph. 4.**
3. ***εὐπείθεια τῶν πρὸς διδαχήν*** — *Obedientia in iis, quae ad ministerium pertinent, ubi verbum Dei postulat obedientiam* — Obedience in those things which pertain to the ministry, where the Word of God requireth obedience.
4. ***ἐπιείκεια*** — *Aequitas in iudicandis docentium moribus* — Equity in judging the manners of them that teach.
5. ***τὸ κατατίθεσθαι καὶ συμβάλλειν τι κατὰ τὰ ὑπάρχοντα, πρὸς τροφὴν καὶ ἱκανὰ τῶν διδασκόντων*** — *collatio facultatum ad alendos docentes* — The contributing of one's substance for the maintenance of them that teach. **1 Cor. 6.**
6. ***ἡ εὐσεβὴς παρατήρησις τῶν ἱερομηνίων τῶν θεοεπέων, τουτέστι, φερομένων ἀπὸ τοῦ θεοῦ, καὶ τῶν τὴν πολιτείαν, εὐταξίαν, καὶ*** <!-- p.214 -->***ἐγκράτειαν σωζουσῶν*** — *Pia observatio ceremoniarum, et quae a Deo sunt mandatae, et quae bonum ordinem et disciplinam servant* — The godly observance of ceremonies, both of those which are commanded of God, and of those which preserve good order and discipline.

### *Extrema* · The Extremes

**The extremes contrary to the virtues which in the Third Commandment are required of hearers are these:**

1. ***ἡ βεβήλωσις τοῦ σαββάτου*** — *profanatio sabbati* — The profaning of the Sabbath.
2. ***καταφρόνησις τῶν ἐκκλησιαστικῶν πανηγυρῶν ἢ συνάξεων, καὶ τῆς εὐαγγελικῆς πολιτείας, καὶ τοῦ κηρύγματος*** — *contemtus conventuum, festorum, et concionum in Ecclesia* — Contempt of the assemblies, feasts and sermons in the Church.
3. ***τῶν μυστηρίων ἤτοι μὴ μεταλαβεῖν σπουδαίως, ἢ μετέχειν ἀναξίως, ἢ καὶ ὑβριστικῶς ἀπέχεσθαι*** — *Sacramentis non uti, vel indigne uti, vel ea contumaciter negligere* — Not to use the Sacraments, or to use them unworthily, or contumaciously to neglect them.
4. ***τὰς ἑορτασίμους τῶν ἡμερῶν μετενεγκεῖν εἰς ἄλλας τινὰς ἀσχολίας, δι' ὧν ἐμποδίζοιτ' ἂν ὁ ἁγιασμός*** — *Festos dies transferre ad alias occupationes, delitias, voluptates, quibus impeditur sanctificatio* — To turn the feast days to other occupations, delights and pleasures, whereby the sanctifying of them is hindered.
5. ***τὰς λειτουργήσεις μεταστρέφειν πρὸς πλεονεξίαν, τόκον, πορισμόν, κέρδος ἢ ἐργασίαν*** — *abuti iis, quae ad publicum ministerium pertinent, ad avaritiam, usuram, quaestum, lucrum* — To abuse the things that pertain to the public ministry unto covetousness, usury, gain and lucre.
6. ***τοὺς πιστοὺς τοῦ λόγου κήρυκας, ποιμένας, καὶ διδασκάλοις μὴ ὠφελεῖν*** — *non iuvare fideles Pastores et doctores* — Not to help faithful Pastors and teachers.
7. ***δολοφροσύνη ὑποκριτική, ἡ ἐχθρεύουσα τῇ τοῦ θείου λόγου διακονίᾳ*** — *simulatio arte premens ministerium* — A dissembling that by craft oppresseth the ministry.
8. ***προσόδους, ἢ χρήματα καὶ κτήματα τὰ ἐκκλησιαστικὰ μηχανᾶσθαι, καὶ ἁρπάζειν*** — *rapere bona ecclesiastica* — To seize upon the goods of the Church, as do our Evangelical Princes and nobles.
9. ***καταναλόειν τὰ χρήματα εἰς εἰδωλολατρείαν*** — *profundere pecunias ad idololatriam, Missam, monasteria* — To pour out money upon idolatry, the Mass, monasteries.
10. ***δεισιδαιμονία*** — *superstitio* — Superstition.

## *Virtutes IIII. Praecepti* · The Virtues of the Fourth Commandment

1. ***φιλοστοργία καὶ εὐσέβεια πρὸς τοὺς γονεῖς, καὶ τὰ τέκνα, καὶ τοὺς ἄλλους συγγενεῖς*** — *Pietas erga parentes, natos, et sanguine iunctos* — Dutiful affection toward parents, children, and them that are joined by blood.
2. ***δικαιοσύνη καθ' ὅλου, ἡ συλλήβδην πάσας ἀρετὰς ἔχουσα*** <!-- p.215 -->— *Iustitia universalis, id est, obedientia erga omnes Leges, honestas in societate politica* — Universal justice [which comprehendeth all the virtues together], that is, obedience toward all the Laws, honesty in civil society.
3. ***δικαιοσύνη διανεμητική*** — *Iustitia distributiva, quae fit proportione geometrica* — Distributive justice, which is done by geometrical proportion.
4. ***ἐπιμέλεια καὶ ἐνδελέχεια ἐν τοῖς πράγμασι*** — *Sedulitas* — Sedulity [diligence and continuance in one's business].
5. ***εὐχαριστία· ἀντίδωρον· θρεπτήριον· ἢ τὸ ἀντιπελαργεῖν*** — *Gratitudo* — Gratitude [thankfulness; a gift in return; the recompense of nurture; or to cherish in turn, as the stork doth].
6. ***αἰδώς*** — *Reverentia* — Reverence.
7. ***σεμνότης*** — *Gravitas* — Gravity.
8. ***μετριότης καὶ σωφροσύνη*** — *modestia* — Modesty.
9. ***φιλησυχία*** — *pacis et tranquillitatis studium in officiis vocationis* — The love of peace and quietness in the duties of one's calling.
10. ***φιλοκαλία*** — *honestatis studium, et gloriae appetitio* — The love of honesty, and the desire of good report. ***εὔκλεια*** (good fame).
11. ***ἡ ἀγαθὴ συνείδησις*** — *bona conscientia* — A good conscience.

### *Extrema* · The Extremes

**Contrary to dutiful affection toward our own are:**

1. ***ἀστοργία*** — *carere naturali amore* — To be without natural affection.
2. ***ἀκηδία καὶ ἔνδοσις*** — *indulgentia* — Indulgence [carelessness and giving way].
3. ***ἀσέβεια τῶν παίδων*** — *liberorum impietas* — The undutifulness of children, as of Tullia.[^E-5]
4. ***ἀσεβὴς καὶ δεισιδαιμονικὴ ὑποταγὴ ἢ ὑπακοή*** — *impia et superstitiosa obedientia, seu servitus* — Ungodly and superstitious obedience, or servitude.
5. ***στάσις*** — *seditio* — Sedition.

**Contrary to universal justice are:**

1. ***ἀπείθεια καὶ αὐθάδεια ἢ προπέτεια*** — *contumacia* — Contumacy.
2. ***ἀδικία*** — *iniustitia* — Injustice.
3. ***στάσις*** — *seditio* — Sedition, and all ***παρανομία καὶ ἀνομία*** — *legum transgressio* — transgression of the laws.
4. ***ὑποταγὴ ἀσεβής*** — *impia obedientia* — Ungodly obedience.

**Contrary to distributive justice are:**

1. ***σύγχυσις τῶν τάξεων ἢ συστημάτων ἐν τῷ βίῳ, ἥ ἐστιν ἀναρχία*** — *confusio graduum* — The confounding of degrees [of the orders or estates in life, which is anarchy].
2. ***ἰσότης ἡ ἀριθμητική*** — *aequalitas Arithmetica* — Arithmetical equality.
3. ***τυραννὶς καὶ ὠμότης*** — *crudelitas* — Cruelty [tyranny and savageness].
4. ***ἔνδοσις*** — *indulgentia et remissio* — Indulgence and slackness.

**Contrary to sedulity are:**

1. ***ῥᾳθυμία καὶ ἀργία*** — *ignavia* — Sloth.
2. ***πολυπραγμοσύνη*** — *aliena dicere, et facere* — To say and to do the things that belong to others.

**Contrary to gratitude are:**

1. ***ἀχαριστία*** — *ingratitudo* — Ingratitude.
2. ***ὑπόκρισις*** — *simulatio gratitudinis* — The feigning of gratitude.
3. ***κολακεία*** — *adulatio* — Flattery.

<!-- p.216 -->

**Contrary to reverence are:**

1. ***ἀλαζονεία*** — *superbia, contemnens alios* — Pride, despising others.
2. ***ζήλωσις, καὶ κολακεία*** — *affectatio et assentatio* — Affectation and fawning.

**Contrary to gravity are:**

1. ***κουφότης*** — *levitas* — Lightness; and ***βωμολοχία*** — *scurrilitas, a* ***σκώς***, *id est stercus* — scurrility, from ***σκώς***, that is, dung.
2. ***ἀγροικία καὶ σκαιότης*** — *rusticitas* — Clownishness.
3. ***δυσχέρεια*** — *morositas et difficultas* — Moroseness and frowardness.
4. ***εἰρωνεία*** — *irrisio, dissimulatio* — Mockery, dissembling.

**Contrary to modesty:**

1. ***ἀλαζονεία*** — *iactantia, superbia* — Boasting, pride.
2. ***ἐλαφρία*** — *levitas* — Lightness; and ***γελωτοποιΐα*** — *scurrilitas* — buffoonery.
3. ***αἰσχρότης*** — *obscoenitas* — Filthiness.
4. ***φιλοτιμία*** — *ambitio* — Ambition.
5. ***ζῆλος, ἔρις*** — *aemulatio, contentio* — Emulation, strife.
6. ***ἀναισχυντία*** — *impudentia* — Shamelessness. ***ἄτρεπτον εἶναι πρὸς τὸ κακῶς ἀκούειν, καὶ ἀπαθῆ ὄντα ὀλιγωρίᾳ δόξης*** (to be unmoved at being evil spoken of, and without feeling, through contempt of good report).
7. ***ἔκπληξις*** — *stupor* — Dullness.
8. ***ὑπόκρισις*** — *simulatio modestiae* — The feigning of modesty.

**Contrary to the love of quietness, to the desire of good report, and to the care of a good conscience** are the same vices; chiefly contention, ***πολυπραγμοσύνη*** (meddlesomeness), shamelessness, ambition, and the like.

## *Virtutes V. Praecepti* · The Virtues of the Fifth Commandment

1. ***ἡ ἐν μέρει ἢ κατὰ μέρος δικαιοσύνη*** — *iustitia particularis, tribuens unicuique, quod suum est* — Particular justice, which rendereth to every man that which is his own.
2. ***δικαιοσύνη διανεμητικὴ ἐν ταῖς τιμωρίαις*** — *distributiva iustitia, afficiens sceleratos poenis* — Distributive justice, which visiteth evildoers with punishments.
3. ***ἀνδρεία*** (fortitude), either:
    - ***τηβενοφόρος*** — *fortitudo togata* — the fortitude of the gown, ***εἰρηνική*** (of peace); or
    - ***πολεμικὴ ὑπὲρ βωμῶν καὶ ἑστίας*** — *fortitudo bellica pro lege et pro grege, sive pro Aris et focis* — the fortitude of war, for the law and for the flock, or for altars and hearths.
4. ***ὑπομονὴ φιλοσοφικὴ ἡ στέργουσα τὰ παρόντα*** — *patientia philosophica, boni consulens praesentia* — Philosophical patience, which taketh in good part the things that are present, according to the saying: ***ἀνέχου καὶ ἀπέχου***, *sustine et abstine*, bear and forbear, *leid und meid*.[^E-6]
5. ***πραότης*** — *mansuetudo* — Meekness.
6. ***ἐπιείκεια*** — *aequitas* — Equity. ***χρηστότης*** — *lenitas* — Gentleness.
7. ***τὸ ἔλεος, ἢ ἡ ἐλεημοσύνη*** — *misericordia* — Mercy.
8. ***φιλησυχία*** — *studium concordiae* — The love of concord. ***φιλοφιλία*** (the love of friends).
9. <!-- p.217 -->***φιλία*** — *amicitia* — Friendship, which is made either:
    - ***φύσει*** — *natura* — by nature: men with God; husband and wife, ***σύζυγοι***, ***ἀρχὴ γαμική*** (the rule of wedlock); parents and children, ***τοκεῖς, τέκνα***; kinsfolk, ***συγγενεῖς***;
    - ***νόμῳ*** — *lege* — by law, as: ***πατρίς***, *patria*, one's country; ***ἐκκλησία***, the Church; ***πολιτεία***, *respublica*, the commonwealth; ***οἱ κατὰ τὴν οἰκίαν***, *familia*, the household;
    - ***χρήσει*** — *usu* — by use, as: ***γείτονες***, *vicini*, neighbours; ***ὁμοσίπυοι, ὁμόκαπνοι, σύσσιτοι, ὁμοῤῥόφιοι***, *contubernales, convictores*, them that share one lodging and one table [one meal-tub, one smoke, one board, one roof]; ***συνοδοίποροι, ὀπαδοί, ἀκόλουθοι***, *comites*, companions [fellow-travellers, attendants, followers]; ***συνάρχοντες, παραστάται***, *collegae, manipulares*, colleagues, comrades in the ranks.
10. ***τὸ πιστόν*** — *fidelitas* — Faithfulness.
11. ***εὐγνωμοσύνη*** — *candor animi* — Candour of mind.
12. ***φιλανθρωπία*** — *humanitas* — Humanity.
13. ***εὐπροσηγορία*** — *affabilitas* — Affability.
14. ***Νέμεσις ἢ ζῆλος*** — *zelus* — Zeal.

### *Extrema* · The Extremes

**Contrary to particular justice are:**

1. ***ὕβρις καὶ ἀδικία*** — *iniuria, violentia* — Wrong, violence.
2. ***τὸ λίαν δίκαιον, ἢ ἀκριβοδίκαιον*** — *Summum ius* — The utmost rigour of right.
3. ***διαβολή*** — *fraus et calumnia* — Fraud and slander.
4. ***ἀσέλγεια*** — *petulantia* — Wantonness.
5. ***μοχθηρία, ἢ πανουργία*** — *nequitia* — Wickedness [or knavery].

**Contrary to distributive justice in the execution of punishments are:**

1. ***ἔνδοσις καὶ ἐπίμυσις*** — *indulgentia et conniventia* — Indulgence and connivance.
2. ***προσωποληψία*** — *inaequalitas, respectus personarum* — Inequality, respect of persons, according to the verse:

    > *Dat veniam corvis, vexat censura columbas.*  
    > Censure giveth pardon to the ravens, and vexeth the doves.[^E-7]

3. ***ἀπηνότης καὶ ὠμότης*** — *crudelitas* — Cruelty.

<!-- p.218 -->

**Contrary to the fortitude of the gown are:**

1. ***κουφότης καὶ ἀστασία*** — *levitas et inconstantia* — Lightness and inconstancy.
2. ***μαλακία*** — *mollities animi* — Softness of mind.
3. ***ἰσχυρογνωμοσύνη*** — *pervicacia, pertinacia* — Obstinacy, stubbornness.
4. ***δυσχέρεια ἡ στωϊκή*** — *morositas Stoica* — Stoical moroseness, that is, ***τὸ εἶναι δύσκολον καὶ σκύθρωπον*** (to be froward and of a sour countenance).

**But contrary to the fortitude of war are:**

1. ***δειλότης*** — *timiditas* — Cowardice.
2. ***αἰσχρουργία*** — *foedum facinus* — A foul deed, as drunkenness, whoredom, blasphemy, unjust manslaughter, rapine, shameful flight, ***καὶ τὸ εἶναι ῥιψάσπιδα*** (and to be one that casteth away his shield), as the soldiers are wont to do today.
3. ***περιπέτεια, καὶ ἀβουλία*** — *temeritas* — Rashness.

**Contrary to philosophical patience are:**

1. ***τῶν παρόντων καὶ εἰωθότων ὑπεροψία, ἢ τὸ ἁψίκορον*** — *fastidium rerum praesentium* — A loathing of things present [a disdain of things present and accustomed, or the being soon sated].
2. ***ἀτλησία*** — *impatientia* — Impatience.
3. ***ἀσυνεσία καὶ ῥᾳθυμία ῥᾳστωνεύουσα*** — *socordia et ignavia negligens occasiones, quibus offeruntur bona* — Dullness and sloth, neglecting the occasions whereby good things are offered.
4. ***κατάπληξις*** — *stupor* — Amazement.

**Contrary to meekness are:**

1. ***ὀργιλότης*** — *iracundia* — Wrathfulness.
2. ***τὸ μῖσος*** — *odium* — Hatred.
3. ***χαυνότης*** — *lentitas* — Slackness.

**Contrary to equity:**

1. ***κουφότης*** — *levitas* — Lightness.
2. ***ἔνδοσις*** — *indulgentia* — Indulgence, or ***ἄνεσις κακή*** — *remissio, et venia immodica* — slackness and immoderate pardon.
3. ***ἀπήνεια καὶ ἀπανθρωπία*** — *crudelitas* — Cruelty.
4. ***ἐκδίκησις καὶ ἐναντίωσις ἀμυντική*** — *vindicta privata* — Private revenge.

**Contrary to mercy:**

1. ***συγγνώμη βλαβερή*** — *indulgentia* — Indulgence [a hurtful pardoning].
2. ***ὠμότης*** — *crudelitas* — Cruelty.
3. ***ἐπιχαιρεκακία*** — *laetari in aliorum aerumnis et adversitatibus* — To rejoice in the miseries and adversities of others.

**Contrary to the love of concord:**

1. ***φιλονεικία*** — *serens discordias* — Contentiousness, sowing discords.
2. ***φθόνος*** — *invidentia, livor* — Envy, spite.
3. ***βραδυτὴς καὶ χαυνότης ἐν τῇ τῆς ἀληθείας ὁμολογίᾳ*** — *lentitas non pugnans de rebus necessariis* — A slackness [in the confession of the truth] that contendeth not for things necessary, such as there is today in most Princes and Theologians, who, under the colour and pretext of concord, let go and lose the truth.

**Contrary to friendship are:**

1. ***ἔχθρα*** — *inimicitia* — Enmity.
2. ***τὸ μῖσος*** — *odium* — Hatred.
3. ***ὑπόκρισις*** — *simulatio amicitiae* — The feigning of friendship.
4. ***μισανθρωπία*** — *inhumanitas* — Inhumanity.
5. ***ἀρέσκεια ἢ κολακεία*** — *comitas assentatrix, seu* <!-- p.219 -->*adulatio Aulica* — A fawning complaisance, or the flattery of Courts. ***κακαὶ φρένες, ἡδὺ λάλημμα*** — *fel in corde, mel in ore* — gall in the heart, honey in the mouth. ***τὸ φίλημα ἢ στόμιον τοῦ Ἰούδα*** — *osculum Iudae* — the kiss of Judas. Cadmean brethren,[^E-8] such as many are today, even among the Theologians.
6. ***τὸ συγκατανευσίφαγον εἶναι*** — *esse conepulonem, et parasitum, et omnium dictis inter epulas facile annuere* — To be a fellow-feaster and a parasite, and readily to nod assent at table to whatsoever any man saith.

**Contrary to faithfulness:**

1. ***ἀπιστία*** — *perfidia* — Perfidy.
2. ***κολακεία*** — *assentatio, et stulta fidelitas contra bonos mores* — Fawning, and a foolish faithfulness against good manners.

**Contrary to candour of mind:**

1. ***ὑποψία καὶ ὑφόρασις*** — *suspicio* — Suspicion.
2. ***ἀπειθία ἢ δυσπειθία*** — *diffidentia* — Distrust.
3. ***ἑτοιμοπιστία, ταχυπείθεια*** — *nimia credulitas* — Overmuch credulity.[^E-9]
4. ***φθόνος*** — *invidia* — Envy.
5. ***ἐπιχαιρεκακία*** (joy at another's hurt).
6. ***ὑπόκρισις*** (dissembling).
7. ***λυκοφιλία, ὑαινοφιλία*** (the friendship of the wolf, the friendship of the hyena).

**Contrary to humanity:**

1. ***δυσχέρεια*** — *morositas* — Moroseness. ***μισανθρωπία*** (the hatred of mankind).
2. ***ἀστασία ἢ ὑπόκρισις*** — *levitas et simulatio* — Lightness and dissembling, such as there is in Courts.

**Contrary to affability** is to be ***σκύθρωπον***, *morosum, torvum*, sour and grim, ***δυσπρόσοδον, δυσόμιλον καὶ δυσέντευκτον***, *aditu difficilem et inaffabilem*, hard of access and not to be spoken with.

**Contrary to zeal:**

1. ***χαύνωσις*** — *lentitas et laxatio* — Slackness and remissness.
2. ***ἀγριότης*** — *feritas, et iniusta asperitas* — Fierceness and unjust harshness.

## *Virtutes VI. Praecepti* · The Virtues of the Sixth Commandment

**I.**

**The virtue:**

- ***ἁγνεία ἢ καθαρότης*** — *Castitas* — Chastity.
- ***παρθενία*** — *Virginitas* — Virginity.
- ***γάμος*** — *Nuptiae* — Marriage.
- ***συζυγία*** — *coniugium* — Wedlock.

**In defect:**

- ***ἐπιθυμία*** — *libido* — Lust.
- ***πορνεία*** — *scortatio* — Whoredom.
- ***μοιχεία*** — *adulterium* — Adultery.
- ***τὸ ἀπολύειν τὴν γυναῖκα*** — *repudiare uxorem* — To put away one's wife.
- ***διακόρευσις*** — *stuprum* — Defloration.
- ***φθορὰ καὶ σύζευξις τῶν*** <!-- p.220 -->***συγγενῶν*** — *incestus* — Incest.
- ***λογισμοὶ σαρκικοί*** — *cogitationes spurcae* — Filthy thoughts.
- ***αἰσχρολογίαι*** — *obscoeni sermones* — Filthy communications.
- ***πᾶσα ἀκαθαρσία, καὶ τῶν ἀφροδισίων χρῆσις ἄνομος καὶ ἄθεσμος*** — *omnis impuritas et incontinentia* — All uncleanness and incontinence.

**In excess:**

- ***ζυγὸν παρθενίας κατ' ἀνάγκην*** — *prohibitiones coniugii* — The forbiddings of marriage [a yoke of virginity by constraint]. **1 Tim. 4.**
- ***ἀγαμία βίαιος*** — *coelibatus violentus* — Enforced celibacy.
- ***ὑποκριτικὴ ἁγνεία*** — *simulata castitas* — Feigned chastity, as that of Origen.

**II.**

**The virtue:** ***ἡ αἰδὼς καὶ σωφροσύνη*** — *verecundia sive pudor* — Shamefacedness, or modesty.

**In defect:**

- ***ἀναίδεια*** — *impudentia* — Shamelessness.
- ***ἀκολασία καὶ ἀτασθαλία*** — *protervitas et contumelia* — Wantonness and insolence.
- ***ματαιότης*** — *vanitas* — Vanity.

**In excess:** ***δυσωπία*** — *stupor rusticus* — A clownish bashfulness.

**III.**

**The virtue:** ***σωφροσύνη*** — *Temperantia* — Temperance. ***νηφαλισμὸς καὶ προσοχή*** — *sobrietas* — Sobriety.

**In defect:**

- ***ἀκρασία*** — *intemperantia* — Intemperance.
- ***ἀσωτία*** — *luxuria* — Riotous living, of which it is said: Every ***ἄσωτος*** is ***ἄσωστος*** (every prodigal is past saving).
- ***μέθη*** — *ebrietas* — Drunkenness.
- ***πολυφαγία*** — *voracitas* — Voracity.
- ***λαιμαργία*** — *gula* — Gluttony. *Crapula* (surfeiting), from ***κάρα πάλλειν*** (to make the head to shake). *Eluatio* (gormandizing), ***λαιμαργία***. ***ἀσωτοποσία*** (riotous drinking).

**In excess:**

- ***νηστεία ἡ ὑπερμέτρως*** — *ieiunium immodicum* — Immoderate fasting.
- ***ἰχθυοφαγία δεισιδαιμονική*** — *superstitiosus piscium esus* — The superstitious eating of fish.
- ***ἐγκράτεια βίαιος*** — *abstinentia nimia et violenta* — Excessive and enforced abstinence.

## *Virtutes VII. Praecepti* · The Virtues of the Seventh Commandment

**I.**

**The virtue:** ***τὰ ἀναγκαῖα*** <!-- p.221 -->***κτᾶσθαι ἐννόμως, καὶ τοῖς ἰδίοις κτήμασι χρῆσθαι ἐλευθερίως*** — *Iusta possessio et bonus usus rerum* — The just possession and the good use of things [to get things needful lawfully, and to use one's own possessions liberally].

**In defect:**

- ***πᾶσαι ἀφαιρέσεις τῶν ἀλλοτρίων*** — *omnia aucupia opum* — All fowling after wealth [all takings away of other men's goods].
- ***κλοπαί*** — *furta* — Thefts.
- ***δόλοι ἐν τοῖς συναλλάγμασι*** — *fraudes in contractibus* — Frauds in contracts.

**In excess:** ***πλεονεξία*** — *avaritia* — Covetousness.

**II.**

**The virtue:** ***δικαιοσύνη ἀλλακτική*** — *Iustitia commutativa constans* ***ἰσότητι*** *iuxta proportionem Arithmeticam* — Commutative justice, which standeth in equality, according to arithmetical proportion.

**In defect:**

- ***συνάλλαγμα ἄδικον*** — *iniustus contractus* — An unjust contract.
- ***πανούργευμα, καὶ χρηματισμὸς ἄμετρος καὶ ἄδικος*** — *lucrum nimium et iniustum, supra sortem* — Excessive and unjust gain, above the principal.
- ***κέρδος ἄδικον*** — *lucrum iniustum* — Unjust lucre.
- ***ἁρπαγὴ καὶ σύλησις*** — *Expilatio publica* — Public pillage, ***οἷά ἐστι, τὰ ὑπὸ τῶν δυνατῶν καὶ ἀρχόντων ἐπιτιθέμενα τοῖς πελάταις καὶ ἀρχομένοις τῶν εἰσπράξεων καὶ φόρων καὶ τελέων ἄχθη, καὶ ἤτοι πεπανουργευμέναι ἢ βεβιασμέναι τῶν χρημάτων ἀποτρίψεις, καὶ συσκευαί*** (such as are the burdens of exactions and tributes and tolls laid by the mighty and by rulers upon their dependants and subjects, and the wringings away of money, whether by craft or by force, and the contrivances), the arts of publicans and treasurers.
- Likewise, ***βία τῶν ἀλλοτρίων ἁρπαζομένων*** — *vis bonorum raptorum* — The violent seizing of other men's goods.
- ***κλοπαὶ αἱ ἴδιαι ἢ τῶν ἰδίων*** — *furta privata* — Private thefts, of which a description is found in the notable theft in **Herodotus**.[^E-10]
- Likewise, ***ἀποφορὰ καὶ μισθὸς τῆς παρεργούσης ἀπάτης καὶ ἀργίας ἢ ῥᾳθυμίας*** — *iniustae mercedes et stipendia, quae devorantur ab ignavis et deceptoribus* — Unjust wages and stipends, which are devoured by idlers and deceivers, in whom most princes today take delight, to the great hurt and dishonour both of themselves and of their subjects.
- And hither let there be referred also ***ἁρπαγμοί, καὶ αἰφνίδιοι καὶ μετὰ βίας ἀφαιρέσεις τῶν ἀγαθῶν τῶν ἐκκλησιαστικῶν*** — *rapinae bonorum Ecclesiasticorum* — the robberies of the goods of the Church, which are enjoyed chiefly by ***τὰ ἐτώσια*** <!-- p.222 -->***ἄχθη ἀρούρης, καὶ κακὰ θηρία, γαστέρες ἀργαί*** (the useless burdens of the earth, and evil beasts, slow bellies).[^E-11]
- Moreover also ***ἡ ῥᾳθυμία, ἀπιστία, καὶ ἀργία τῶν ὑπηρετῶν τῶν κατ' οἰκίαν*** — *ignavia et infidelitas familiae* — The sloth and unfaithfulness of the household.
- Likewise ***πτωχεία τῶν ἀέργων καὶ κακούργων*** — *mendicitas ignavorum, et fraudulentorum hominum* — The beggary of idle and deceitful men.

**In excess:**

- ***ἡ ἀσωτία*** — *prodigentia et profusio* — Prodigality and lavishing.
- ***τὸ σφαῖραν ἀποδεικνύναι τὴν οὐσίαν*** (to make a ball of one's substance).

**III.**

**The virtue:** ***Ἐλευθεριότης καὶ εὐποιΐα ἐκ τῶν ὑπαρχόντων*** — *Liberalitas et beneficentia* — Liberality and beneficence [out of that which one hath].

**In defect:** ***ἀνελευθερία. βαναυσία. πλεονεξία. ῥυπαρία. μικροπρέπεια. σμικρολογία.*** — *avaritia et sorditudo, nemini benefaciens* — Covetousness and sordidness, doing good to no man [illiberality; vulgar meanness; greed; filthy niggardliness; pettiness; penny-counting].

**In excess:** ***πρόχευμα καὶ ἀσωτία*** — *profusio, et prodigalitas* — Lavishing and prodigality.

**IIII.**

**The virtue:** ***φειδὼ καὶ πρόνοια*** — *Parsimonia, frugalitas* — Thrift, frugality [and forethought]; ***αὐτάρκεια καὶ σωφροσύνη*** (contentment and soberness).

**In defect:** ***τὸ σπαθᾶν καὶ τρυφᾶν*** — *prodigalitas* — Prodigality [to squander and to live delicately].

**In excess:**

- ***ἀδικία καὶ βία*** — *iniustitia et vis* — Injustice and violence.
- ***φειδωλία*** — *tenacitas et avaritia* — Closefistedness and covetousness.

**V.**

**The virtue:** ***φιλοξενία*** — *Hospitalitas* — Hospitality. ***ξενισμός, καὶ ξένισις*** (the entertaining of strangers).

**In defect:** ***ἀξενία*** — *inhospitalitas* — Inhospitality, joined with ***μισανθρωπίᾳ καὶ πλεονεξίᾳ***, *inhumanitate et avaritia*, inhumanity and covetousness. It is called <!-- p.223 -->also ***ἀμιξία*** (unsociableness), and ***κακοξενία*** (evil entertainment of strangers).

**In excess:**

- ***τὸ ἀλαζονεύεσθαι καὶ σοβαρεύεσθαι*** — *ostentatio et fastus* — Ostentation and haughtiness.
- ***ἀσωτία*** — *profusio* — Lavishing.
- ***τὸ ξενίζειν πάντας ἀκρίτως*** — *Omnes sine discrimine hospitio excipere* — To receive all men to lodging without distinction, which is the part of lightness, and of innkeepers.

## *Virtutes VIII. Praecepti* · The Virtues of the Eighth Commandment

**I.**

***Ἀλήθεια*** — *Veritas* — Truth: ***ἡ πάντων μὲν ἀγαθῶν θεῷ ἡγεῖται, πάντων δὲ ἀνθρώποις*** (which is the leader of all good things for God, and of all for men).[^E-12]

**Contrary to truth are:**

1. ***πᾶν ψεῦδος ἐν τῷ βίῳ, ἢ ψευδολογία, καὶ πᾶσα σοφιστεία καὶ βεβιασμένη ἐξήγησις περὶ τὰ μαθήματα*** — *Omne mendacium in vita et oratione, et omnis sophistica in disciplinis* — Every lie in life and in speech, and all sophistry [and forced exposition] in the sciences.
2. ***αἵρεσις*** — *haeresis* — Heresy, ***ἡ δόξα βιαζομένη τὴν ἀλήθειαν*** (the opinion that doth violence to the truth); ***λεπτολογίαι, ἢ λεπτολεσχίαι*** — *subtilia nugamenta* — subtle triflings, such as are today those of the Sacramentarians; ***λεπτομέρεια*** — *exilitas* — thin-spun slightness.
3. ***πᾶσα διαβολή*** — *omnis calumnia* — All slander; ***ἡ λαθραίως κατηγορία ἐξ ἐρημίας γενομένη, τὸν κατηγορούμενον λεληθυῖα, ἐκ τοῦ μονομεροῦς ἀναντιλέκτως πεπιστευμένη*** — *clandestina delatio, accusato absente et inscio, et altera tantum parte audita, contradicente nemine* — secret informing, the accused being absent and unaware, and one side only being heard, no man gainsaying.
4. ***ἀπιστία ἐν ταῖς ὑποσχέσεσιν, ἐπαγγελίαις, ὅρκοις, συναλλάγμασι, σπονδαῖς*** — *perfidia in promissionibus, iuramentis, contractibus et foederibus* — Faithlessness in promises, oaths, contracts and leagues.
5. ***εἰρωνεῖαι*** — *irrisiones, et dissimulationes* — Mockings and dissemblings, as are sarcasm, *mycterismus* (the sneer), and ***ἀστεϊσμός*** (the polished jest). Likewise, ***σκώμματα τῆς ὑποκρίσεως ἐνεδρευτικὰ τῶν πλησίον*** — *scommata, quibus insidiae proximo tacite struuntur* — gibes whereby snares are privily laid for one's neighbour.
6. ***συκοφαντία, ἐπηρεασμός, μεταλαλία, ἀλαζονεία, κολακεία, ἀστασία, μηδὲν ὑγιές*** — *sycophantia, iactantia, obtrectatio, fastus, adulatio, levitas, nihil sinceri* — False accusing, vaunting, backbiting, haughtiness, flattery, lightness, nothing sound.[^E-13] ***προπέτεια*** — *immodestia* — Forwardness. ***γλῶσσα*** <!-- p.224 -->***ἀκόλαστος*** — *petulans lingua* — An unbridled tongue, ***ἧς τέλος ἡ δυστυχία*** (whose end is misfortune).[^E-14] Likewise ***αἰσχρολογία*** — *turpitudo in sermone* — filthiness in speech.
7. ***πᾶσα ὑπόκρισις ἐν τοῖς λόγοις, ἤθεσι, τρόποις, καὶ ἐν τῷ ὅλῳ βίῳ*** — *simulatio in sermonibus, moribus, gestibus, et in tota vita* — All dissembling in words, manners, bearing, and in the whole life.
8. ***κατάκρυψις μετ' ἐπιβουλῆς, ἢ τὸ ὕπουλον*** — *occultatio insidiosa* — Treacherous concealment [or that which festereth beneath the surface].

**II.**

**The virtue:** ***ἁπλότης*** — *Simplicitas* — Simplicity. ***τὸ σαφές*** — *perspicuitas* — Plainness.

**In defect:**

- ***διπλότης καὶ φενακισμός*** — *duplicitas et impostura* — Doubleness and imposture.
- ***τὸ σκότισον***, of **Heraclitus**: *obscurus esto*, "Be thou dark." ***τὸ ἀκατάληπτον*** (that which cannot be comprehended).

**In excess:**

- ***ὑπόκρισις*** — *simulatio simplicitatis* — The feigning of simplicity. ***τὸ εἶναι βαυκοπάνουργον*** — *in cute ovis, intus lupus* — in the skin a sheep, within a wolf [to be a mincing knave].
- ***εὐήθεια*** — *fatuitas* — Silliness.

**III.**

**The virtue:** ***εὐγνωμοσύνη*** — *Candor* — Candour.

**In defect:** ***διαβολή*** — *calumnia* — Slander. ***κάλυμμα ἐπιβουλευτικόν*** — *insidiosa occultatio* — Treacherous concealment.

**In excess:** ***ταχυπείθεια*** — *credulitas nimia et praepropera* — Credulity overmuch and overhasty.

**IIII.**

**The virtue:** ***καρτερία*** — *Constantia* — Constancy.

**In defect:**

- ***ἀστασία*** — *levitas* — Lightness.
- ***ἐποχὴ ἀκαδημική*** — *Suspensio Academica disputatorum scholasticorum* — The Academic suspension of judgement, of the disputers of the schools.

**In excess:** ***αὐθάδεια καὶ ἰσχυρογνωμοσύνη*** — *pertinacia* — Stubbornness.

<!-- p.225 -->

**V.**

**The virtue:** ***εὐμάθεια*** — *Docilitas* — Teachableness. ***εὐσυνεισία*** (quickness of understanding).

**In defect:** ***ἐποχή, διαβολή. αὐθάδεια.*** (suspension of judgement, slander; self-will).

**In excess:** ***κουφότης*** — *levitas* — Lightness.

**VI.**

**The virtue:** ***σιγή, σιωπή, ἐχεμυθία ἡ καλή*** — *Taciturnitas* — Silence [stillness, holding one's peace, the keeping of counsel that is seemly].

**In defect:**

- ***ἀδολεσχία καὶ πολυλογία*** — *garrulitas* — Talkativeness.
- ***φλυαρία τῶν χυδαίων, εἰκαιοσύνη*** — *futilitas, nugacitas, vanitas* — Babbling, trifling, vanity [the prating of the common sort; idle rashness].

**In excess:** ***ἡ οὐ καθήκουσα καὶ οὐκ ἐν δέοντι γενομένη ἐχεμυθία*** — *tacere ubi tacendum non est: et esse mutum canem, cum vocatio flagitat liberam confessionem* — To keep silence where silence is not to be kept, and to be a dumb dog when one's calling demandeth a free confession. ***νηπιέη*** — *infantia* — Speechlessness.

**VII.**

**The virtue:** ***Λογιότης*** — *Eloquentia* — Eloquence.

**In defect:** *Infantia* — Want of utterance.

**In excess:** ***ἀδολεσχία*** — *garrulitas* — Talkativeness. ***πᾶσα κατάχρησις τῆς λογιότητος*** — *omnis abusus eloquentiae* — Every abuse of eloquence.

**VIII.**

**The virtue:** ***Εὐτραπελία, φιλοφροσύνη, μετὰ σεμνότητος χαριέδης***[^E-15] — *comitas* — Courtesy [ready pleasantness, friendliness, graceful with gravity].

**In defect:** ***δυσχέρεια*** — *morositas* — Moroseness.

**In excess:**

- ***εὐτραπελία***, **Eph. 5**, which is ***μωρολογία, κουφότης, ἀπαιδευσία. καὶ βωμολοχία*** — *scurrilitas et levitas* — Jesting, which is foolish talking, lightness, ill breeding, and buffoonery: scurrility and lightness.
- ***κολακεία*** — *adulatio* — Flattery.

**IX.**

**The virtue:** ***χαριεντισμὸς*** <!-- p.226 -->***καὶ ἀστειότης*** — *Urbanitas, civilitas* — Urbanity, civility.

**In defect:**

- ***ἀπειροκαλία*** — *Insulsitas* — Tastelessness.
- ***ἀναρμοστία καὶ ἀπρέπεια*** — *indecentia* — Unseemliness.
- ***τὸ ἀπροσδιόνυσα λέγειν, ἢ παραφθέγγεσθαι*** — *absurda loqui* — To speak things beside the purpose.

**In excess:** ***βωμολοχία. σπερμολογία καὶ φλυαρία*** — *scurrilitas* — Scurrility [buffoonery; idle prating and babbling].

## *Virtutes Noni et Decimi Praecepti* · The Virtues of the Ninth and Tenth Commandments

***δικαιοσύνη ἡ ἀνέκαθεν, καὶ πρώτη: εἰκὼν τοῦ θεοῦ ἐν ἀνθρώποις*** — *Iustitia originalis. Imago Dei in hominibus* — Original righteousness. The image of God in men.

Contrary to this is all ***ἁμαρτία, ἐπιθυμία, ἀπειροπάθεια, ἢ τῶν παθῶν ἀμετρία, καὶ ἀταξία. πᾶσα μοχθηρία, καὶ ἐννόησις τῆς ἀδικίας, ἡ ὄρεξις, ὁρμή, βούλευσις, ἐπιβολή*** — *omnis inobedientia et transgressio* — all disobedience and transgression [sin, concupiscence, boundless passion, or the want of measure and of order in the affections; all wickedness, and the very thinking upon unrighteousness, the appetite, the impulse, the purposing, the setting about it].

[^E-1]: David Chytraeus (1530–1600), professor at Rostock, *Regulae vitae: virtutum descriptiones methodicae* (first printed in the 1550s), a schoolbook that sets out the virtues and vices in the order of the Decalogue.

[^E-2]: Ecebolius, the sophist of Constantinople who changed his religion with each emperor; his story is told by Socrates Scholasticus, *Ecclesiastical History* 3.13.

[^E-3]: So the print. Michael Servetus was burned at Geneva on 27 October 1553.

[^E-4]: The print has ***περιπέτεια*** (a sudden reversal) here and again under the fortitude of war; the Latin *temeritas* shows that ***προπέτεια*** (rashness) is meant, the word which the print itself gives under universal justice and under the Eighth Commandment.

[^E-5]: Tullia, daughter of King Servius Tullius, who drove her chariot over the body of her father (Livy 1.48).

[^E-6]: The maxim of Epictetus, reported by Aulus Gellius, *Attic Nights* 17.19.

[^E-7]: Juvenal, *Satires* 2.63.

[^E-8]: Probably the armed men sprung from the dragon's teeth sown by Cadmus, who fell upon one another and slew each other.

[^E-9]: The print has *nimia crudelitas* ("overmuch cruelty"), an evident misprint for *nimia credulitas*, as the Greek shows and as the same word is rendered under the Eighth Commandment (*credulitas nimia et praepropera*).

[^E-10]: The robbing of the treasury of King Rhampsinitus, Herodotus 2.121.

[^E-11]: Two quotations joined: Homer, *Iliad* 18.104 (***ἐτώσιον ἄχθος ἀρούρης***), and **Titus 1**, the verse of Epimenides on the Cretans.

[^E-12]: Plato, *Laws* 5, 730c, where the received text has ***θεοῖς*** ("for the gods").

[^E-13]: The third Greek word is plainly printed ***μεταλαλία***, a form not found in the lexica. By the order of the Latin it answers to *obtrectatio* (backbiting), so that ***καταλαλία*** may be meant.

[^E-14]: After Euripides, *Bacchae* 386–388.

[^E-15]: So the print, plainly: ***χαριέδης***, a form not found in the lexica. Some word for "graceful" is evidently meant (***χαρίεις*** or ***χαριτώδης***); the rendering follows that sense.

# *De Secunda Parte Catechismi* · On the Second Part of the Catechism

### What is the second part of the Catechism?

The Apostles' Creed (*Symbolum Apostolicum*).

### What is contained in the Creed?

A certain summary doctrine of the Articles of faith, or heads of the Christian Religion, by the true understanding and profession whereof the Church is distinguished from all other sects which are strangers to the Christian Religion.

<!-- p.227 -->

### What is the reason of the name?

*Symbolum*, ***ἀπὸ τοῦ συμβάλλειν***, from bringing together (*a conferendo*), is a soldier's word, and signifieth the token (*tessera*) given unto soldiers, whereby they may be discerned from enemies and spies; in German, *ein losung, oder kennzeichen* (a watchword, or mark of recognition). The Symbol of the Church, therefore, is the token of the soldiers of Christ, that is, of them that believe, by which they testify that they have given in their names to Christ alone, and that they dissent from all other sects.

### Why is this Creed called the Apostles'?

Either because it was written by the Apostles, and delivered as it were from hand to hand unto the Church, as Antiquity hath always held, as doth plainly appear out of **Irenaeus** and **Tertullian**, who name this Creed the Apostolic tradition; or because the Apostolic doctrine, gathered as it were into one bundle and comprehended, is extant in this Creed, that it may be as it were the most firm foundation of the whole Christian doctrine.

### Why is the Creed to be recited daily?

1. Because it putteth us in mind of God, and of the chief works and benefits of God.
2. Because in this recitation the benefits of the Son of God are applied unto him that believeth.
3. Because it is a consolation in the greatest dangers.
4. Because it is a confession against the devils and heretics and other ungodly men, and withal it ministereth weapons whereby Satan is put to flight and overcome.
<!-- p.228 -->
5. Because it accustometh us to think upon the testimonies of the doctrine, namely, the revelations of God and the miracles.

### How many Creeds of the Christian faith are there in the Church?

There are three authentic ones: the Apostles', the Nicene, and the Athanasian.

Of the Apostles' Creed the words of **Irenaeus** are these: *"We ought not to seek among others the truth which it is easy to take from the Church, seeing the Apostles have most fully bestowed upon her, as into a rich storehouse, all things that are of the truth, that every man, whosoever will, may take from it the drink of life.
