The Passion belongeth unto the person, not in the divine, but in the human nature, according to that saying of **Peter**: *"Christ hath suffered in the flesh."* For whatsoever of reproach and of wrong was laid upon the flesh of Christ, or is yet laid upon it by his enemies, all that hath fallen and doth fall to the reproach of the eternal Son of God.

The human nature is likened unto Iron, the Divine unto Fire, so that there is made a fiery iron (*ferrum ignitum*). For so hath Antiquity always spoken. Now if any man shall essay to touch and to handle the Iron, he shall feel the force, not of the iron, but of the fire; which thing all they shall really prove, who are either manifest blasphemers and revilers of the Son of God, or diminishers of the Divine Majesty communicated unto Christ the man.

[^F2-1]: The definition is Philip Melanchthon's.

[^F2-2]: So the print. The words stand in Heb. 1:6.

[^F2-3]: *Particularitas*: the thought that the promise of grace is particular, belonging to certain men only, and not to all.

[^F2-4]: The treatise *De spiritu et anima* went under Augustine's name in the Middle Ages and in the sixteenth century; it is not his.

[^F2-5]: Martin Chemnitz, *De duabus naturis in Christo*, first printed at Jena in 1570 and enlarged in 1578.

[^F2-6]: So the print (*in aeternum*). The Vulgate and the Greek have "in the truth" (John 17:19).

<!-- p.275 -->

**Argument.**

Passion and death are properties agreeing not to the Divine, but to the human nature.

Therefore it is not rightly said that the Passion is of the person.

**I answer.** It is one thing to speak of the Passion and Death of Christ in so far as it is a property of the human nature, which alone is torn, smitten, scourged, and dieth: which injury nevertheless falleth to the reproach of the Son of God, because that body is divine, that is, it is the body and flesh of the Only-begotten of God, who receiveth, beareth, and interpreteth that injury with no other mind than if it were done to His own divine nature. But it is another thing, that by that passion and death the wrath of the Father hath been appeased, the head of the serpent bruised, death destroyed, life restored, the captives set free. For these are operations of the divine power, yet not without the flesh. For the divine power of the Son of God, as **Cyprian** saith,[^F3-1] *"was as it were a certain hook, covered with the habit of human flesh,"* that, being devoured by the serpent Leviathan, He might through that infirmity of His passion and death take and overcome that strong man armed.

### How dost thou understand the customary saying of Irenaeus, that Christ was crucified and died, the Word resting, that He might be able to be crucified and to die?

**Irenaeus**[^F3-2] setteth forth to be considered that wonderful obedience of the Son of God resting, that is, that His Divine nature, dwelling bodily in Christ, although it could most easily have repelled all injuries, sufferings, and death, that they should not be able to fall upon that mass of the assumed human nature, <!-- p.276 -->in which all the fulness of the Godhead dwelt, yet did not use its Power, did not put forth its strength to keep off from its own flesh injuries, sufferings, and death, but gave place to the wrath of the eternal Father against the sin of mankind, permitting that that flesh might be crucified and die.

### How manifold is the Meditation of the Passion of Christ?

Threefold: the Pedagogical, or Historical; the Spiritual; and the exemplary, or Paradigmatical.

**To the Pedagogical meditation these things are required:**

- Consideration of the Prophetical Predictions concerning the Passion of Christ.
- Consideration of the Types and Figures, as in Abel, Isaac, Jonah; and of the Sacrifices.
- The Definition of the Passion.
- The Time of the Passion.
- The Causes.
- The Kind and manner of the Passion, according to the History.
- How manifold the Passion is.
- The Difference between the Passion of Christ and that of other Saints.

**To the spiritual meditation these things are required:**

- The fear of God, contrition, acknowledging the heinousness of sin, the greatness of the wrath of God, and the severity of His justice.
- Trust in grace through and on account of the ***λύτρον*** (ransom).
- Invocation.
- Thanksgiving for the love and humility of the Son.
- Godliness of life toward God and our neighbour.
- Holy care in our Vocation.
- A good conscience founded in the Blood of the Son of God.
- Consideration of the tempering of justice and mercy in God.

<!-- p.277 -->

**To the exemplary these things are required:**

- Patience under the cross.
- Humility.
- Consolation.
- Mutual love, Peace, Concord, Forgiveness, Beneficence.
- Consideration of the images which paint the state of the Church, which God willeth to be made conformable to the Image of His Son, **Rom. 8**.

### How is the application of the Passion of Christ made?

It is made by the Word, by Faith, and by the use of the Sacraments.

### Why is mention made of Pilate in the Creed?

1. That we may know at what time and place the Lord suffered.
2. That we may consider the Prophecies concerning Shiloh, and the seventy weeks of Daniel.
3. That we may refute the dotages of the Jews who look for a Messiah.

### Why was Christ crucified?

1. To shew forth the abomination and heinousness of sin.
2. To prove that Christ took upon Himself our guilt, and the punishment of our guilt, being made a curse for us.
3. That the Truth might answer to the figures, to the sacrifices lifted up on high, to the offering of Isaac, to the Brasen serpent, etc.

### Why would Christ die?

1. That He might fulfil the predictions of the Prophets.
2. <!-- p.278 -->That He might represent the figures in very deed. For the victims were slain.
3. That by His passion and death He might bring forth these effects and fruits: 1. That He might render entire Obedience unto death, even the death of the cross. 2. That He might overcome the Devil, and bind him. 3. That He might deliver Man from sin and death. 4. That He might abolish Death by His own death. 5. That He might make Jews and gentiles equal, the enmity being abolished through death. 6. That He might bring it to pass that we should die unto sin, and live unto righteousness for ever.

**Augustine:** *"Through the Death of our Redeemer we have been called from darkness to light, from death to life, from corruption to incorruption, from exile to our Country, from mourning to joy, from the earth to the heavenly Kingdom."*

### Why was Christ buried?

1. That He might shew that He had truly been dead.
2. That He might bury our Sins together with Himself.
3. That He might consecrate our sepulchres by the touch of His most holy Body.
4. That He might afford us a way out of our sepulchres unto life.
5. That we, being buried with Him, might rest from sin, and walk in newness of life.

## *Triumphus* · The Triumph

## *Descendit ad inferos* · He Descended into Hell

**Ephes. 4.** *"He descended into the lower parts of the earth."* **Ps. 16.** *"Thou wilt not leave my soul in hell."* These words pertain not to the passion, or to the burial, much less to torments inflicted in hell, but are a part of the triumph and Victory of Christ, and therefore are joined with the Resurrection. <!-- p.279 -->And this hath ever been the faith of the orthodox Church.

### Why did Christ descend into Hell?

1. That He might testify that He is the Conqueror of Hell.
2. That He might utterly tread down the head of the serpent, and destroy the gates of hell, and bind the Devils, that is, overcome them, and condemn them for ever by a decisive sentence, which He Himself denounced unto His enemies by His own voice and presence.
3. That He might deliver us from the power of Hell.

## *Tertia die resurrexit a mortuis* · The Third Day He Rose Again from the Dead

### Why did Christ rise again?

1. That He might shew that He is the Conqueror of sin, of death, of hell, and of the whole kingdom of Satan.
2. That our righteousness before God might be certain. For the Fruits of the Resurrection of Christ are:

    1. Remission of sins, **1 Corinth. 15**.
    2. Justification, **Romans 4**.
    3. The giving of the Holy Ghost, **Galat. 4**.
    4. The confirmation of the covenant of grace, or the stipulation of a good conscience, **1 Peter 3**.
    5. Our renewing, in newness of life, **Rom. 6**.
    6. Immortality, and the resurrection of bodies, **1 Cor. 15**.
    7. Mutual brotherhood, for that we have one God and Father.
    8. A remedy against all calamities, according to that saying of Paul: *"Remember that the Lord Jesus was raised."*
    9. <!-- p.280 -->A lively hope of glory that fadeth not away, and of eternal salvation, **1 Pet. 1**.

## *Ascendit ad coelos* · He Ascended into Heaven

### What is it to ascend into Heaven?

To ascend into Heaven signifieth in the Creed to pass out of this visible conversation into the life invisible. How this was done the History sheweth, **Acts 1**.

### What doth "to Ascend" signify?

It signifieth visibly to pass out of this visible conversation, and to be exalted by the Right Hand of God above all Heavens unto the Height, and Eminence, and Divine Glory, and to have all things subject unto Himself, and present, and serving.

### Who doth so expound the force of the word "to Ascend"?

The Son of God Himself, saying: *"I ascend unto my Father, and your Father."* And: *"All Power is given unto me in Heaven and in earth."* And the Apostles interpret this Article in these Forms of speech:

> He ascended into the Heavens. He was received up into Heaven.  
> He received Heaven.  
> He ascended up far above all Heavens.  
> He entered into the Glory of His Father.  
> He was made Higher than the Heavens.  
> He received Power over all Powers.  
> All things are put under His feet.  
> He hath passed through the Heavens.  

<!-- p.281 -->

### What doth "Heaven" signify in this Article?

It signifieth not only the Air, the Elements, the Clouds, or the Firmament, the Stars, the Planets, or the Corporeal Heavens, all which Christ ascending passed through with His Body; neither doth it signify a Physical place in some Heaven, where Christ, circumscribed in His Body, is contained: but (as we say, *Our Father, which art in heaven*) it signifieth the heavenly Habitation and Throne of God, who dwelleth in the light which no man can approach unto, above all Heavens, and above all places. For Christ ascended up far above all Heavens, and entered into the Glory of His Father, being gone into Heaven, Angels, and Authorities, and Powers being made subject unto Him, **1 Pet. 3**.

## *Sedet ad dexteram Dei Patris omnipotentis* · He Sitteth on the Right Hand of God the Father Almighty

### Why did Christ ascend with His Body into Heaven?

1. That He might refute the dreams of the Jews concerning a Political Kingdom of the Messiah.
2. That He might testify that He would not converse with us in a visible and comprehensible manner before the Last Day.
3. That He might prepare a place for us, and an Access unto the Heavens and unto the Fellowship of all Angels and men.
4. That He might put all things under His feet, and Himself be the Head of the Church, in Merit, Efficacy, Dignity, and Perfection.
5. That He might bear witness of the Victory against the Kingdom of sin and death.
6. <!-- p.282 -->That He might rule His Church from above, and be present with her even unto the End of the world, and might fill all things, **Ephes. 4**. *"He ascended up far above all Heavens, that He might fill all things,"* giving Gifts unto men, entering into the Holy of Holies, our Propitiator and Advocate.
7. That He might draw our hearts unto Himself, **Phil. 3**. *"Our* ***πολίτευμα*** (conversation, or citizenship) *is in Heaven."* **Coloss. 3.** *"Set your affection on things above, where Christ sitteth on the Right Hand of the Father."*
8. That He might sit on the Right Hand of the Father, and might bestow also in Himself upon His human nature the Glory which from eternity He had as God with the Father, according to that saying, **John 17**: *"Father, glorify thou me with thine own self with the Glory which I had with thee before the World was."* Of which words **Cyril** speaketh most weightily thus: *"His own Glory, which He always hath as God, He asked as man. Nor are these things said by Him because He was ever void of His own Glory, but because He would bring up His own Temple into the Glory which is always present with Him as God."* And: *"He asked not Glory of the Father for Himself, whereof, as being God by nature, He had no need; but by asking, as man, He bringeth over the Father's glory unto the man whom He assumed."* Likewise **Augustine**: *"He ascended into the Heavens, that is, the condition of our nature, which, being born of a human mother, He assumed, He placed above the Heavens on the right hand of God the Father,* ***ἐκάθισεν***, *that is, He made it to sit."* And **Cyprian**: *"He ascended into the Heavens, not where God the Word had not been before, seeing He was always in the Heavens, and abode in the Father, but where the Word made flesh did not sit before."* And: *"To sit on the right hand of the Father is a Mystery of the assumed flesh: for neither are these things fitly added to that incorporeal nature without the assumption of the flesh; neither doth the divine nature seek the perfection of the heavenly seat, but the human."*

<!-- p.283 -->

### Did Christ so ascend into Heaven, that before the last day He can be with His Body nowhere else than in a certain place of Heaven?

By no means. For because He ascended into Heaven that He might carry up His Body to the right hand of the Majesty and power of God, therefore also He can be with His Body wheresoever He will be, without hurt to the truth of His Body, although the manner whereby that may be done square not with Physical rules and properties. For all things are possible with God, *"who doth with His Body whatsoever He will, and yet it remaineth a Body,"* saith **Augustine**.

### Must not Christ be received by Heaven, that is, be contained, as it is written, Acts 3: "Christ must be received by Heaven until the times of the restitution of all things"?

In no wise is Christ contained by Heaven, so that He cannot be elsewhere on earth with His Body and Blood. And the version of the place **Acts 3** is plainly perverse.[^F3-3] For Christ received Heaven, possessed it, occupied it, subjected it unto Himself, that is, as **Peter** expoundeth himself: *"He is on the right hand of God, being gone into Heaven, Angels, Authorities, and Powers being made subject unto Him."* And the Heavens received Christ ascending no otherwise than that with joy and triumph they most humbly subjected themselves unto Him, and acknowledge and salute and proclaim Him Lord.

### Is not Christ by a bodily location in some place of Heaven, ***σώματι περίγραπτος*** (circumscribed in body)?

Most freely can Christ be wheresoever He will be. <!-- p.284 -->By a bodily location indeed, as touching the manner of a true Body, Physically: but without Physical circumscription, Hyperphysically and in a Divine manner, unknown to us, because He hath not only a human Body, as other men have, but hath carried up that very Body to the Right Hand of His Almighty Father, above all heavens, above all places, and above all Orders, He only excepted which did put all things under Him, because He sitteth not above Him, but on His right hand.

### In what place then is Christ with His human Body?

No other place can be named but the Right Hand of God, if this for teaching's sake may be called a place. For to him that asketh, Where Christ is in Heaven, the Scripture answereth: On the Right Hand of God the Father Almighty. For He ascended into the Heavens that He might sit on the Right Hand of the Father.

### What doth the Right Hand of God signify?

**Augustine:**

> *"By the Right Hand understand ye the Power which that man received, who was taken up by God."*

**Cyril:**

> *"He ascended with the flesh into the Glory of the Godhead, and is believed to be Lord of all even with the flesh. Every knee boweth to Him, and that not to the grief and reproach of the Father, but rather to His Glory. For He rejoiceth and is glorified together, while the Son is adored of all, although He was made like unto us."*

The Right Hand of God therefore is not a Corporal place, but is the Majesty, Power, and supreme Dominion of God, the Might and Magnificence of God.

### What doth it signify to Sit on the Right Hand of God?

It signifieth to be partaker of the supreme Dominion and Judgement, and <!-- p.285 -->to reign with equal power with God over all Creatures, and to be adored and worshipped of all Creatures.

### To which nature in Christ was it said by the Father: "Sit thou at my Right Hand"?

To the human nature. For according to His eternal Divinity Christ not only always reigneth with the Father and the Holy Ghost with equal power, but is, and is called, the very Right Hand of God. But according to the manhood He began at a certain time to sit on the Right Hand of God, and reigneth for ever God and man in the Trinity itself over all Creatures, and is invoked of us God and man, as often as we say:

> Our Father, which art in Heaven.

**Chrysostom:**

> *"Let us learn what Nature it is to which He said: Sit thou at my Right Hand, and be partaker of my Seat. That, namely, which had heard: Earth thou art, and unto Earth shalt thou go."*

**Athanasius:**

> *"These words (Sit thou at my Right Hand) were spoken of the Lord's Body. For if 'I fill Heaven and Earth, saith the Lord,' God containeth all things, and is contained of none: on what seat doth He not sit? It is the Body therefore to which He saith: Sit thou at my Right Hand."*

That is, the Divinity of Christ is beforehand everywhere, and filleth all things, and therefore unto it it cannot be said: *Sit thou at my Right Hand.* But these words are directed unto the human nature in Christ, or unto the Son of man, in so far as He is man, the holy Body not being laid aside, but united together in the perfection of the one Godhead unto equality of honour. Likewise **Cyprian**: *"That the invisible God received the visible Son unto His Right Hand is manifest: and that the lowliness of our flesh, honoured with the Throne of Judgement, was well-pleasing to the Father's Highness."*

<!-- p.286 -->

### Doth Christ sit in His humanity on the Right Hand of the Power and Majesty of God in the same manner wherein He sitteth on the Same in His eternal and Divine Nature?

There is not a twofold Session at the Right Hand of God, and a twofold Throne of God, or a twofold Omnipotence and Majesty: but Christ hath at a certain time so given, communicated, and attributed unto His assumed nature also His Glory, Majesty, Dominion, and Power, which He hath from everlasting to everlasting, that that Glory, Might, and Majesty doth wholly shine in the human nature, putteth itself forth through it, and exerciseth its actions with it.

**Cyril:**

> *"The Divine Nature quickeneth of itself, unto whose power the Flesh ascendeth by conjunction. But how that is done we can neither understand with the mind nor speak with the tongue, but we receive it with silence and firm Faith."*

### Dost thou say that the human nature in Christ is equal to God?

No. For God is the Head of Christ the man, **Ephes. 1**,[^F3-4] and the human nature is always under God, although it be carried up above all Creatures. God giveth, it receiveth. God is the Creator: it is created. The Son of God is the very Right Hand of God: but it is carried up to the Right Hand of God. The Son of God is the Lord, the inhabitant: it is the temple and habitation, wherein dwelleth all the fulness of the Godhead ***σωματικῶς*** (bodily). The Son of God is He that assumeth and glorifieth: it is assumed, and glorified in Christ. The Son of God could do all things even without it: but it without Christ and out of Christ could do nothing. The Son of God of Himself and in Himself hath all things: but it, not according to itself, but as given unto it of Christ, receiveth, and in Christ hath and retaineth, all <!-- p.287 -->Glory and Power. And yet it is one and the same Christ, Creator, Father, God, Lord, and our brother, our flesh and blood at the Right Hand of God, to whom be praise, honour, glory, thanks, worship, and celebration for ever, Amen.

## *Inde venturus est iudicare vivos et mortuos* · From Thence He Shall Come to Judge the Quick and the Dead

Christ shall return in the visible form wherein also He ascended into Heaven, **Acts 1**, and in the universal judgement He shall judge the quick, which shall then remain in the World, and the dead, which are already buried, and shall be buried before the judgement, being raised up and called together by the Trump of the Archangel; that is, He shall punish the ungodly, and deliver the godly, and adorn the Church with life eternal, according to the process which Christ Himself describeth, **Matth. 25**.

## *De Tertio Articulo Symboli Apostolici, id est, de Spiritu Sancto, de Ecclesia, et praecipuis Dei beneficiis* · On the Third Article of the Apostles' Creed, that is, of the Holy Ghost, of the Church, and of the Chief Benefits of God

### What are the testimonies proving the Divinity of the Holy Ghost?

1. The testimonies of Scripture, **Acts 5**: *"He hath lied to the Holy Ghost, not unto men, but unto God."*
2. The work of creation, **Job 33**: *"The Spirit of the Lord hath made me."*
3. To be everywhere, **Ps. 139**: *"Whither shall I go from thy Spirit?"*
4. To be ***καρδιογνώστην*** (a knower of hearts), **Wisd. 1**: *"The Spirit of the Lord hath filled the world, containeth all things, and hath knowledge of the voice."*
5. To be the quickener, **Genes. 1**: The Spirit of the Lord did quicken by His moving the things created.
6. <!-- p.288 -->The worship of faith, which is due to God alone.
7. The Confession of the true Church.

### What are the benefits of the Holy Ghost?

1\. To teach. 2. To renew and sanctify. 3. To help by testifying of Christ, by giving, by comforting, by praying. 4. To seal the promises. 5. To be the earnest of our salvation and eternal inheritance. See concerning these the *Paedagogia*.[^F3-5]

### What is the Church?

The Church is the visible assembly of men who embrace the uncorrupt sense of the Gospel, and retain the true use of the Sacraments, and Reverence toward the Ministry, wherein the Son of God is truly effectual through the word, regenerateth and sanctifieth many, that they may be made heirs of eternal life, although in this assembly there be many hypocrites mingled, who nevertheless retain consent concerning Doctrine and concerning the use of the Sacraments.

### What is the Catholic Church?

The Catholic Church is that which throughout the whole world is gathered out of the whole human race by the voice of the Gospel, and embraceth the universal and entire doctrine delivered by the Prophets and Apostles, and retaineth consent and unity in the foundation, although in rites and ceremonies there be some unlikeness, as in the greek and latin Church the Ceremonies are unlike concerning Easter, concerning leaven, concerning fasts.

And the word *Catholic* is opposed to Jewish, Popish, and heretical imaginations; for ***καθολικὸν*** is the same as *universal*: and therefore the Church is so named, because she is not tied to a certain people, to certain persons, to certain places, and to an ordinary succession of Bishops, but is wheresoever the voice of the Gospel soundeth. Next, because in all ages of the world she is gathered out of the human race. Lastly, because she embraceth the whole body of the Prophetical and Apostolical doctrine.

<!-- p.289 -->

### What are the signs that shew the true Church?

They are three. 1. Consent in the uncorrupt doctrine of the Gospel, as touching the foundation. 2. The lawful use of the Sacraments. 3. The Obedience due to the Ministry according to the Gospel. These are the substantials. But Ceremonies are accidentals.

### How is the Church holy, seeing she hath yet much infirmity and filth?

As the justification of man before God consisteth in imputation alone, whereby the righteousness of Christ is imputed to them that believe, which is to be received by faith: so the sanctification of man in this life consisteth in the same imputation, and moreover in an inchoation, whereby spiritual newness is begun in them that believe through the Holy Ghost. The Church therefore in this life is holy in two ways, by Imputation and by inchoation. In the world to come she shall be holy by consummation, where God shall be all in all.

### Why callest thou the Church holy, whereas nevertheless there are many ungodly, evil, and wicked men in the Church?

The article of the holy Church is understood of the living members of the Church, not of the dead members; and she is in this life named holy by *Synecdoche*, because there are many members sanctified by the Holy Ghost and elect. Now the naming is made from the principal part, and the sanctified members are commonly named the living members of the Church. The others, not holy, are called dead members, if so be they consent concerning doctrine.

<!-- p.290 -->

### What is the meaning of these words: "I believe the communion of Saints"?

These words are the definition of the Church, and are added by grammatical apposition. For to him that asketh what the Church is, answer is made briefly and roundly in the words of the Creed: The Church is the communion of Saints, that is, the Society or assembly of men who are called by the Gospel unto the inheritance of eternal life, and are one body, because they partake of one bread, which is the ***κοινωνία*** or communion of the Body of Christ, whereby the Body of Christ is communicated unto Christians, and they have the benefits of Christ in common, namely, Remission of sins, the Holy Ghost, righteousness and life eternal, although in gifts there be unlikeness, and although they live in divers times and places.

### For what causes is the Church of God subject to the cross?

Of all the calamities of the whole human race, of the Church and of the ungodly, the common efficient and impelling causes are four.

1. The common sin of the whole human race, which God willeth to be abolished; therefore He destroyeth the sinful nature by death and other calamities.
2. The errors of the human mind.
3. Evil lusts.
4. The tyranny of the Devil casting men headlong into miseries and calamities.

**And the chief ends are two.**

1. That men may acknowledge that God is wise and just, <!-- p.291 -->and that the wrath of God against sin and unrighteousness is true and dreadful.
2. That by calamities men may be bowed unto the fear of God, unto modesty, and unto the bridling of the lusts whereby afflictions are drawn upon them.

**But of the calamities of the Church fourteen peculiar causes are reckoned, whereof four are impelling.**

1. The Fall of our first Parents, and the sin thence derived unto all.
2. The inward uncleanness of nature, namely doubtings concerning God, carnal security, admiration of self, the ***ἀταξία*** (disorder) of all the affections, etc., which the world neglecteth; this God willeth to be acknowledged and amended by the Church.
3. The hatred of the Devil, who lieth in wait for the Church more furiously than for other men.
4. Certain offences of the Saints in the Church, which are punished with certain punishments.

**The Final Causes are ten.**

1. God layeth not calamities upon us to the end that He may destroy us, but that He may do us good. **Ezech. 33.** *"As I live, I have no pleasure in the death of the sinner; but this I will, that he turn and live."* **Ps. 118 [119].** *"It is good for me, O Lord, that thou hast afflicted me."*
2. That the virtues of the Saints may be proved, exercised, and confirmed, the Fear of God, Faith, invocation, patience, Confession, etc. These highest virtues grow when the Saints are exercised with afflictions; in prosperity they become more languid, and by little and little are utterly quenched.
3. That the Church may acknowledge the wrath of God against the common sin of the whole human race, and against the inward uncleanness, which the world neglecteth.
4. <!-- p.292 -->That afflictions may be testimonies of the immortality of the godly.
5. That they may be testimonies of the certainty of the doctrine, and may be occasions of learning the doctrine more diligently.
6. That the godly may be made like unto the image of the Son of God, **Rom. 8**.
7. That they may amend inward sins, doubtings, pride, security, etc., and that they may beware of future falls.
8. That it may appear that the Church obeyeth, not for any commodities of the body, but for the glory of God alone.
9. That the presence and help of Christ may be more clearly beheld in wonderful deliverances.
10. That they may obtain the most ample rewards promised to the afflicted. **Matth. 5.** *"Of such is the kingdom of Heaven."* **2 Cor. 4.** *"The momentary lightness of afflictions worketh an eternal weight of glory."*

### How are the degrees of afflictions in the Church distinguished?

The kinds are four.

1. ***τιμωρίαι*** (punishments), as the exile of David.
2. ***δοκιμασίαι*** (trials), as the Prison of Joseph.
3. ***μαρτύρια*** (testimonies, or martyrdoms), as the death of the Baptist, of Paul, etc.
4. ***λύτρον*** (ransom), which is the obedience of Christ alone.

Christ hanging on the cross payeth the ***λύτρον***. The Thief on the left hand suffereth a ***τιμωρίαν*** (punishment) corporal and eternal. The Thief converted becometh a ***μάρτυρ***, and witness of Christ's innocency and Merit. Mary and John standing by the cross ***δοκιμάζονται*** (are tried).

### What are the chief and effectual consolations to be set against the cross and calamities?

<!-- p.293 -->

The Heathen or philosophical consolations are seven.

1. The Necessity of bearing evils which cannot be taken away by grieving.
2. Virtue is to be preferred before grief.
3. A good conscience.
4. Examples.
5. The comparison of events.
6. The final Causes, or the profits.
7. The uncertain hope of deliverance.

But of Christian consolation there are twelve fountains, whereof the six former, although in title they agree with the Philosophical, yet contain Greater things; and six others are added, truly effectual, and unknown to the Philosophers, but revealed in the Gospel alone and proper to the Church.

1. The Necessity of obeying God, under whose mighty hand we must be humbled, and in no wise resist His wisdom and justice.
2. The Dignity of virtue, and of godliness toward God, and of confession, which is to be preferred before all things prosperous or adverse.
3. A good conscience, and the healing of the wounded conscience through faith in the Son of God.
4. The examples of the Son of God, of Abel, of the Apostles, of the Martyrs, and of the whole Church, which must be made like unto the image of the Son of God.
5. The comparison of events. For in prosperity all men become more secure and more insolent.
6. The ten final Causes have been before recited, whereunto let testimonies be joined.
7. The acknowledgement of the good will of God certainly promised for Christ's sake, or of the benevolence of God toward us.
8. The Presence of God in afflictions.
9. The Promise of Divine help strengthening hearts in afflictions, that they may be able to bear the burden through.
10. <!-- p.294 -->The Promise of the mitigation of calamities in this life, and of certain deliverance, either in this life or in the life eternal.
11. When unto these promises there cometh faith, which is assured that God is present with us and doth mitigate calamities, invocation, obedience, hope of help and deliverance, patience, etc.: all these joined together become a sacrifice most acceptable unto God.
12. The last and chief place of consolation is the immortality of the Church, the resurrection of bodies, and the haven of eternal life, wherein the Church, delivered out of all miseries, shall most sweetly rest, and shall be filled of God with new Light, Wisdom, Righteousness, glory and gladness.

### Are not calamities mitigated unto them that are truly converted unto God?

That either deliverance or mitigation of temporal punishments doth by God's hand befall them that are converted unto God, the example of the Ninevites sheweth, and the saying of Christ: *"Except ye repent, ye shall all likewise perish."* Now temporal punishments are mitigated altogether for the sake of the Son of God, who is the shadow and covert of the Church. Afterward this also is true, that in the converted the endeavour of well-doing hath its rewards even in this life.

## *Credo Remissionem peccatorum* · I Believe the Remission of Sins

These words deliver the doctrine of the justification of man before God, to which doctrine may fitly be added the doctrine of continual repentance. For we have always need of the free remission of sins for the Son's sake. We shall therefore repeat the true, customary, and simple questions.

### What is justification?

It is the Remission of sins, and the imputation of the righteousness of Christ, <!-- p.295 -->and the free acceptation unto life eternal, not for any virtues or works, but for Christ the Mediator alone, apprehended by faith alone.

### How is remission of sins, or justification, received?

By faith alone in the Son of God Jesus Christ, that is, freely, not for our own works or merits, but only for the one Mediator, Jesus Christ, who was made a sacrifice and propitiator for us, doth man obtain remission of sins, and is made righteous, that is, pleasing and accepted unto God, righteousness being imputed for Christ's sake, and is made an heir of eternal life, when he certainly believeth that these good things are given him of God for the Son's sake. For so it is written, **Acts 10**: *"To him give all the Prophets witness, that through his name all who believe in him receive Remission of sins."* **Rom. 3.** *"We are justified freely by his grace through the redemption that is in Christ Jesus: whom God hath set forth to be a propitiator, through faith in his blood."*

### What is the difference between the righteousness of the Law and of the Gospel?

The Law promiseth remission of sins, righteousness, and Life eternal, but withal requireth perfect obedience, as the cause.

Therefore the righteousness of the Law is either a perfect obedience of all the powers toward the whole law of God, void of all sin, such as hath been in no man, Christ only excepted; or it is an obedience, such as it is, of the outward members, performing the outward works which the Law of God requireth, and it never satisfieth the law of God.

But the Gospel freely promiseth Remission of <!-- p.296 -->sins, righteousness, and Life eternal, not for our obedience toward the law, but for the Son of God alone, our Lord Jesus Christ, whom we embrace by faith alone.

### What are the weights of the words, "I believe the remission of sins"?

First, it is a testification concerning faith, whereby every man ought firmly to hold that unto himself, believing, remission of sins is certainly given in this life, against the popish doubting.

Next, whereas sins are named in the plural number, it is a confirmation of most sweet consolation: That every sin is remitted, namely unto him that repenteth and by faith embraceth the promise of grace, certainly ratified for the sake of the Son of God.

Thirdly, the word *remitting* teacheth that not only the fault is forgiven, but also the eternal punishment. For to remit is to deliver from the fault and from the eternal punishment: and this relation is chiefly comprehended in the word *remission*, namely the loosing of the obligation unto eternal death and damnation, and the forgiving of the punishment due.

Fourthly. Because the recitation of the Creed is made daily, let the remission of sins be understood of a remission perpetually enduring and promised, even after we are born again; and let the regenerate also know that in this life there always remain the remnants of sin, the remission whereof is to be asked of God.

### What is the cause of Remission?

Unless remission were free, it could not be called remission, but rather compensation. Therefore there is in us no Meritorious cause of remission: But, if thou look unto Christ, there is a full satisfaction for sins.

<!-- p.297 -->

### What doth the word "remission" teach besides?

1\. It destroyeth the dogma of the Monks concerning satisfactions. 2. It overthroweth all human merits. 3. It refuteth the error of the Novatians, who denied to the lapsed a return unto grace. 4. It testifieth that all the regenerate carry sin about with them, so long as they are conversant in this life. 5. It openeth the door of salvation to wretched and afflicted consciences. 6. In the agony of death it is a fountain of consolation. 7. It perpetually testifieth of the price of the sacrifice, by the benefit whereof we are received into grace, earnestly asking pardon.[^F3-6]

### Is not sin abolished, when it is remitted?

There are two things in sin, namely the defect and the guilt. The defect itself is daily diminished in the Saints through the mortification of the old man. But while we carry about this mass, it is not altogether abolished. For there remaineth perpetually in this life the strife of the spirit and the flesh in the Saints, **Galat. 5**. But the guilt is so abolished in the godly, that God saith He will not remember their sins, **Isaiah 45**:[^F3-7] *"I blot out thine iniquities before me, and will not remember thy sins."*

### Is the remission of the fault distinct from the remission of the eternal punishment?

It is not distinct, but the remission of the fault is altogether the same as the Remission of the eternal punishment, according to the saying, **Isa. 45**: *"I daily blot out thine iniquities before me, and will not remember thy sins any more."* And **Isaiah 1**: *"Though your sins be as Scarlet, and as crimson,"* etc.

<!-- p.298 -->

### Is the remission of the fault distinct from the remission of temporal punishment in this life?

It is distinct. As Adam and David receive remission of the fault, and yet withal are subjected to punishments in this life. But these punishments are in no wise compensations or merits in place of the eternal punishment, as the Monks have feigned, but have altogether other causes, which it is necessary for all men to know, for doctrine and consolation.

### Why then are the Saints often punished for their sins?

The causes have been set forth above. Yet the Saints are not so much chastised because they have sinned, as restrained, that they sin no more. For God chasteneth His Saints with a fatherly discipline, that they should not be condemned with this world; but He punisheth not with judicial vengeance, that they should perish.

### What is justifying Faith?

It is a knowledge of the person and benefits of Christ and of the whole doctrine delivered of God: and an assent, whereby we embrace the whole doctrine, and in it the promise of free reconciliation, or of the imputation of righteousness, to be given for Christ's sake: and a trust resting in Christ the Mediator according to the promise, and certainly holding that sins are remitted unto him, that he pleaseth God, that he is received and heard for Christ alone, freely, not for his own worthiness.

### How is this proposition to be understood, "By faith alone we are righteous"?

<!-- p.299 -->

It is to be understood correlatively, namely, that for the Son of God alone, our Lord Jesus Christ, we receive remission of sins, righteousness, and life eternal freely, not for our own merits.

And the Scripture therefore useth this form of words, *By faith we are justified*, that it may teach both things.

1. What is the cause or merit of justification, or what are the benefits of Christ: Namely, that through and for Christ alone there is given unto us remission of sins, righteousness, and life eternal.
2. Next, how they ought to be applied unto us and transferred unto us: Namely, by faith alone embracing the promise and resting in Christ. It is commonly said thus: *"Not for our believing, but for Him in whom we believe, are we justified."* Likewise: *"As a ring is greatly esteemed for the gem's sake: So faith justifieth, not because it is a quality in us, but because it apprehendeth the Mediator."*

[^F3-1]: The sentence stands in the *Exposition of the Creed* of Rufinus of Aquileia, which the sixteenth-century editions printed among the works of Cyprian. The two sayings on the ascension and the session quoted under Cyprian's name below (*"He ascended into the Heavens, not where God the Word had not been before…"* and *"To sit on the right hand of the Father is a Mystery of the assumed flesh…"*) come from the same work.

[^F3-2]: Irenaeus, *Adversus haereses* III, 19, 3: the Word "resting" (*requiescente Verbo* in the old Latin version), that He might be able to be tempted, dishonoured, crucified, and to die.

[^F3-3]: Selnecker cites and rejects the passive Latin rendering of Acts 3:21, which he quotes as *Oportet Christum coelo capi* ("Christ must be received by heaven", taken as "contained"), which was used by the Reformed against the bodily presence in the Supper; he reads the clause actively, "who must receive heaven". His Latin is translated here as he gives it; the King James wording ("whom the heaven must receive") is not his.

[^F3-4]: So the print. The words "the head of Christ is God" stand in 1 Cor. 11:3; Eph. 1 speaks of Christ set at the right hand and given as head to the Church.

[^F3-5]: Selnecker's own *Paedagogia Christiana*, to which he refers the student for fuller treatment.

[^F3-6]: The print has *petentes condemnationem* ("asking condemnation"), an evident misprint for *condonationem* ("pardon"), which is translated.

[^F3-7]: So the print, here and in the next answer. The words are those of Isa. 43:25.

### What do these particles exclude: By faith alone, Freely, Without the Law, Without the works of the Law?

They do not exclude the merit of Christ, but they exclude the merit and worthiness of all our works, whether these go before, or are present, or follow after; and they transfer the cause of the remission of sins, or of justification, unto Christ the Mediator alone, laid hold of by faith, so that the article of Justification standeth in the sole merit of Christ alone, without any addition of our works. And in the beginning, the middle and the end it is always to be held that we are righteous by faith and pleasing unto God, and heirs of life everlasting, for the sake of Christ the Mediator alone, not for the sake of any works or virtues of ours, as **Paul** disputeth at large, **Gal. 3**. **Rom. 5**: *"Being justified <!-- p.300 --> by faith, we have peace with God through our Lord Jesus Christ."* **Hab. 2**: *"The just shall live by his faith."* Neither can faith lean upon two things, Christ and our works. Otherwise it would become altogether uncertain, and would bring no firm consolation, **Rom. 4**. For sins always remain even in the Saints in this life, for which they pray: *"Forgive us our debts."* Therefore Christ is a Priest for ever, making intercession for us and applying his righteousness unto us; *"neither is there salvation in any other."*

### Why is the exclusive particle "Freely" to be retained, and likewise "We are justified by faith alone"?

For five causes.

1. Because there is no other means whereby we can acknowledge and lay hold of the promise of grace and of Christ, save faith alone.

2. That the honour which is due and proper unto Christ may be given unto him: namely, that his obedience alone is the merit of the remission of sins, of righteousness, and of the life everlasting that is to be given unto us, and that our works are not a merit for the sake of which we are received.

3. That consciences may have a sure and firm consolation, the promise of grace leaneth upon the Son of God alone and not upon our merits; therefore it is said, *"By faith alone,"* that the heart may assuredly hold that God is gracious unto us for Christ's sake. This cause **Paul** rehearseth, **Rom. 4**: *"Therefore it is of faith, freely, that the promise might be sure."*

4. That the difference of the Law and the Gospel may be the more clearly seen. For the particles *Freely*, *By faith*, and the express mention of the Mediator do chiefly distinguish the Law and the Gospel.

5. That true invocation may be rendered, which must needs lean upon the Mediator alone, the Son of God, not upon our virtues.

### What is it that is named Grace, and what the Gift?

<!-- p.301 -->

*Grace* (*gratia*) is justification itself, that is, the absolution from sin of the person that believeth in Christ, the imputation of the righteousness of Christ, and the acceptance unto life everlasting, freely, for Christ's sake. But the *Gift* (*donum*) is the giving of the Holy Ghost, whereby the man that is justified by faith alone is regenerated, that is, is mortified and quickened, so that he now becometh a new creature and liveth according to the will of God, so far indeed as this can be done through human weakness, and that after the example of the holy Patriarchs and of other godly men, whose chief endeavour it was to set obedience toward God before all the things of the world. Even as we see in Abraham, who at the commandment of God was ready to slay his only son, than whom he had nothing in the world more precious, more pleasant, more sweet. And therefore **Paul** admonisheth that, *"warring a good warfare, we hold faith and a good conscience."*

### What is repentance?

Repentance (*poenitentia*), or conversion unto God, is to be terrified by the knowledge of the wrath of God against sins, and to grieve that we have offended God, and yet not to sink under the grief, but by faith in Christ to receive remission of sins, grace and the Holy Ghost, and in this consolation to begin a new obedience agreeing with the Law of God, and to be an heir of life everlasting.

### What are the parts of repentance?

They are three: 1. Contrition, which is truly to be terrified by the knowledge of the wrath of God against our sins, and to grieve that we have offended God. 2. Faith, acknowledging that the wrath of God against our sins is appeased for Christ's sake, and assuredly holding that sins are forgiven it, and that it pleaseth God for Christ's sake. 3. New obedience, which is, faith in Christ shining before, <!-- p.302 --> to obey God according to all his commandments, to this end, that God may be honoured and praised.

### Is the preaching of repentance to be taken from the Law, or from the Gospel?

Repentance properly so called, that is, contrition, the knowledge of sin and of the wrath of God, which is grief in the conscience, hath its origin simply from the Law. But that this contrition may become wholesome, and may not degenerate into servile fear and into despair, but may become filial fear, and that the man may be converted unto God, there is need of the doctrine of the Gospel, which showeth the Mediator, and through which the Holy Ghost kindleth in the mind the thought of the promise concerning the free remission of sins for Christ's sake, and enlighteneth and moveth the will, that it may assent and by true confidence apply the merit of Christ unto itself.

### In what articles chiefly is the conflict with the Adversaries?

In three: namely, concerning the Church, concerning the Remission of sins, concerning Repentance. The Church they transform into a political kingdom; they feign that the Roman Pontiff is the monarch and supreme head in the Church; they ascribe unto the Pope, the Bishops and the Councils the power of framing doctrines and worships, and of interpreting the Scriptures with a royal or praetorian authority; they bind the Church to the ordinary succession of the Pope and the Bishops, and to their traditions and interpretation. They contend that the observances of such decrees and traditions are of the necessity of salvation. Afterward they enact laws to establish their own power, as is the law concerning celibacy, and by bodily force they compel men to obey. This hypothesis being laid down, they afterward under the pretext of the Church establish idols, as the invocation <!-- p.303 --> of the dead, the profanation of the Supper in Masses, and the carrying about of the bread in shows.

The doctrine concerning justification, or the remission of sins, they corrupt in four ways. First, they teach that it is enough when a man hath a general faith, whereby he believeth that remission of sins befalleth some, although he distrusteth that it befalleth himself. Secondly, they teach that a man meriteth remission of sins and is righteous, not for the sake of Christ alone by faith alone, but also by good works, or by his own virtues agreeing with the Law of God; likewise by monastic works, Masses, fastings, etc. Thirdly, since no man knoweth whether he hath enough of good works, they teach that we must always doubt whether we have remission of sins, and whether we be in grace. Fourthly, they teach that a man can in this life satisfy the Law of God, and by this fulfilling of the Law is righteous, and meriteth life everlasting.

The parts of repentance they make to be: contrition of the heart, confession of the mouth, satisfaction of the work. They therefore omit faith, which is the chief part of repentance, and unto those very parts which they reckon up they add horrible errors; as, concerning contrition they have two foul errors:

1. That contrition ought to be sufficient; whereas it is certain that no griefs of any creature, how great soever, are equal to the wrath of God, nor can make compensation for sins.

2. That contrition meriteth remission of sins. This detestable error transferreth unto our work the honour that is due unto the Son of God alone, and fighteth with the whole doctrine of justification.

Concerning confession they teach that the enumeration of all offences is necessary, and meriteth remission of sins. Concerning satisfaction they teach that works not due, that is, not commanded by the Law of God, but works of supererogation, merit the remission of the pains of purgatory, or of other pains of this life, etc. Against these lies <!-- p.304 --> it is enough to set this one rule: *"In vain they do worship me with the commandments of men."*

## *Credo carnis resurrectionem, et vitam aeternam* · I Believe in the Resurrection of the Flesh, and the Life Everlasting

**Cyprian:**[^F4-1]

> *"I believe in the resurrection of this flesh,"* ***δεικτικῶς*** (demonstratively, that is, pointing to it).

### What is the Resurrection of the dead?

It is a universal outward action of God, whereby God, according to his immense power, and according to the most free decree of his will, shall deliver the bodies of all men from this alternation of generations and corruptions, and shall join them again with their souls, that unto all eternity they may either pay the penalties of their wickedness together with the devils, or enjoy the communication of the divine light, wisdom, righteousness and life.

### Recite some testimonies concerning the immortality of the soul, the Resurrection of bodies, the Last Judgment, and the Life everlasting.

**On the immortality of the soul.**

**Matt. 10.**

> *"They are not able to kill the soul."*

**Luke 23.**

> *"To day shalt thou be with me in paradise."*

**Matt. 17.**

Christ talketh with Moses and Elias, who had died long before.

**Exod. 3.**

> *"God is the God of Abraham, Isaac and Jacob."*

This saying is cited <!-- p.305 --> **Matt. 22**.

**Luke 16.**

Lazarus and the rich man live.

**2 Pet. 2.**[^F4-2]

> *"Christ preached unto the spirits in prison."*

**On the Resurrection of bodies.**

The seat of the matter concerning the Resurrection of the dead is the fifteenth chapter of the former Epistle to the Corinthians.

**1 Thess. 4.**

> *"Concerning them which are asleep I would not have you to be ignorant, brethren, that ye sorrow not, even as others which have no hope of the Resurrection."*

**John 5.**

> *"The hour shall come, when all that are in the graves shall hear the voice of the Son of God, and shall come forth, some unto the resurrection of life, others unto the resurrection of punishment."*

**John 6.**

> *"This is the will of God, that every one which seeth the Son, and believeth on him, should not perish, but have everlasting life: and I will raise him up at the last day."*

**John 11.**

Lazarus is raised up. *"I am the resurrection, and the life."*

**Job 19.**

> *"I know that at the last day I shall rise again out of the earth, and I shall be clothed again with my skin, and in my flesh I shall see God,"* etc.

**On the Last Judgment.**

**Matt. 25.**

> *"When the Son of man shall come in his glory, and all the holy angels with him, then shall he sit upon the throne of his glory, and shall judge,"* etc.

<!-- p.306 -->

**Acts 17.**

> *"God hath appointed a day, in the which he will judge the world in righteousness."*

**2 Thess. 1.**

> *"In the revelation of the Lord Jesus from heaven they shall be punished with everlasting destruction, that obeyed not the Gospel."*

**Rom. 2.**

> *"Thou treasurest up unto thyself wrath against the day of wrath and revelation of the righteous judgment of God, who will render to every man according to his deeds."*

**On the Life everlasting.**

**John 3.**

> *"God so loved the world, that he gave his only begotten Son, that whosoever believeth in him should not perish, but have everlasting life."*

**John 6.**

> *"This is the will of God, that every one which believeth on the Son may have everlasting life."*

### How manifold is the condition of them that shall rise again?

Fourfold.

1. They shall rise again with the same bodies.

2. They shall have incorruptible bodies: the good in glory, the evil in punishment.

3. They shall rise again with the entireness of all the parts of the body.

4. They shall never die.

### How manifold shall be the condition of the good that rise again?

Fourfold.

<!-- p.307 -->

1\. Brightness (*claritas*). 2. Impassibility (*impassibilitas*). 3. Agility (*agilitas*). 4. Subtilty (*subtilitas*). For the bodies shall be spiritual, not that they shall be spirits, but in all things subject unto the spirit, so that where the spirit shall will to be, there the bodies shall be, at the beck and thought and will of the blessed man. And seeing this shall be so with our bodies, what is to be thought of the Body of Christ in the unity ***τοῦ λόγου*** (of the Word), lifted up unto the right hand of the Father Almighty?

### How many kinds of Life and of Death are there?

Four.

1. The life of nature, and the death of nature.
2. The life of sin, and the death of sin.
3. The life of grace, and the death of grace.
4. The life everlasting, and the death everlasting.

### What is the Life of Nature?

It is that whereby we naturally live in this life, so long as soul and body are joined together. This is good in itself, because it is the gift of God, and because it is promised unto them that obey God as the reward of obedience, **Exod. 20**. But this life varieth according to the condition of men. For unto Abraham it was good, because he spent it in godliness and in the spreading abroad of the glory of God. But unto Nero it was evil, because he abused it unto the reproach of its Author, and unto his own destruction.

Unto this life of nature is opposed the death of nature, which is the departing of souls from bodies. This is in itself evil, but it varieth according to the condition of men. For as unto Abraham it was good, for it was unto him a passage from the calamities of this world unto everlasting felicity, so unto Nero it was evil, for unto him it was a passage from the pleasures, the power and the tyranny of this world unto perpetual reproach and everlasting contempt.

<!-- p.308 -->

### What is the Life of sin?

It is that whereby sin liveth in a man, and reigneth through its lusts. This is always evil, because it is both hateful unto God, and is the way unto everlasting death and damnation. Unto this is opposed the death of sin, and it is when sin is mortified in a man by the power of Christ. This death is good, as being a passage from deserved damnation unto free blessedness.

### What is the Life of Grace?

It is that whereby Christ liveth in us through faith and his Spirit, **Gal. 2**. This is always good, because it is the power of the Spirit of God in us, and is the way unto glory. Unto this is opposed the death of grace, which is the privation of grace, namely, when we cast away faith and fall back into sins, so that sin reigneth in us anew through its lusts, and we obey it knowingly and willingly. This death is evil, because it is condemned of God, and is the guide unto everlasting torments.

### What is the Life everlasting?

It is both Christ himself, **1 John 5**, and the blessed felicity which the elect shall enjoy in all eternity, their bodies being raised up. This life is the end of good things, and so the chief of all good things, which the Lord had promised to them that love him. Unto this is opposed the death everlasting, which is the separation of a man from Christ, so that he enjoyeth not the sight of him. This death is the uttermost of all evils, never to be ended. Hither belongeth that of **Augustine**: *"As the soul is the life of the body, so God is the life of the soul. As the body expireth when it loseth the soul, so the soul expireth when it loseth God. God lost is the death of the soul; the soul lost is the death of the body."*

<!-- p.309 -->

### To what purpose is it profitable to hold these differences of life and death?

That we may learn rightly to die, and rightly to live: to die indeed unto sin, but to live unto grace, that after this life, passed in true godliness even unto the end, we may come unto the life everlasting through Christ Jesus our Lord.

### What is the Life everlasting?

The Son of God our Lord, **John 17**, delivereth this wonderful definition: *"This is life eternal, that they might know thee to be the true God, and Jesus, whom thou hast sent, to be the Christ"*; namely, life everlasting is the knowledge of the true God and of Christ. And this definition is to be understood not only of the nature of that life, but also of the beginning of the life everlasting in this world.

Of the life to come it is commonly said: Life everlasting is the sight of God, wherein the Church, raised up from death and wholly delivered from all sin, shall behold face to face the Essence and Will of the whole Godhead, of the eternal Father, the Son and the Holy Ghost, and shall be filled with a new and perpetual light, wisdom, righteousness, life and everlasting gladness by God, who shall be all in all, and shall in turn praise God. For when God shall show himself face to face as favourable, and shall pour through us his light and wisdom, then shall there be kindled in us an unspeakable and perpetual gladness and life, when, namely, *"we shall see him as he is,"* **1 John 3**, and shall behold his Essence face to face, when God shall be all in all. The saying of **Augustine**: *"They shall rejoice as much as they shall love; they shall love as much as they shall know."* And they shall know God showing himself in immense mercy, and laying open his wisdom and righteousness, and pouring through us his own wisdom, righteousness and felicity.

Unto this is opposed the death everlasting, which is the perpetual casting away[^F4-3] <!-- p.310 --> of all the damned from God, and the torments of soul and body that are never to be ended.

### How is the life everlasting begun in us in this life?

Life everlasting is begun in us in this life, when out of the revealed Word we know God, and rest in his mercy, promised for the Son's sake, and when our hearts feel the consolation of the Holy Ghost, whereby there is at the same time kindled a new light, righteousness and gladness, which is the taste of the life everlasting. Thus much of the Creed.

# *De Tertia Parte Catechismi* · On the Third Part of the Catechism

### What is the third part of the Catechism?

The Lord's Prayer.

### What is Invocation?

Invocation is, in the name of and in confidence in Christ the Mediator, to ask benefits of God, and to give thanks for good things received, and to praise and proclaim the goodness of God their Author.

### What things doth Invocation comprise?

Two: prayer and thanksgiving.

### What is prayer?

It is, in the name of and in confidence in Christ, to ask something of God, and assuredly to believe that our prayers are heard and received for the sake of Christ, who is our Mediator and High Priest, bearing our groanings unto the Father, and maketh intercession for us.

<!-- p.311 -->

### What is thanksgiving?

It is to acknowledge that God is not idle, but truly careth for us, heareth us, and giveth benefits present and eternal; and to praise the immense power and goodness of God, and to submit ourselves unto him humbly from the heart, and by our obedience and confession to testify our thankfulness toward God, and to invite others to ask and to look for benefits from God.

### What things are required unto true Invocation?

Six heads.

1. Who the God is, whom we address.

2. The commandment of God concerning invocation: *"Ask, and it shall be given you."* *"Men ought always to pray."* **1 Thess. 5**: *"Rejoice evermore, pray without ceasing, and in every thing give thanks."* Likewise: *"Call upon me in the day of trouble."* It is therefore a horrible and enormous sin not to call upon God. And of the ungodly the Psalmist saith: *"They have not called upon the Lord."*

3. Let every man consider whether he be truly converted unto God. For *"God heareth not sinners."*

4. The promise is to be considered, that we may know whether and why we are heard, as it is written: *"The Lord is nigh unto all them that call upon him, to all that call upon him in truth. He will fulfil the desire of them that fear him, and will hear their cry."* And **Isa. 65**: *"While they are yet crying, I will answer."* Likewise: *"Whosoever shall call upon the name of God shall be saved."* And: *"Verily, verily, I say unto you, Whatsoever ye shall ask the Father in my name, he will give it you."*

5. Faith is to be stirred up, that thou mayest believe that thou art assuredly heard: in spiritual and eternal good things without any condition, but in bodily things with the condition of the cross. And <!-- p.312 --> faith comprehendeth both, petition and obedience, and holdeth that our prayers are not void, although the event is deferred, or answereth not unto our thought.

6. Consider what thou wouldest ask of God, lest the prayer be an empty ***βαττολογία*** (vain babbling).

### What is the difference between true Invocation and the false, or heathen?

Two things make the difference, namely, the knowledge of the Essence, and the knowledge of the Will. The heathen and the Turks, although they say, as Nestor in **Homer**, that we must pray, seeing we all have need of God, yet, because they deny that he is God who is the eternal Father, Son and Holy Ghost, they stray from the Essence of God, according to the saying of Christ: *"Ye worship ye know not what."* Therefore they call upon a feigned name, that is, an idol, which is nothing, as she saith: *"Jupiter, I call upon thee, wheresoever thou art, whether in heaven, or in the air, or on the earth, or in the waters."* And since they know not the promises and the Mediator, they stray from the Will of God, and doubt, as that barbarian king in **Virgil** saith: *"And we cherish an empty report."*[^F4-4] But the Church knoweth the Essence of God revealed in the Word, and knoweth his will, and in the name of and in confidence in Christ looketh upon the promise confirmed by the divine oath, and believeth that she is assuredly heard.

### Why did one of the Ancients say that nothing is harder than to pray?

This was truly said. For in prayer that is not an empty murmur, or ***βαττολογία*** (vain babbling), all these things are required:

1. The thought with how great a Majesty thou speakest.

<!-- p.313 -->

2. The consideration of the testimonies concerning God.

3. The revelation, and the precept concerning invocation.

4. Attention unto the several parts of the prayer.

5. Conversion, without which prayer is not only not effectual, but is a mocking and contempt of God.

6. The thought of the divine promises.

7. Faith, without which prayer not only taketh not away sin, but itself also becometh sin.

### What things are to be asked of God?

All things spiritual and bodily that agree with the commandments, which are set out in the best order in the Lord's Prayer. The exposition and phrase of this we have set down above.

### What are the causes for which Invocation is to be continually exercised?

1. The glory [of God].
2. The public necessity and welfare of the Church.
3. Every man's own necessity and welfare.
4. That spiritual learning and wisdom may be increased.
5. Consolation.
6. That the devils may be confounded and put to flight.
7. Confession, distinguishing the Church from other sects.

# *De Quarta Parte Catechismi* · On the Fourth Part of the Catechism

### What is the fourth part of the Catechism?

The doctrine concerning the Sacrament of Baptism.

### What is a Sacrament?

A Sacrament is a sacred action, instituted of God, consisting <!-- p.314 --> of the Word and the entire element, and added unto the promise which is proper unto the Gospel, that it may also seal and confirm faith in the living.

### Why were the Sacraments instituted?

1. That they may be testimonies of the will of God toward us, and ***σφραγῖδες*** (seals) of grace and of imputed righteousness.

2. That in very deed there may through them be given unto us the grace of Christ and the salvation obtained through Christ, not by the work wrought (*ex opere operato*), but unto them that believe, and that by faith apply unto themselves the merit and benefits of the Son of God. For the Sacraments are not only signs and representations of spiritual cleansing and quickening, but in the Sacraments the merit of Christ is really given and applied unto them that believe.

3. That they may be signs of confession, and may distinguish the Church from the other nations.

### How many Sacraments of the New Testament are there?

Two: Baptism and the Lord's Supper.

### What is Baptism?

It is an action, or ceremony, instituted of God, containing the dipping, or washing, and the entire pronouncing of the words: *"I baptize thee in the name of the Father, of the Son, and of the Holy Ghost"*; which testifieth that he who is baptized is received of God, is endued with the Holy Ghost, and is accepted unto the inheritance of life everlasting; and it is the means through which there is given reconciliation, deliverance from sin, the righteousness of Christ, and life and salvation unto all that believe and that abide in faith, according to the promise: *"He that believeth and is baptized shall be saved."*

### What is the principal end and use of Baptism?

<!-- p.315 -->

The principal end is the application of the remission of sins unto him that is baptized. For Baptism is the means through which the merit of Christ is given, and is by faith applied, really and effectually, because it is *"the washing of regeneration and of renewing through the Holy Ghost."*

### Are infants to be baptized?

They are to be baptized, because unto them pertaineth the promise of salvation, as it is said: *"Of such is the kingdom of heaven."* But there is no salvation without the Church, as it is written, **John 3**: *"Except a man be born of water and of the Spirit, he shall not enter into the kingdom of heaven."* Therefore it is necessary that infants be regenerated through Baptism, and ingrafted into the Church. And Christ is effectual in infants through Baptism, and worketh in them faith and other motions, as he saith, **Matt. 18**: *"It is not the will of the Father, that one of these little ones should perish, which believe in me."*

# *De Quinta Parte Catechismi, quae est Coena Dominica* · On the Fifth Part of the Catechism, which is the Lord's Supper

### What is the Lord's Supper?

The definition is to be taken from the words of the Institution: *"Take, eat; this is my body, which is given for you. Drink; this cup is the new testament in my blood."* And from the exposition of **Paul**: *"The bread is the communion of the body of Christ. The cup, or the wine, is the communion of the blood of Christ."*

This therefore is the definition, out of the words of the Son of God himself:

The Lord's Supper in the Church of Christ is the true body and <!-- p.316 --> the true blood of our Lord Jesus Christ, which body is received with the mouth, and which blood is drunk with the mouth, as often as the bread and wine of blessing are received with the mouth; which Supper was instituted by Christ himself, when he was now about to die for our sins, that it might be a remembrance of Christ offered for us upon the Cross as a sacrifice of atonement, and a confirmation of the New Covenant concerning grace and the remission of sins, and might testify that Christ is in us not only spiritually through faith, but also with his body and blood, or by a natural participation, and that we ourselves are ingrafted into him not only according to the soul, but also according to the body, for the assurance of our resurrection, and for consolation in all temptations and distresses.

I ask what the Supper is. The Son of God himself answereth that it is his last Will and Testament, which he made in the night in which he was betrayed.

I ask what is given by Christ in that Supper. Our Lord Jesus Christ himself, taking bread and wine, answereth that there is given by him his body and blood, offered and shed for our sins.

I ask what that bread is. The Son of God answereth: *"This is my body; the cup is my blood."*

I ask what manner of body, whether the true and natural, or a spiritual one, which is received by faith only. The Son of God himself answereth: *"It is my body, which is given for you."* Likewise: *"This cup is my blood, which is shed for your sins."*

I ask how the body of Christ is received. The Son of God himself answereth: *"Take, eat."* And this is done with the mouth, and not by any other means or instrument.

I ask how this is possible. The Son of God himself answereth: *"I say, Take, eat; this is my body."* Let it therefore suffice us that our Lord Jesus Christ, almighty and true, saith it, with whom nothing is impossible. <!-- p.317 --> His saying is doing and being done. *"Whatsoever he saith unto you, do it,"* saith Mary; even as the disciples do not ask how he should or could give his body, and how they should or could receive it with their mouth and eat it, but in silence humbly submit themselves unto the words and commandment of him whom they know to be almighty and true, and, bringing their reason into captivity unto the obedience of Christ, humbly consecrate and offer it wholly unto Christ, assuredly holding that he cannot err who with a simple and suppliant faith and humility believeth the words and the last will of Christ, in whose mouth there is no guile.

I ask what **Paul** meaneth, when he nameth the bread of blessing the communion, ***κοινωνίαν***, of the body of Christ. I answer: he meaneth the same that Christ meaneth. For it is a communion, because the body of Christ is communicated unto us, that is, with the bread the body of the Lord is delivered and received.

### Wherefore, or to what end, was the Lord's Supper instituted?

1. That it may confirm faith.

2. That we may give thanks unto God. For it is the *Eucharist*.

3. That it may be the sinew of the public assembly. For it is the *Synaxis*.

4. That it may be a testimony of confession and of doctrine.

5. That it may be a token of mutual love, and of alms, whence it is called ***ἀγάπη*** (love-feast).

6. That it may be a token of our resurrection, as the Nicene Canon calleth the body and blood of Christ ***σύμβολα τῆς ἡμετέρης ἀναστάσεως*** (tokens of our resurrection); of which matter we have discoursed more fully in the Second Part of the *Paedagogia*, in the doctrine concerning the Resurrection.

### Against what doth the doctrine of the presence of the true body of Christ, and of the bodily eating of it, delivered by Christ himself, strike?

<!-- p.318 -->

1. Against the judgment and reason of man.

2. Against the rules and properties of natural philosophy.

3. Against certain sayings of the Fathers; of whom, nevertheless, all and each, there is none that denieth that the body of Christ is in the Supper ***σωματικῇ παρουσίᾳ*** (by a bodily presence), and is in very deed eaten with the mouth.

### But what is to be judged of these things?

All things are to be set after the Word, the commandment and the institution of God. And the theological rule of all Christians ought to be this: Whatsoever striketh not against the omnipotence and the will, or the promise, of God expressed in the Word, that, if it be founded in the Word of God, is true and unmoved, and is to be received and held with a simple faith, although it be against all natural reason and rule. On the contrary, whatsoever striketh against the divine omnipotence and truth, as doth the opinion of all that deny the bodily presence and eating of Christ in the Supper, that, although it agree in all things with human reason and the rules of natural philosophy, is yet erroneous and false, and altogether to be abhorred in the doctrine of the Christian religion.

### Who use the Lord's Supper worthily?

All that are converted, or that repent.

### Who are named unworthy?

They that repent not, or that examine not themselves. These, whether they be hypocrites, or defiled with other sins against conscience, when they partake of the bread of blessing in the holy Supper, which is the communion of the body of Christ, are made partakers also of the body of Christ, unto judgment and damnation to themselves, because by eating and drinking they become guilty of the body and blood of Christ.

<!-- p.319 -->

### What are the chief abuses of the Supper among the Papist adversaries?

1. The figment of transubstantiation.
2. The shutting up and the carrying about.
3. The adoration of the bread, ***ἀρτολατρεία*** (bread-worship).
4. The sacrifice of the Mass.
5. The work wrought (*opus operatum*).
6. The mutilation, or taking away of the one kind.
7. The figment concerning the deliverance of the dead out of Purgatory, to the furthering of which the Supper is transferred.

### In what ways hath the holy Supper been profaned among the Sacramentarians?

1. They deny that the body of Christ is present, and is communicated unto them that eat of the bread of blessing; so that, although they say that the presence of the body of Christ in the Supper is true, real and substantial, not feigned nor imaginary, and that the delivering also is true, and that the body of Christ is taken with the mouth through the outward tokens (for so Albert Hardenberg spake and wrote), yet they always hide a deceit, and beguile the simple with the words *By faith*, *In the Spirit*, *Sacramentally*, by which they mean this, that the body of Christ is as far removed from the Supper as the highest heavens are distant from the earth.

2. They say that the body of Christ is locally received and contained by heaven, so that on earth, even though Christ should will it, it cannot be.

3. They affirm that the bread and wine of blessing are only signs of the absent body and blood.

4. They teach that the eating is done through faith ascending by its imagination into heaven, in which the body of Christ is locally detained.

5. That the unworthy receive nothing else save bread and wine, <!-- p.320 --> but that the worthy are by faith better than the unworthy; and yet that both receive only bread and wine with their mouth.

6. They contradict Christ, the Evangelists, Paul and all the Orthodox, and set the omnipotence and truth of Christ after the rules of natural philosophy.

7. They teach that Christ is as far and as distantly removed from us in his body, as heaven is distant from the earth.

8. They devise a Communication of Properties (*Communicatio Idiomatum*) that is only verbal and dialectical, without truth in the exaltation of the human nature in Christ; and the right hand of God, unto which Christ as man is lifted up, they feign to be a bodily place, wherein Christ is circumscribed, detained and contained; and that Christ in one place only of heaven, wheresoever that be, is God and man together, but elsewhere is only God.

9. Being convicted by the truth they are provoked, and begin to rage with threats, hatred, biting speech and implacableness, and yet they creep further with their opinions among the secure and the worldly in courts and commonwealths, with minds beyond measure bitter, hard and harsh, according to that saying of **D. D. Luther**: *"Ich habe noch nie gesehen, daß ein Schwermer bekeret sei worden"* (I have never yet seen that a fanatic was converted).

10. Of the war against images, of the ***διαφωνίᾳ καὶ ἀμουσίᾳ*** (discord and want of music) in the churches, of the extenuating of Baptism, of the ***ἀταξίᾳ*** (disorder) in ceremonies and the ecclesiastical confusion, of the conventicles, and of the irreverence in the administration of the Supper in many places, we must speak elsewhere. These things only have we noted, that the boys may learn to flee monstrous opinions and blasphemies against the Son of God, and may pray God that he would sanctify us, and keep us in his truth, that is, in his Word.

### What is private Absolution?

It is the announcing and applying of the remission of sins, <!-- p.321 --> which is done in the name and by the authority of Christ through the voice of a man in the ministry, according to the saying: *"Whose soever sins ye remit, they are remitted unto them,"* **John 20**. **Matt. 16** and **18**: *"Whatsoever ye shall loose on earth shall be loosed in heaven, and whatsoever ye shall bind on earth shall be bound in heaven"*; that is, absolution applied through the Minister is effectual, ratified and firm before the judgment seat of God; and in like manner excommunication also.

### Is the Confession of all, or of each, or of some offences necessary?

Before God that confession is necessary whereby a man owneth himself guilty of all sins, and acknowledgeth them, as **Paul** saith: *"Let a man examine himself."* But in the confession which they call *auricular*, before the Ministers of the Church, enumeration is not necessary, both because it is not commanded, and because the enumeration of every offence is impossible, according to that word: *"Who can understand his errors?"* But where there is need of doctrine, instruction, the trying of faith, counsel and consolation, there both he that confesseth and he that absolveth will do those things which conscience and the nature of the office require.

### Recite the form of Confession.

Reverend Sir, I pray you to hear my confession, and in the name, in the stead and by the command of Christ to absolve me from my sins.

> I, a poor sinner, confess before God and before you that I have many sins; and that, being conceived and born in sins, I have in manifold wise offended, and do offend, against the First and the Second Table, and that I am guilty of the wrath of God and of everlasting punishments. But I grieve with my whole heart that I have ever <!-- p.322 --> offended my God, and do offend him, and I humbly pray that God would have mercy upon me, and forgive me all and each of my sins, and justify me through and for the sake of the Son, the Mediator, and sanctify me by his Holy Spirit, that I may be and may abide a temple of God, and an instrument wholesome unto my own soul and unto the Church, and a vessel of mercy. That I may assure and seal this faith in myself, I will come unto the holy Synaxis, in which, according to the commandment, institution and will of Christ my Lord, I shall receive the true body and the true blood of my Redeemer Jesus Christ, offered and shed for me. I ask therefore of you, that in the name and in the stead of Christ you would absolve me from my sins, and announce unto me the remission of sins. I promise thankfulness unto God and unto you, the Holy Ghost helping and governing me, and amendment of life.

***τῷ θεῷ δόξα*** (To God be the glory.)

— ❦ —

[^F4-1]: The exposition of the Creed which the older editions printed among the works of Cyprian, and which Selnecker cites under his name, is that of Rufinus of Aquileia; it explains that the Church of Aquileia confesses the resurrection "of *this* flesh," the speaker pointing to his own body.

[^F4-2]: So the print. The words stand in 1 Pet. 3.

[^F4-3]: The print reads *obiectio*; the sense requires *abiectio* ("casting away"), and it is so translated.

[^F4-4]: Virgil, *Aeneid* 4.218 (*famamque fovemus inanem*), in the prayer of Iarbas to Jupiter.

[^F4-5]: So the print; the question on private absolution stands on p. 320.
